  • Minimum IPO size: ₹400 crore, with at least 90 % of proceeds deposited in an escrow/trust account invested only in government securities or AAA‑rated PSU bonds.
  • Acquisition window: 24 months from listing, extendable by 6 months with shareholder approval.
  • Sponsor lock‑in: 3 years on promoter shares; sponsor must hold at least 20 % of post‑IPO equity.
  • Redemption right: Public shareholders may redeem up to 100 % of their holdings at the IPO price plus accrued interest if they dissent from the proposed acquisition.
  • Net‑worth threshold: Sponsor group net worth ≥ ₹100 crore.

Compared with the U.S. SEC regime (Rule 419 and the 2021 SPAC‑specific disclosure rules), India’s draft imposes a higher escrow ratio (90 % vs. 85 %), a shorter acquisition window (24 vs. 36 months), and a mandatory sponsor lock‑in absent in the U.S. The 2023 SEBI Board meeting approved the framework with minor tweaks: the minimum IPO size was reduced to ₹250 crore for “small‑cap SPACs” and a “dual‑track” option allowing simultaneous pursuit of a traditional IPO and a SPAC merger was permitted.

As of June 2024, no SPAC has completed an IPO on Indian exchanges; the first filings are expected from fintech‑focused sponsors leveraging the GIFT City IFSC route, which offers a tax‑neutral environment for offshore investors.

Worked Example: SPAC Compliance Check
A sponsor proposes a SPAC with a trust account of ₹500 crore, 90 % invested in government securities, and a 30‑month acquisition window. Under SEBI's draft SPAC framework (as approved in 2023), which condition is violated?
The acquisition window exceeds the permitted 24 months (extendable by 6 months only with shareholder approval). A 30‑month window without prior shareholder consent breaches the regulation. The trust‑account composition (90 % in government securities) and size (≥ ₹250 crore) satisfy the norms.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a question cites a SPAC feature, first verify the acquisition window and escrow ratio against the 2023 approved parameters. Remember the sponsor lock‑in (3 years) and the 20 % minimum promoter holding — these are frequent “odd‑one‑out” distractors.

Cross‑Border Listing Regime: Indian Issuers on Overseas Exchanges and Foreign Issuers on Indian Platforms

The SEBI (Listing of Specified Securities on International Exchanges) Regulations, 2022, enable Indian companies to list equity or depository receipts on “specified foreign exchanges” — currently the NYSE, NASDAQ, London Stock Exchange, Hong Kong Exchange, and Singapore Exchange. Eligibility requires:

  • Minimum net worth of ₹500 crore and a track record of three years of profitability.
  • Compliance with the Foreign Exchange Management Act (FEMA) and the Overseas Direct Investment (ODI) guidelines of the RBI.
  • Appointment of a foreign custodian and a local compliance officer in the host jurisdiction.

Conversely, foreign companies may access Indian investors through Indian Depository Receipts (IDRs) under the SEBI (Issue of Capital and Disclosure Requirements) Regulations, 2018. The IDR framework mandates a minimum issue size of ₹100 crore, a minimum 25 % public float, and adherence to Indian GAAP or IFRS with a reconciliation statement. The 2021 amendment allowed “dual‑listed” entities (already listed on a specified foreign exchange) to issue IDRs via a fast‑track route, reducing the prospectus filing timeline to 15 working days.

Notable cross‑border listings include Freshworks Inc. (NASDAQ, 2021) and Zomato Ltd. (BSE/NSE, 2021) — the latter a domestic IPO but with significant foreign institutional participation. In 2023, Tata Consultancy Services explored a secondary listing on the LSE under the new regime, though the plan was deferred. The regime also interacts with the GIFT City IFSC framework, where a “foreign‑owned, foreign‑controlled” entity can list on the India International Exchange (India INX) without a full SEBI ICDR compliance, subject to IFSCA approval.

Regulatory Architecture for Over‑the‑Counter Derivatives and Credit Default Swaps in India

OTC derivatives in India are governed by a dual regulatory matrix: the RBI regulates interest‑rate and currency derivatives under the “Framework for OTC Derivatives” (2016, amended 2020), while SEBI oversees credit derivatives, notably Credit Default Swaps (CDS), under the SEBI (Credit Default Swaps) Regulations, 2021. Key pillars:

  • Central Clearing Mandate: Standardised single‑currency interest‑rate swaps (IRS) and CDS on corporate bonds must be cleared through the Clearing Corporation of India Ltd. (CCIL), which acts as a qualified central counterparty (QCCP).
  • Trade Reporting: All OTC derivative transactions must be reported to the CCIL Trade Repository within 30 minutes of execution, with a Legal Entity Identifier (LEI) mandatory for both counterparties.
  • Margin Requirements: Initial margin (IM) and variation margin (VM) follow the BCBS‑IOSCO framework; non‑centrally cleared trades attract a minimum IM of 1.5 % of notional for IRS and 2 % for CDS.
  • Eligible Participants: Only regulated entities (banks, NBFCs‑ML, insurers, mutual funds, AIFs) and corporates with net worth ≥ ₹500 crore may buy protection; sellers must be banks or primary dealers.
  • Position Limits: Gross notional CDS exposure of a protection buyer capped at 10 % of the reference entity’s outstanding bonds; seller caps at 5 % of its Tier‑1 capital.

The 2023 SEBI circular introduced “Standardised CDS on Corporate Bonds” with fixed tenors (1, 3, 5 years) and a standardised credit event definition aligned with ISDA 2014 definitions, aiming to deepen the corporate bond market. As of March 2024, the outstanding notional of cleared CDS stood at ₹1.2 lakh crore, with the top five reference entities (mostly public‑sector banks) accounting for 60 % of volumes.

Investment Adviser and Research Analyst Registration: Code of Conduct, Fee‑Based Advice and Recent SEBI Amendments

The SEBI (Investment Advisers) Regulations, 2013, amended in 2020 and 2022, and the SEBI (Research Analysts) Regulations, 2014, amended in 2021 and 2023, together shape the professional advice ecosystem. Highlights:

  • Registration Categories: Individual IA (net worth ≥ ₹25 lakh), Corporate IA (net worth ≥ ₹50 lakh), and “Qualified Investment Adviser” (QIA) for those managing ≥ ₹100 crore AUM.
  • Fee‑Only Model: The 2022 amendment mandates that IAs disclose whether they operate on a fee‑only or fee‑plus‑commission basis; conflict‑of‑interest disclosures must be updated quarterly.
  • Fiduciary Duty: IAs must act in the client’s best interest, documented through a “Client Agreement” specifying scope, fees, and risk profiling.
  • Research Analyst Qualification: Minimum NISM‑Series‑XV certification, a post‑graduate degree in finance/economics or equivalent experience, and a clean disciplinary record.
  • Segregation Requirement (2023): Entities offering both advisory and distribution services must segregate the two functions into separate legal entities or distinct business units with Chinese‑wall policies, effective 1 April 2024.
  • Cooling‑Off Period: A research analyst who leaves a broker‑dealer must observe a six‑month cooling‑off before issuing independent research on the same securities.

Enforcement actions in 2023‑24 included penalties totalling ₹12 crore against three corporate IAs for undisclosed commission arrangements and a ₹3 crore fine on a research analyst for front‑running recommendations. The regulatory thrust is toward transparent, fee‑based advice and clear separation of research from distribution.

  • Confusing the municipal bond net‑worth threshold (₹100 crore) with the SPAC sponsor net‑worth requirement (₹100 crore) — they apply to different entities and have distinct track‑record conditions.
  • Assuming the SPAC acquisition window is 36 months as in the U.S.; India’s approved framework caps it at 24 months (extendable by 6 months only with shareholder approval).
  • Believing any foreign exchange qualifies for cross‑border listing; only the five “specified foreign exchanges” notified by SEBI are eligible.
  • Thinking CDS can be sold by any corporate; only banks and primary dealers may act as protection sellers under the 2021 regulations.
  • Overlooking the 2023 segregation mandate for dual‑role advisory‑distribution firms; the Chinese‑wall requirement is now a licensing condition, not a voluntary best practice.
  • Mixing up the IA net‑worth thresholds: ₹25 lakh for individuals, ₹50 lakh for corporates, and ₹100 crore AUM for QIA status.
  • Neglecting the mandatory LEI for OTC derivative reporting — a frequent factual error in trade‑repository compliance questions.
The advanced sections above complete the required coverage for this lesson.

Peer‑to‑Peer Lending Platform Regulation: RBI Master Direction 2023 and SEBI Oversight

The Reserve Bank of India issued the Master Direction – Peer‑to‑Peer Lending Platform (Reserve Bank) Directions, 2023, replacing the 2017 guidelines and bringing the sector under a tighter prudential framework. A P2P platform must be a non‑banking financial company (NBFC) registered with the RBI and hold a minimum net owned fund (NOF) of ₹2 crore. The platform’s leverage ratio is capped at 1:2, meaning total outstanding loans cannot exceed twice the NOF. Borrower‑lender matching is restricted to a maximum of ₹50 lakh per lender across all platforms, and a single borrower cannot raise more than ₹10 lakh from a single platform.

Key operational requirements include a board‑approved credit‑assessment policy, mandatory credit‑bureau reporting for every loan, and a cooling‑off period of 30 days before a borrower can take a fresh loan. Platforms must maintain a grievance‑redressal mechanism with a designated nodal officer and resolve complaints within 30 days. The RBI also mandated that platforms hold a minimum of 5 % of the loan portfolio as a risk‑retention buffer, which cannot be sold or securitised for 12 months.

SEBI’s role is limited to the extent that any P2P platform issuing securities (e.g., pass‑through certificates) must comply with the SEBI (Issue and Listing of Non‑Convertible Securities) Regulations, 2021. The platform’s own equity capital raising, if done through a public offer, falls under SEBI’s ICDR framework. The 2023 Master Direction also introduced a “fit‑and‑proper” test for directors and key managerial personnel, aligning with the RBI’s broader NBFC governance norms.

Enforcement actions in 2023‑24 saw the RBI cancel the registration of three platforms for violating the leverage cap and for inadequate credit‑bureau reporting. The directions also require platforms to submit quarterly returns on loan origination, delinquency, and recovery metrics, enabling the RBI to monitor systemic risk from the growing P2P segment.

Capital Adequacy and Leverage Ratio for Small Finance Banks and Payment Banks: RBI’s 2022 Framework

In November 2022 the RBI released the “Capital Adequacy Framework for Small Finance Banks (SFBs) and Payment Banks (PBs)”, harmonising their requirements with the Basel III‑based norms applicable to universal banks while recognising their distinct business models. SFBs must maintain a minimum Common Equity Tier‑1 (CET1) ratio of 9 % and a total capital ratio of 12 %, identical to scheduled commercial banks. In addition, a leverage ratio of 3 % (Tier‑1 capital to total exposure) is prescribed, calculated on a quarterly average basis.

Payment Banks, which cannot extend credit, are required to hold a minimum CET1 of 7 % and a total capital ratio of 10 %. Their leverage ratio is set at 3 % as well, but the exposure measure excludes the value of government securities held for statutory liquidity ratio (SLR) purposes. Both categories must comply with the Capital Conservation Buffer (CCB) of 2.5 % and the Countercyclical Capital Buffer (CCyB) as activated by the RBI.

The framework introduces a “scale‑based” approach: SFBs with assets above ₹500 crore are subject to the full suite of Pillar 2 supervisory review, including stress‑testing of loan portfolios, while smaller SFBs follow a simplified supervisory template. Payment Banks are required to maintain a minimum net‑worth of ₹100 crore at all times, and any breach triggers a prompt corrective action (PCA) matrix similar to that for universal banks.

Transitional arrangements allowed existing SFBs and PBs to meet the new ratios by March 2024. As of the March 2024 reporting date, all ten SFBs and six PBs reported compliance, with the aggregate CET1 of SFBs standing at 11.3 % and that of PBs at 9.8 %. The RBI also mandated that SFBs disclose their leverage ratio in the quarterly financial statements, enhancing market discipline.

Examiner’s Twist
A question asks: “Which of the following statements about the 2022 RBI capital framework for Small Finance Banks is correct? (a) The minimum CET1 ratio is 7 %. (b) The leverage ratio is 4 %. (c) The Capital Conservation Buffer is 2.5 %. (d) The Countercyclical Buffer is mandatory at 1 %.”
The correct answer is (c). The 2022 framework prescribes a CET1 of 9 % for SFBs (not 7 %), a leverage ratio of 3 % (not 4 %), and a Capital Conservation Buffer of 2.5 % applicable to both SFBs and PBs. The Countercyclical Capital Buffer is activated by the RBI only when systemic risk builds up; it is not a fixed mandatory 1 %.
Exam‑Day Strategy
Memorise the three‑tier capital ratios (CET1, Tier‑1, Total) for each bank category and the associated buffers. When a question mixes SFB and PB numbers, isolate the entity first, then match the ratio. A quick mental table (SFB = 9/12/3, PB = 7/10/3) prevents confusion.

Corporate Green Bond Issuance under SEBI’s Green Bond Guidelines and External Review Requirements

SEBI’s “Green Bond Guidelines” (issued in 2023 as a circular under the SEBI (Issue and Listing of Non‑Convertible Securities) Regulations, 2021) define a green bond as a debt instrument where proceeds are exclusively applied to eligible green projects such as renewable energy, energy efficiency, pollution prevention, sustainable agriculture, and climate‑change adaptation. The issuer must publish a Green Bond Framework aligned with the International Capital Market Association (ICMA) Green Bond Principles, 2021.

A mandatory external review is required before issuance. The reviewer can be a second‑party opinion provider, a certified climate‑bond verifier, or an approved rating agency. The review must assess the project eligibility, allocation transparency, and impact reporting methodology. Post‑issuance, the issuer must provide an annual “Green Bond Report” detailing the use of proceeds, qualitative and quantitative environmental impact, and any material changes to the project portfolio.

The guidelines also prescribe a “use‑of‑proceeds” tracking mechanism: proceeds must be credited to a separate escrow account or a designated sub‑account, and any unallocated funds must be invested in liquid, low‑risk instruments until deployment. SEBI requires the issuer to disclose the green bond’s taxonomy classification (e.g., “Renewable Energy – Solar”) in the offer document and in the continuous disclosure filings.

As of March 2024, Indian corporates have raised approximately ₹45,000 crore through green bonds under this framework, with the largest issuances coming from power‑generation companies and renewable‑energy developers. The external‑review requirement has been credited with improving investor confidence, reflected in tighter spreads compared with vanilla non‑convertible debentures of similar tenure.

Foreign Portfolio Investment in Government Securities: Fully Accessible Route, Limits and Settlement Mechanics

The Fully Accessible Route (FAR) for Foreign Portfolio Investors (FPIs) in Indian government securities was introduced in 2020 and expanded in 2023. Under FAR, specified government securities (currently the 10‑year benchmark and select 5‑year and 30‑year papers) are open for investment without any individual or aggregate investment limits, subject only to the overall FPI limit of 30 % of the outstanding stock of each security.

FPIs must register with SEBI under the FPI Regulations, 2019, and obtain a Legal Entity Identifier (LEI). Settlement occurs through the Clearing Corporation of India Ltd. (CCIL) on a T+1 basis, with delivery versus payment (DvP) in the RBI’s Negotiated Dealing System‑Order Matching (NDS‑OM) platform. The RBI monitors the aggregate FPI holding through the “Investment Limit Monitoring System” and publishes daily utilisation percentages.

In 2023 the RBI raised the FAR limit for the 10‑year benchmark from 6 % to 10 % of outstanding stock, and added two additional securities to the FAR basket. The move aimed to deepen the investor base and improve price discovery. FPIs are also permitted to hedge their rupee exposure using exchange‑traded currency futures and options on recognised exchanges, subject to position limits prescribed by the RBI.

Tax treatment: interest income earned by FPIs on government securities is subject to a concessional withholding tax of 5 % under the India‑Mauritius tax treaty (subject to the Principal Purpose Test) and 20 % under the domestic law, whichever is lower, provided the FPI holds a valid Tax Residency Certificate. Capital gains on sale of these securities are exempt for Category I and II FPIs under the Income‑Tax Act, 1961, while Category III FPIs are taxed at 10 % (long‑term) and 15 % (short‑term).

Market Making and Liquidity Provision in the Corporate Bond Segment: SEBI’s Market Maker Framework

To address chronic illiquidity in the corporate bond secondary market, SEBI introduced the “Market Maker Framework” in 2022 (SEBI Circular SEBI/HO/MRD/MRD‑DOP/P/CIR/2022/001). Eligible market makers include scheduled commercial banks, primary dealers, and NBFCs‑ML with a minimum net‑worth of ₹500 crore. They must enter into a market‑making agreement with the stock exchange and commit to providing two‑way quotes for a minimum of 10 securities, with a maximum spread of 50 basis points for investment‑grade bonds and 100 basis points for high‑yield bonds.

The market maker is required to maintain a minimum quote size of ₹5 crore per side and to refresh quotes at least once every 15 minutes during trading hours. In return, the exchange grants a rebate of 0.5 basis points on transaction charges and priority in the order‑matching queue. SEBI also mandated that market makers disclose their inventory positions daily to the exchange, which publishes an aggregated “Market Maker Inventory Report” for transparency.

Since implementation, the average daily turnover in the corporate bond segment on the NSE and BSE has risen from ₹3,200 crore (FY 2022) to ₹5,800 crore (FY 2024). The bid‑ask spread for the top‑10 most‑traded investment‑grade issuers narrowed from 45 bps to 22 bps. SEBI’s 2023 review proposed extending the framework to include “designated market makers” for newly issued bonds during the first 30 days, ensuring primary‑market liquidity.

  • Confusing the P2P platform leverage cap (1:2) with the NBFC‑ML leverage ratio (typically 7:1) — they apply to different regulatory regimes.
  • Assuming the Small Finance Bank CET1 requirement is 7 % like Payment Banks; SFBs must meet 9 %.
  • Believing any external reviewer can certify a green bond; SEBI only accepts reviewers accredited by ICMA or recognised rating agencies.
  • Thinking FPIs can invest unlimited amounts in all government securities under FAR; only specified securities are eligible and the 30 % aggregate cap still applies.
  • Mixing the market‑maker spread limits: 50 bps for investment‑grade, 100 bps for high‑yield — not a uniform 75 bps.
  • Overlooking the mandatory 5 % risk‑retention buffer for P2P platforms, which cannot be securitised for 12 months.
  • Neglecting that Payment Banks’ leverage ratio excludes SLR‑eligible government securities from the exposure measure.

Pension Fund Regulatory and Development Authority Framework: NPS Architecture, Investment Guidelines and Recent Amendments

The Pension Fund Regulatory and Development Authority (PFRDA) was established under the PFRDA Act, 2013, and became the statutory regulator for the National Pension System (NPS) and other pension funds in India. The NPS operates on a two‑tier structure: Tier I is a mandatory, non‑withdrawable retirement account for all subscribers, while Tier II is a voluntary, withdrawable savings account that can be opened only after a Tier I account exists. Both tiers are administered through a network of Points of Presence (PoPs) — banks, post offices, and registered intermediaries — and the Central Recordkeeping Agency (CRA) maintains the consolidated subscriber database.

Investment guidelines for NPS assets are prescribed in the PFRDA (Investment) Regulations, 2015, and subsequent circulars. The equity exposure ceiling for the Active Choice option was raised from 50 % to 75 % in 2022 (PFRDA Circular No. PFRDA/2022/13), while the Auto Choice lifecycle funds follow a glide‑path that reduces equity allocation from 75 % at age 35 to 10 % at age 55. Debt instruments are limited to sovereign bonds, state development loans, and highly rated corporate bonds (minimum AA). In 2023, PFRDA permitted pension funds to invest up to 5 % of assets in Real Estate Investment Trusts (REITs) and Infrastructure Investment Trusts (InvITs) subject to a minimum credit rating of AA (Circular PFRDA/2023/07). The 2024 amendment (PFRDA/2024/02) introduced a governance framework requiring independent trustees for each pension fund, mandatory quarterly disclosure of portfolio‑level ESG scores, and a cap of 2 % on exposure to unlisted equity.

Partial withdrawal rules were liberalised in 2022: subscribers may withdraw up to 25 % of contributions for higher education, marriage, or purchase of a first house after three years of enrollment, without exiting the scheme. The 2023 circular (PFRDA/2023/15) extended the withdrawal window to include medical treatment for specified critical illnesses. Tax treatment remains EEE (exempt‑exempt‑exempt) for Tier I contributions up to ₹1.5 lakh under Section 80CCD(1) and an additional ₹50,000 under Section 80CCD(1B); Tier II contributions enjoy no tax benefit but withdrawals are taxed at slab rates.

Recent developments include the launch of the “NPS‑Lite” mobile app in 2024 for real‑time portfolio tracking, and the integration of the Account Aggregator framework to enable consent‑based fetching of subscriber financial data for KYC updates. The PFRDA also signed a Memorandum of Understanding with the International Financial Services Centres Authority (IFSCA) in 2023 to facilitate cross‑border pension portability for Indian expatriates working in GIFT City.

RBI Payment and Settlement Systems Act, 2007: Regulatory Architecture, Licensing of Payment System Operators, and Recent Amendments

The Payment and Settlement Systems Act, 2007 (PSS Act) provides the legal foundation for the regulation and supervision of payment systems in India. Under Section 4, the Reserve Bank of India (RBI) is the designated authority for authorising, regulating, and overseeing all payment systems. The Act distinguishes between “payment system providers” (PSPs) — entities that operate the technical infrastructure — and “payment system operators” (PSOs) — entities that offer payment services to end‑users. Licensing is mandatory for both categories; the RBI issues a Certificate of Authorisation after evaluating the applicant’s net‑worth, technology risk management, consumer protection mechanisms, and compliance with the KYC/AML framework.

The 2021 amendment (Notification RBI/2021‑22/112) introduced mandatory interoperability for all authorised payment systems, requiring QR‑code based person‑to‑merchant (P2M) transactions to be accepted across competing networks. It also mandated that all PSPs store payment data exclusively within India, a provision reinforced by the 2023 Data Localisation Circular (RBI/2023‑24/45) which prescribed a 180‑day migration timeline for existing operators. The 2022 Guidelines for Payment Aggregators (PA) and Payment Gateways (PG) (RBI/2022‑23/89) set a minimum net‑worth of ₹15 crore for PAs, prescribed a settlement cycle of T+1 for merchant payouts, and required PAs to maintain an escrow account with a scheduled commercial bank for merchant funds.

In 2024, the RBI released the “Framework for Offline Payments” (RBI/2024‑25/12) allowing low‑value transactions up to ₹2,000 per transaction and ₹20,000 per day without internet connectivity, using near‑field communication (NFC) or QR‑code based instruments. The framework mandates that offline payment instruments be issued only by authorised PSOs, carry a unique device identifier, and support mandatory transaction logging for audit purposes. The same year, the RBI introduced a “Regulatory Sandbox for Payment Innovations” (RBI/2024‑25/18) with a cohort‑based entry process, enabling fintechs to test solutions such as voice‑based payments, biometric authentication, and blockchain‑based settlement under controlled conditions.

Supervisory oversight is exercised through periodic on‑site inspections, off‑site returns (monthly transaction volume, fraud reports, system availability metrics), and the “Payment System Indicators” dashboard published quarterly. Non‑compliance attracts penalties under Section 30 of the PSS Act, ranging from monetary fines up to ₹1 crore per contravention to suspension or revocation of authorisation. The 2023 enforcement action against a major payment gateway for failure to report a data breach within the prescribed 72‑hour window resulted in a ₹2.5 crore penalty and a directive to appoint an independent chief information security officer.

Examiner's twist on regulatory overlap
A question asks: “Which regulator has the primary authority to issue licences to a fintech company that proposes to operate a unified payments interface (UPI) based payment aggregator service?” The options include RBI, SEBI, PFRDA, and IRDAI.
The correct answer is RBI. Under the Payment and Settlement Systems Act, 2007, the RBI is the sole authority for authorising payment system operators and payment aggregators. SEBI regulates securities markets, PFRDA regulates pension funds, and IRDAI regulates insurance. The 2022 PA/PG guidelines explicitly place payment aggregators under RBI’s licensing regime, and the 2021 interoperability amendment reinforces RBI’s primacy over UPI‑based services.
Exam‑day strategy
Memorise the hierarchy: PSS Act → RBI authorisation → PA/PG guidelines → data localisation circular → offline payments framework. When a question mixes fintech functions (aggregator, gateway, wallet), identify the core activity (fund settlement vs. investment vs. insurance) and map it to the correct regulator. Keep a one‑page table of key circular numbers (2021‑112, 2022‑89, 2023‑45, 2024‑12, 2024‑18) for quick recall.

NBFC Liquidity Risk Management: Liquidity Coverage Ratio, Net Stable Funding Ratio, and Contingency Funding Plans under Scale‑Based Regulation

The Reserve Bank’s Scale‑Based Regulation (SBR) for Non‑Banking Financial Companies, introduced via the Circular RBI/2021‑22/112 dated 22 October 2021, classifies NBFCs into four layers — Base, Middle, Upper, and Top — based on asset size, interconnectedness, and supervisory inputs. Liquidity risk management requirements are calibrated to each layer. NBFC‑Middle Layer (NBFC‑ML) and NBFC‑Upper Layer (NBFC‑UL) entities are mandated to maintain a Liquidity Coverage Ratio (LCR) of at least 60 % from 1 December 2022, rising to 100 % by 1 December 2024 (RBI/2022‑23/56). The LCR is computed as the stock of High‑Quality Liquid Assets (HQLA) divided by total net cash outflows over a 30‑day stress horizon, with HQLA limited to Level 1 assets (cash, central government securities) and Level 2A/2B assets (state government securities, highly rated corporate bonds) subject to haircuts of 15 % and 50 % respectively.

The Net Stable Funding Ratio (NSFR) requirement was extended to NBFC‑UL from 1 October 2023 (RBI/2023‑24/31) with a minimum of 100 %. NSFR measures the proportion of available stable funding (equity, long‑term debt, stable deposits) to required stable funding (weighted assets and off‑balance‑sheet exposures). NBFC‑ML entities are required to compute NSFR for reporting purposes from 1 April 2024, with a supervisory expectation of 100 % by 1 October 2025. The RBI’s 2024 calibration circular (RBI/2024‑25/09) introduced a differentiated run‑off factor for retail deposits (5 % for insured, 10 % for uninsured) and a higher outflow factor for committed credit lines to small businesses (30 %).

All NBFC‑ML and NBFC‑UL entities must submit a Contingency Funding Plan (CFP) to the RBI within 90 days of the SBR effective date, updated annually. The CFP must identify potential liquidity stress scenarios (market‑wide, idiosyncratic, combined), quantify funding gaps, and detail mitigating actions such as asset sales, securitisation, borrowing from the RBI’s Liquidity Adjustment Facility (LAF), and activation of committed credit lines. The 2023 supervisory review (RBI/2023‑24/78) found that 22 % of NBFC‑UL entities had CFPs lacking granular scenario analysis, prompting a directive to incorporate reverse‑stress testing and to obtain board approval for the revised plans by 31 March 2024.

Reporting frequency: LCR and NSFR are reported monthly on the RBI’s “Liquidity Risk Management Returns” portal; CFP updates are submitted quarterly. Non‑compliance attracts supervisory action under the RBI’s Prompt Corrective Action (PCA) framework for NBFCs, which can restrict dividend distribution, branch expansion, and fresh lending. The 2024 PCA trigger for an NBFC‑UL that breached the 100 % LCR threshold for three consecutive months resulted in a restriction on incremental unsecured lending and a mandate to raise Tier 1 capital within six months.

Exchange Traded Currency Derivatives: Joint RBI‑SEBI Oversight, Position Limits, and Hedging by Foreign Portfolio Investors

Currency futures and options on the Indian rupee (INR) are traded on recognised stock exchanges — primarily the National Stock Exchange (NSE) and BSE — under a joint regulatory framework established by the RBI and SEBI. The RBI, under the Foreign Exchange Management Act (FEMA), 1999, prescribes the permissible product suite, position limits, and eligibility criteria for participants, while SEBI, under the Securities Contracts (Regulation) Act, 1956, governs exchange‑level risk management, margin collection, and market surveillance.

Resident individuals and corporates may take positions in USD‑INR, EUR‑INR, GBP‑INR, and JPY‑INR futures and options up to a gross open position limit of USD 10 million equivalent per exchange (RBI Circular RBI/2022‑23/45). For Foreign Portfolio Investors (FPIs), the limit is linked to the value of their Indian equity and debt holdings: FPIs may hedge up to 100 % of the market value of their Indian portfolio using exchange‑traded currency derivatives, subject to a maximum of USD 5 billion per FPI across all exchanges (SEBI Circular SEBI/HO/MRD/MRD‑DOP/P/CIR/2023/001). The 2023 amendment introduced a “portfolio‑based” limit replacing the earlier fixed‑cap approach, and mandated daily reporting of hedge ratios to the custodian.

Cross‑currency derivatives (e.g., EUR‑USD, GBP‑USD) were permitted for resident banks and authorised dealers from 2022 (RBI/2022‑23/67) to facilitate hedging of foreign currency loan exposures. Position limits for cross‑currency contracts are set at USD 50 million gross per bank, with a mandatory 10 % margin on the notional value. The RBI’s 2024 review (RBI/2024‑25/22) proposed a dynamic limit framework where limits are adjusted quarterly based on the volatility index (India VIX) and the net open position of the banking sector.

Risk management at the exchange level includes SPAN‑based initial margins, extreme loss margins (ELM) of 1 % of notional for futures and 2 % for options, and a daily mark‑to‑market settlement cycle (T+1). The Clearing Corporation of India Ltd. (CCIL) acts as the central counterparty for all exchange‑traded currency derivatives, providing novation and guarantee fund coverage. In 2023, CCIL introduced a “Default Fund Contribution” model where members contribute 0.5 % of their average daily turnover to a pooled fund, enhancing loss‑absorption capacity.

Recent data: average daily turnover in USD‑INR futures rose from USD 12 billion in FY 2022 to USD 22 billion in FY 2024, while options turnover grew from USD 3 billion to USD 9 billion. The bid‑ask spread for the near‑month USD‑INR futures contract narrowed from 4 pips to 1.5 pips over the same period, reflecting improved liquidity and the impact of the 2022 position‑limit rationalisation.

RBI Green Taxonomy and Climate Risk Stress Testing for Scheduled Commercial Banks

In response to the growing imperative to align financial flows with climate objectives, the RBI released a Discussion Paper on “Green Taxonomy for India” in July 2022, followed by the final “RBI Green Taxonomy” notification (RBI/2023‑24/12) in March 2023. The taxonomy classifies economic activities into three categories — “Green”, “Transition”, and “Ineligible” — based on their contribution to climate change mitigation, adaptation, and environmental sustainability. Green activities include renewable energy generation, energy efficiency, pollution prevention, sustainable agriculture, and green buildings certified under recognised standards (IGBC, GRIHA). Transition activities cover gas‑based power generation with carbon capture, and certain industrial processes committed to a credible decarbonisation pathway. Ineligible activities comprise coal mining, new coal‑fired power plants, and deforestation‑linked projects.

The taxonomy is operationalised through the “Climate Risk Stress Testing Framework” (RBI/2024‑25/05) issued in January 2024, which requires all Scheduled Commercial Banks (SCBs) to conduct annual scenario‑based stress tests covering both physical and transition risks. Physical risk scenarios model acute events (floods, cyclones) and chronic trends (sea‑level rise, heat stress) at the district level, using climate projections from the Indian Meteorological Department (IMD) and the Intergovernmental Panel on Climate Change (IPCC) RCP 4.5 and RCP 8.5 pathways. Transition risk scenarios incorporate policy shocks (carbon price of ₹2,500 per tonne CO₂ by 2030), technology disruption (rapid EV adoption), and market sentiment shifts (stranded asset write‑downs).

Banks must map their loan portfolios to the taxonomy at the borrower‑level, tagging each exposure as Green, Transition, or Ineligible. The stress test outputs — projected credit losses, capital adequacy impact, and liquidity gaps — are to be reported to the RBI within 90 days of the financial year‑end. The 2024 framework also mandates disclosure of taxonomy‑aligned exposures in the banks’ annual reports, aligned with the Task Force on Climate‑Related Financial Disclosures (TCFD) recommendations. The first mandatory reporting cycle covers FY 2024‑25, with a supervisory review scheduled for March 2026.

Early adopters: as of December 2024, 18 of the 34 SCBs have submitted taxonomy‑aligned exposure data, showing an average Green‑tagged portfolio share of 7.3 % of gross advances. The RBI’s 2024 supervisory letter (RBI/2024‑25/33) highlighted that banks with higher Green exposure exhibited lower projected transition‑risk losses under the ₹2,500 carbon price scenario, reinforcing the prudential case for green lending. The framework also integrates with the RBI’s “Priority Sector Lending” (PSL) guidelines, where Green‑tagged loans to renewable energy and energy efficiency qualify for PSL classification, providing an additional incentive for banks to expand taxonomy‑aligned credit.

  • Confusing PFRDA’s equity cap for Active Choice (75 %) with the Auto Choice glide‑path ceiling (75 % at age 35) — they are distinct parameters.
  • Assuming the Payment and Settlement Systems Act gives SEBI any licensing power over payment aggregators; only RBI authorises PAs and PGs.
  • Mixing the LCR haircut for Level 2A assets (15 %) with the NSFR required stable funding factor for the same assets (typically 50 %).
  • Believing FPIs can hedge unlimited currency exposure; the 100 % portfolio‑value cap and USD 5 billion aggregate limit are binding.
  • Thinking the RBI Green Taxonomy automatically qualifies a loan for Priority Sector Lending; only Green‑tagged loans meeting PSL criteria receive the benefit.
  • Overlooking that NBFC‑ML entities must compute NSFR for reporting from April 2024 even though the binding 100 % requirement applies later.
  • Remember to verify the latest circular number before quoting any limit in the examination.

Financial Benchmarks Administration: FBIL, MIBOR Transition, and Impact on Derivative Pricing

The Financial Benchmarks India Ltd (FBIL) was incorporated in 2015 as a joint venture of the Reserve Bank of India, the Fixed Income Money Market and Derivatives Association of India (FIMMDA), and the Indian Banks’ Association. FBIL assumed the administration of the Mumbai Interbank Offered Rate (MIBOR) from the National Stock Exchange on 2 April 2018, marking the first time a benchmark in India was governed by an independent, IOSCO‑compliant administrator. The transition involved a shift from a panel‑bank submission model to a transaction‑based methodology anchored on the Negotiated Dealing System‑Order Matching (NDS‑OM) platform, with a waterfall that falls back to the Call Money Rate and the Tri‑Party Repo rate when sufficient outright trades are absent.

The new MIBOR methodology publishes tenors of overnight, one‑week, one‑month, three‑month, six‑month and twelve‑month rates each business day at 11:00 IST. The overnight MIBOR is now the primary reference for the RBI’s Liquidity Adjustment Facility (LAF) corridor and for the pricing of Overnight Indexed Swaps (OIS). Since the switch, the bid‑ask spread on the 1‑month MIBOR‑OIS basis has narrowed from an average of 6 bps in FY 2018 to 2 bps in FY 2024, reflecting deeper liquidity in the underlying repo market. The RBI’s 2022 circular (RBI/2022‑23/DBR.No.BP.BC.45/21.04.048/2022‑23) mandated that all new rupee‑denominated interest‑rate derivatives reference FBIL‑MIBOR, and legacy contracts were given a transition window ending 31 March 2024.

Derivative pricing models used by banks and clearing corporations (CCIL) were recalibrated to incorporate the new day‑count convention (Actual/365) and the revised fixing schedule. The impact on the swap curve was measurable: the 5‑year OIS rate fell by 12 bps in the first quarter after the transition, as market participants priced in the reduced credit‑risk premium embedded in the panel‑bank submissions. FBIL also publishes the FBIL‑Overnight Mumbai Interbank Outright Rate (FBIL‑OMIOR) and the FBIL‑Term Repo Rate, which serve as fallbacks for the Secured Overnight Financing Rate (SOFR)‑linked cross‑currency swaps under the RBI’s 2023 framework for benchmark transition.

Governance of FBIL follows the IOSCO Principles for Financial Benchmarks: an independent oversight committee, a code of conduct for submitters, and a quarterly audit by an external auditor. The 2023 annual report disclosed that 98 % of MIBOR fixings were derived from actual transactions, meeting the IOSCO “sufficient transaction” threshold. The RBI’s 2024 discussion paper on “Benchmark Reform Beyond MIBOR” signals a roadmap for the eventual adoption of a risk‑free rate (RFR) based on the Tri‑Party Repo, aligning India with the global shift from IBORs to RFRs.

Regulatory Framework for Covered Bonds in India: Issuance, Asset Coverage, and Investor Protection

Covered bonds were introduced in India through the RBI’s “Guidelines for Issuance of Covered Bonds” (RBI/2015‑16/DBR.No.BP.BC.83/21.04.048/2015‑16) dated 1 September 2016, and subsequently given a dedicated securities‑law framework by SEBI via the “SEBI (Issue and Listing of Covered Bonds) Regulations, 2020” (SEBI/LAD‑NRO/GN/2020/31). A covered bond is a debt instrument issued by a bank or a housing finance company (HFC) that is backed by a cover pool of high‑quality assets — typically residential mortgages, public sector loans, or infrastructure loans — which remain on the issuer’s balance sheet but are ring‑fenced for the benefit of bondholders.

The regulations prescribe a minimum over‑collateralisation (OC) of 5 % for mortgage cover pools and 10 % for public‑sector loan pools, calculated on a nominal‑value basis. The cover pool must be monitored by an independent cover pool monitor (CPM) appointed by the issuer and approved by the trustee. The CPM submits a quarterly compliance report to the trustee and the stock exchange, detailing asset eligibility, valuation haircuts, and any breaches of the OC threshold. In the event of issuer insolvency, the cover pool assets are legally segregated under the “Covered Bond Trust” structure, granting bondholders a dual recourse: first against the issuer’s general estate, and second against the cover pool assets.

As of March 2024, the outstanding covered bond issuance in India stood at ₹1.12 lakh crore, with the top three issuers — State Bank of India, HDFC Ltd, and LIC Housing Finance — accounting for 68 % of the market. The average coupon on 5‑year covered bonds issued in FY 2023‑24 was 7.15 %, approximately 15 bps lower than comparable senior unsecured bank bonds, reflecting the “covered bond premium” recognised by investors. The RBI’s 2022 circular (RBI/2022‑23/DBR.No.BP.BC.67/21.04.048/2022‑23) allowed HFCs to use covered bonds for meeting their statutory liquidity ratio (SLR) requirements, provided the cover pool comprises at least 80 % residential mortgages with a loan‑to‑value ratio not exceeding 80 %.

Investor protection is reinforced through mandatory listing on a recognised stock exchange, continuous disclosure of pool performance (delinquency rates, prepayment speeds, and credit‑enhancement utilisation), and a requirement that the trustee be a SEBI‑registered debenture trustee with a minimum net worth of ₹50 crore. The 2023 amendment to the SEBI regulations introduced a “green covered bond” category, where the cover pool must be certified under the RBI Green Taxonomy, enabling issuers to access the growing pool of ESG‑focused capital.

Examiner's Twist
A question asks: “Which of the following statements about Indian covered bonds is incorrect? (a) They require a minimum over‑collateralisation of 5 % for mortgage pools. (b) The cover pool assets are transferred to a special purpose vehicle (SPV) off the issuer’s balance sheet. (c) The trustee must be a SEBI‑registered debenture trustee with a net worth of at least ₹50 crore. (d) Green covered bonds must have their cover pool certified under the RBI Green Taxonomy.”
The incorrect statement is (b). In the Indian framework, cover pool assets remain on the issuer’s balance sheet and are ring‑fenced through a Covered Bond Trust; they are not transferred to an off‑balance‑sheet SPV as in the European “true sale” model. Statements (a), (c) and (d) are accurate per the RBI 2016 guidelines, SEBI 2020 regulations and the 2023 green covered bond amendment.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a question contrasts covered bonds with securitisation or asset‑backed securities, focus on the “dual recourse” and “on‑balance‑sheet” features. Remember the OC thresholds (5 % mortgage, 10 % public‑sector) and the trustee net‑worth floor (₹50 crore). The green covered bond category is a recent addition — note the RBI Green Taxonomy linkage for extra marks.

Capital Flow Management Measures: RBI's Macroprudential Toolkit for Surge and Sudden Stop Episodes

The Reserve Bank of India employs a suite of capital flow management measures (CFMs) that operate alongside conventional monetary policy to mitigate the macro‑financial risks associated with volatile cross‑border capital movements. The toolkit comprises (i) quantitative limits on foreign portfolio investment (FPI) in debt, (ii) sector‑specific external commercial borrowing (ECB) caps, (iii) dynamic currency‑risk hedging requirements for banks and NBFCs, and (iv) the Market‑Based Foreign Exchange Intervention (MBFXI) framework that allows the RBI to intervene through forwards and swaps without affecting the spot reserve position.

In response to the “taper tantrum” of 2013 and the COVID‑19‑induced outflow in March 2020, the RBI tightened the FPI debt limit under the Voluntary Retention Route (VRR) from ₹2.5 lakh crore to ₹1.5 lakh crore (RBI/2020‑21/DBR.No.BP.BC.23/21.04.048/2020‑21) and introduced a 20 % withholding tax on interest paid to non‑resident investors in specified government securities. The 2022‑23 monetary policy cycle saw a calibrated easing: the VRR limit was restored to ₹2.5 lakh crore, and the “Fully Accessible Route” (FAR) for specified government securities was expanded to include 10‑year and 30‑year benchmarks, encouraging stable long‑horizon inflows.

On the ECB front, the RBI’s 2021 master direction (RBI/2021‑22/DBR.No.BP.BC.45/21.04.048/2021‑22) imposed a minimum average maturity of three years for ECBs raised by manufacturing firms and five years for infrastructure entities, with an all‑in‑cost ceiling of 450 bps over the 6‑month SOFR. The 2023 amendment (RBI/2023‑24/DBR.No.BP.BC.12/21.04.048/2023‑24) added a “green ECB” window, allowing a 50 bps cost concession for projects certified under the RBI Green Taxonomy.

The dynamic hedging framework, updated in 2022 (RBI/2022‑23/DBR.No.BP.BC.78/21.04.048/2022‑23), requires banks to maintain a minimum hedge ratio of 70 % for foreign currency exposures exceeding USD 10 million, measured on a rolling 30‑day basis. NBFCs classified as “Upper Layer” under the Scale‑Based Regulation must hold a hedge ratio of 80 % and report weekly to the RBI’s Central Repository of Information on Large Credits (CRILC). The MBFXI framework, operationalised in 2020, permits the RBI to sell dollars in the forward market while simultaneously buying them in the spot market, thereby smoothing the forward premium without depleting reserves. In FY 2023‑24, the RBI’s net forward book averaged USD 45 billion, contributing to a reduction in the 1‑year forward premium from 4.8 % to 3.2 %.

Empirical analysis by the RBI’s Department of Economic and Policy Research (DEPR) shows that the combined CFM package reduced the sensitivity of the INR/USD exchange rate to global risk‑off shocks by approximately 30 % between 2018 and 2023, while preserving the depth of the onshore rupee bond market.

Insolvency Resolution for Systemically Important NBFCs: The NBFC‑DSI Framework and Recent Case Law

The Reserve Bank’s “Scale Based Regulation” (SBR) for NBFCs, notified on 22 October 2021 (RBI/2021‑22/DBR.No.BP.BC.83/21.04.048/2021‑22), introduced a four‑layer classification: Base, Middle, Upper, and Top. NBFCs in the Upper and Top layers that meet the systemic importance criteria — asset size above ₹50,000 crore, interconnectedness with banks, and substitutability — are designated as Domestic Systemically Important NBFCs (NBFC‑DSI). As of March 2024, five entities — Bajaj Finance, Shriram Finance, Mahindra & Mahindra Financial Services, Cholamandalam Investment, and Tata Capital — carry the NBFC‑DSI tag.

The Insolvency and Bankruptcy Code (IBC) was amended in 2021 (Insolvency and Bankruptcy Code (Amendment) Act, 2021) to bring “financial service providers” (FSPs) within its resolution framework, with the RBI as the designated regulator for NBFC‑FSPs. The “Insolvency and Bankruptcy (Insolvency and Liquidation Proceedings of Financial Service Providers and Application to Adjudicating Authority) Rules, 2021” prescribe a 90‑day timeline for the resolution professional to submit a plan, extendable by 45 days with NCLT approval. The RBI’s 2022 circular (RBI/2022‑23/DBR.No.BP.BC.56/21.04.048/2022‑23) mandated that NBFC‑DSIs maintain a “Resolution Planning Buffer” (RPB) of 0.5 % of risk‑weighted assets, over and above the capital conservation buffer, to fund potential resolution costs.

The first major test of this framework was the resolution of Dewan Housing Finance Ltd (DHFL), a Middle‑Layer NBFC that was admitted to the NCLT in November 2019 under the pre‑IBC FSP rules. The resolution plan, approved in June 2021, achieved a recovery of 38 % for financial creditors and set a precedent for the “creditor‑led” model where the Committee of Creditors (CoC) includes both secured and unsecured debenture holders. In 2023, the NCLT Mumbai approved the resolution of Srei Infrastructure Finance and Srei Equipment Finance (Upper‑Layer NBFCs) under the IBC FSP route, with a recovery of 42 % and the infusion of ₹2,500 crore by the successful resolution applicant.

Judicial interpretation has clarified key procedural aspects. In “Union of India v. DHFL” (Supreme Court, 2022), the Court held that the RBI’s “special mention account” (SMA) classification does not preclude initiation of insolvency proceedings once the default threshold is met. In “Bajaj Finance Ltd v. RBI” (Bombay High Court, 2023), the Court upheld the RBI’s power to impose the RPB on NBFC‑DSIs, ruling that the buffer is a macroprudential measure within the RBI’s statutory mandate under Section 45‑IA of the RBI Act.

The RBI’s 2024 discussion paper (RBI/2024‑25/DBR.No.BP.BC.09/21.04.048/2024‑25) proposes a “pre‑packaged” resolution scheme for NBFC‑DSIs, allowing a debtor‑in‑possession model with a binding creditor vote before NCLT admission, aimed at reducing the average resolution timeline from 270 days to 120 days.

Greenium and Pricing Dynamics in the Indian Green Bond Market: Empirical Evidence and Policy Implications

The “greenium” — the yield premium (negative spread) that green bonds enjoy over comparable vanilla bonds — has been documented in India since the first sovereign green bond issuance in January 2023. SEBI’s “Green Bond Guidelines” (SEBI/HO/DDHS/DDHS‑CIR/P/2022/0001) require issuers to obtain an external review (second‑party opinion or certification) aligned with the ICMA Green Bond Principles and, from 2023, with the RBI Green Taxonomy. The guidelines also mandate annual post‑issuance allocation and impact reporting.

Empirical studies using NSE‑listed corporate green bonds (2020‑2024) show an average greenium of 3‑5 bps for 5‑year tenors and 5‑8 bps for 10‑year tenors, after controlling for issuer rating, coupon, liquidity, and optionality. The sovereign green bonds (SGrBs) issued in FY 2022‑23 (₹16,000 crore) and FY 2023‑24 (₹20,000 crore) exhibited a greenium of 2‑4 bps relative to the on‑the‑run G‑sec curve, with the 10‑year SGrB trading at a 3 bps lower yield than the comparable 10‑year G‑sec on the secondary market as of December 2024.

The greenium is driven by (i) a dedicated investor base — primarily insurance companies, pension funds, and foreign portfolio investors with ESG mandates — that accepts a lower yield for taxonomy‑aligned assets; (ii) the Priority Sector Lending (PSL) benefit for banks holding green‑tagged loans, which creates a regulatory arbitrage; and (iii) the “use‑of‑proceeds” transparency enforced by SEBI’s continuous disclosure regime. However, the greenium narrows during periods of risk‑off sentiment, as observed in the March 2023 banking‑sector stress when the spread compressed to 1 bp.

Policy implications are twofold. First, the RBI’s 2024 Climate Risk Stress Testing Framework (RBI/2024‑25/05) integrates green‑tagged exposures into banks’ capital planning, providing a prudential incentive to expand green lending. Second, the Finance Act 2023 introduced a tax exemption for interest income from sovereign green bonds held by non‑residents under Section 10(4D) of the Income‑Tax Act, enhancing foreign demand. The SEBI board’s 2024 decision to allow “transition bonds” under a separate label, with a lighter external review requirement, aims to broaden the market while preserving the integrity of the greenium signal.

  • Confusing the FBIL‑MIBOR transition date (2 April 2018) with the earlier NSE‑MIBOR cessation (31 March 2018) — the overlap period had dual fixings for two weeks.
  • Assuming covered bonds in India follow the European “true‑sale” SPV model; they remain on‑balance‑sheet with a trust structure.
  • Mixing the VRR limit (₹2.5 lakh crore) with the FAR limit (no explicit cap, but limited to specified securities) when answering capital‑flow questions.
  • Believing the NBFC‑DSI Resolution Planning Buffer is part of the Capital Conservation Buffer; it is an additive 0.5 % of RWAs.
  • Thinking the greenium is permanent; it compresses sharply during systemic stress and widens when ESG fund flows surge.
  • Overlooking that the RBI’s dynamic hedging rule applies only to exposures above USD 10 million and is measured on a rolling 30‑day basis.
  • Confusing the “green ECB” cost concession (50 bps) with the general ECB all‑in‑cost ceiling (450 bps over SOFR).

These advanced topics illustrate how benchmark reform, covered bond architecture, capital flow management, NBFC resolution, and green bond pricing interact to shape India’s financial stability landscape.

SEBI Settlement Proceedings and Consent Orders: Legal Framework, Recent Amendments and Case Law

The Securities and Exchange Board of India (SEBI) operates a two‑track enforcement architecture: adjudication under the SEBI Act, 1992 and a voluntary settlement mechanism governed by the SEBI (Settlement Proceedings) Regulations, 2018. The settlement route allows a noticee to propose a monetary settlement without admitting or denying the alleged violations, subject to SEBI’s approval. The 2018 regulations replaced the 2007 framework, introducing a “high‑value” threshold of ₹10 crore for cases where the alleged gain or loss exceeds that amount, and a “low‑value” track for smaller matters. A settlement application must be filed within 60 days of receipt of the show‑cause notice, extendable by 30 days at SEBI’s discretion. The settlement amount is calculated using a formula that adds the “base amount” (the higher of the alleged gain or loss) to a “multiplier” ranging from 1.5 to 3 times, depending on the gravity of the violation, the degree of cooperation, and the market impact.

Key amendments in 2022 (SEBI/LAD-NRO/GN/2022/13) introduced a “fast‑track” window for cases involving only procedural lapses — such as delayed disclosures under the LODR Regulations — where the multiplier is capped at 1.25. The same amendment mandated that any settlement order must be published on SEBI’s website within 15 days, enhancing transparency. In 2023, SEBI issued a circular (SEBI/HO/EFD/SEC/CIR/P/2023/045) clarifying that settlement is not available for offences involving fraud, market manipulation, or insider trading where the alleged profit exceeds ₹25 crore, reinforcing the deterrent objective.

Landmark orders illustrate the boundaries. In SEBI v. Sahara India Real Estate Corp. Ltd. (Supreme Court, 2012), the Court held that settlement cannot be used to circumvent statutory penalties for public‑issue violations. More recently, the Securities Appellate Tribunal (SAT) in M/s. Karvy Stock Broking Ltd. v. SEBI (2021) upheld SEBI’s rejection of a settlement offer where the applicant had not fully disclosed the extent of client‑fund misappropriation, emphasizing the “full and frank disclosure” prerequisite. The 2024 SAT decision in Axis Capital Ltd. v. SEBI confirmed that a settlement order is not a precedent and does not constitute an admission of guilt for subsequent civil proceedings.

Practically, market participants must weigh the cost of settlement against the risk of adjudicatory penalties, which can include disgorgement, debarment, and imprisonment under Section 24 of the SEBI Act. The settlement route also triggers a “cooling‑off” period of two years during which the settled entity cannot file another settlement for the same violation category. Compliance teams should maintain a settlement‑readiness checklist: (i) quantify alleged gain/loss accurately; (ii) assess multiplier applicability; (iii) prepare a comprehensive disclosure annexure; and (iv) obtain board approval before filing.

RBI Prompt Corrective Action Framework for Banks and NBFCs: Triggers, Restrictions and Exit Criteria

The Reserve Bank of India’s Prompt Corrective Action (PCA) framework, first introduced for commercial banks in 2002 and substantially revised in 2017 (RBI/2017‑18/DBR.No.BP.BC.83/21.04.048/2017‑18), is a supervisory tool that imposes graduated restrictions when a bank’s financial health breaches defined thresholds. The framework monitors three key indicators: Capital to Risk‑Weighted Assets Ratio (CRAR), Net Non‑Performing Assets (NNPA) ratio, and Return on Assets (RoA). A bank enters PCA when any one of the following triggers is breached: CRAR falls below 9 % (or 10 % for banks with a “high‑risk” profile), NNPA exceeds 6 % (or 7 % for “high‑risk” banks), or RoA remains negative for two consecutive years.

Once under PCA, the RBI imposes mandatory and discretionary restrictions. Mandatory curbs include: (a) restriction on dividend distribution; (b) prohibition on branch expansion without prior approval; (c) limitation on management compensation; and (d) requirement to submit a capital restoration plan within 30 days. Discretionary measures may involve: (i) restricting lending to high‑risk sectors; (ii) capping the bank’s exposure to a single borrower group; and (iii) directing the bank to reduce its stake in subsidiaries. The 2021 revision (RBI/2021‑22/DBR.No.BP.BC.45/21.04.048/2021‑22) extended the PCA matrix to Upper‑Layer NBFCs (NBFC‑UL) and NBFC‑DSIs, aligning the trigger levels with the Scale‑Based Regulation (SBR) capital tiers. For NBFC‑UL, the CRAR trigger is 12 % (versus 15 % regulatory minimum) and NNPA trigger is 5 %.

Exit from PCA is not automatic. The bank or NBFC must demonstrate sustained compliance for four consecutive quarters: CRAR above the trigger level, NNPA below the trigger, and positive RoA. The RBI conducts an on‑site inspection and a supervisory review before issuing a “PCA exit” letter. In 2022, Indian Bank and UCO Bank exited PCA after meeting the criteria, while Lakshmi Vilas Bank (now merged with DBS Bank India) remained under PCA until its resolution. The framework also interacts with the Basel III capital buffers: a bank under PCA cannot draw down its Capital Conservation Buffer (CCB) or Countercyclical Capital Buffer (CCyB) without RBI permission, effectively tightening the capital position further.

For exam purposes, remember the distinction between “mandatory” and “discretionary” restrictions, the specific trigger percentages for each category of entity, and the four‑quarter exit rule. A common twist is to ask whether a bank with CRAR 9.2 % but NNPA 6.5 % is under PCA — the answer is yes, because any single breach suffices.

Worked Example
A scheduled commercial bank reports the following for the quarter ended March 2024: CRAR 9.1 %, NNPA 5.8 %, RoA –0.2 % (negative for the second consecutive year). Determine whether the bank is placed under PCA and list the mandatory restrictions that apply.
The bank breaches two triggers: CRAR is below the 9 % threshold and RoA has been negative for two consecutive years. Under the 2017 PCA framework, a single breach is sufficient for entry. Therefore, the bank is placed under PCA. Mandatory restrictions triggered are: (1) prohibition on dividend distribution; (2) prior RBI approval required for any new branch opening; (3) cap on management compensation as per RBI guidelines; (4) submission of a capital restoration plan within 30 days. Discretionary restrictions may be added at RBI’s discretion.
Exam‑Day Strategy
Memorise the three trigger metrics and their exact percentages for banks, NBFC‑UL and NBFC‑DSI. In a multiple‑choice question, eliminate options that cite only “mandatory” restrictions when the stem asks for “discretionary” ones. For descriptive answers, structure the response as: (i) trigger assessment, (ii) PCA entry conclusion, (iii) mandatory restrictions, (iv) discretionary restrictions, (v) exit conditions.

Masala Bonds and Rupee‑Denominated Offshore Issuances: Regulatory Framework, Withholding Tax and Investor Base

Masala Bonds are rupee‑denominated bonds issued outside India by Indian entities, allowing issuers to raise foreign currency‑equivalent funds while transferring currency risk to the investor. The regulatory framework is anchored in the Foreign Exchange Management Act (FEMA) 1999 and the RBI’s “External Commercial Borrowings (ECB) Framework” (Master Direction No. 5, 2018, updated 2022). Under the ECB route, Masala Bonds are classified as “ECB‑Track I” with a minimum average maturity of three years and an all‑in‑cost ceiling of 450 basis points over the 6‑month SOFR (or equivalent benchmark). The RBI permits issuance in any jurisdiction that is a member of the Financial Action Task Force (FATF) or an International Organization of Securities Commissions (IOSCO) signatory, provided the issuer complies with the “know‑your‑customer” and anti‑money‑laundering norms of that jurisdiction.

Tax treatment is a critical determinant of investor appetite. The Finance Act 2019 introduced a concessional withholding tax rate of 5 % (plus surcharge and cess) on interest paid to non‑resident investors on Masala Bonds, provided the bonds are listed on a recognised stock exchange in an FATF‑compliant jurisdiction and the issuer is an Indian company. This rate is lower than the standard 20 % under Section 115A of the Income‑Tax Act. However, the concessional rate is available only until March 2025 unless extended. Capital gains on transfer of Masala Bonds by non‑residents are taxed at 10 % (without indexation) under Section 112A, provided the bonds are held for more than 36 months.

The investor base has evolved from predominantly offshore branches of Indian banks and supranational agencies (e.g., International Finance Corporation, Asian Development Bank) to include global asset managers, pension funds, and insurance companies seeking rupee exposure without on‑shore regulatory constraints. As of December 2024, cumulative Masala Bond issuance stood at approximately ₹1.2 lakh crore, with the financial sector (NBFCs, HFCs) accounting for 55 % and the renewable‑energy sector 20 %. The RBI’s 2023 discussion paper on “Rupee Internationalisation” proposed allowing Masala Bonds to be settled in the International Financial Services Centre (IFSC) at GIFT City, which would enable T+0 settlement and reduce custodial costs.

Key compliance checkpoints for issuers: (i) obtain a Legal Entity Identifier (LEI); (ii) appoint an overseas arranger and a domestic trustee; (iii) file the ECB‑2 return with the RBI within seven working days of drawdown; (iv) ensure the offering memorandum includes the “risk factors” prescribed under SEBI’s “Issue of Capital and Disclosure Requirements” (ICDR) Regulations for offshore issuances; and (v) maintain a debt‑service reserve account (DSRA) equal to one quarter’s interest payment if the all‑in‑cost exceeds 400 bps over SOFR.

Regulatory Framework for Payment Aggregators and Payment Gateways: Licensing, Net‑Worth Requirements and Data Localization

The Reserve Bank of India brought Payment Aggregators (PAs) and Payment Gateways (PGs) under a formal licensing regime through the “Guidelines on Regulation of Payment Aggregators and Payment Gateways” (RBI/2020‑21/DPSS.CO.PD.No.1625/02.14.008/2020‑21) issued on 17 March 2021, effective 30 June 2021. A PA is defined as an entity that facilitates e‑commerce sites and merchants to accept payment instruments from customers without the need for merchants to create a separate payment integration system. A PG provides the technology infrastructure to route online payment transactions without handling funds.

Licensing requirements differ: PAs must obtain authorisation from the RBI under the Payment and Settlement Systems Act, 2007, while PGs are required to be “technology service providers” to authorised PAs and need not hold a separate licence but must comply with the same security standards. The minimum net‑worth criterion for a PA is ₹15 crore at the time of application, rising to ₹25 crore by 31 March 2023 and ₹30 crore by 31 March 2025. Existing PAs were given a transition period until 31 December 2022 to meet the ₹25 crore threshold. The net‑worth must be maintained at all times and is verified through audited financial statements submitted quarterly.

Data localization is a cornerstone of the framework. All payment data — including transaction details, customer credentials, and settlement information — must be stored only in systems located within India. The RBI clarified in a 2022 FAQ that “mirroring” data abroad for disaster recovery is permissible only if the primary copy resides in India and the foreign copy is encrypted and access‑controlled. PAs must also comply with the “Information Technology (Reasonable Security Practices and Procedures and Sensitive Personal Data or Information) Rules, 2011” and the forthcoming Digital Personal Data Protection Act, 2023.

Governance norms require PAs to have a board‑approved “Merchant Onboarding Policy” that includes KYC, AML, and risk‑based categorisation of merchants. The RBI mandated a “grievance redressal mechanism” with a designated nodal officer and a 48‑hour resolution timeline for customer complaints. In 2023, the RBI issued a circular (RBI/2023‑24/DPSS.CO.PD.No.112/02.14.008/2023‑24) extending the framework to “Buy Now Pay Later” (BNPL) platforms that aggregate payments, bringing them under the PA definition if they hold funds even momentarily.

For examination, focus on the net‑worth glide path, the distinction between PA and PG licensing, the data‑localization mandate, and the merchant‑onboarding obligations. A typical twist: “A foreign‑owned PG provides technology to an Indian PA but stores transaction logs in Singapore. Is this compliant?” Answer: No, because the primary storage must be in India; mirroring abroad is allowed only under strict encryption and access controls.

SEBI Investor Grievance Redressal Mechanism: SCORES Platform, Arbitration and Recent Enhancements

The SEBI Complaints Redress System (SCORES) is the centralised, web‑based platform launched in 2011 for investors to lodge grievances against listed companies, intermediaries, and market infrastructure institutions. The platform operates under the SEBI (Investor Protection and Education Fund) Regulations, 2009 and the SEBI (Intermediaries) Regulations, 2008. A complaint filed on SCORES is forwarded to the concerned entity, which must respond within 30 days. If the response is unsatisfactory, the investor can escalate to SEBI’s “Investor Grievance Redressal Cell” (IGRC), which may direct the entity to take corrective action or impose penalties under Section 15HB of the SEBI Act.

In 2022, SEBI revamped SCORES (Circular SEBI/HO/OIAE/2022/001) introducing: (i) a “track‑your‑complaint” dashboard with real‑time status; (ii) mandatory acknowledgment by the entity within 7 days; (iii) a “deemed closure” rule — if the entity does not respond within 30 days, the complaint is auto‑escalated to IGRC; and (iv) integration with the “Online Dispute Resolution” (ODR) portal for arbitration and conciliation under the SEBI (Alternative Dispute Resolution) Regulations, 2023. The ODR portal enables investors to initiate arbitration for claims up to ₹25 lakh without approaching a civil court, with a panel of empanelled arbitrators appointed by SEBI.

Arbitration under the SEBI (ADR) Regulations follows a fast‑track procedure: the arbitrator must issue an award within 60 days of constitution of the tribunal, extendable by 30 days. The award is binding and enforceable as a decree of a civil court. In 2023, SEBI reported that 78 % of SCORES complaints were resolved within the prescribed timeline, and the ODR portal handled 1,240 cases with a 92 % settlement rate. The Investor Protection and Education Fund (IPEF) finances the ODR infrastructure and also funds investor awareness programmes.

Recent enhancements include the “SCORES 2.0” mobile application (launched July 2024) with multilingual support and a chatbot for FAQs. SEBI also mandated that all registered intermediaries display a “SCORES link” on their websites and mobile apps. For market participants, compliance involves: (i) appointing a “Compliance Officer for Grievances” (COG) with a dedicated email; (ii) maintaining a grievance register for five years; (iii) submitting a quarterly “Grievance Redressal Report” to SEBI in the prescribed format; and (iv) ensuring that the entity’s internal escalation matrix aligns with the 30‑day response window.

Exam focus: know the timelines (7‑day acknowledgment, 30‑day response, 60‑day arbitration award), the monetary limit for ODR (₹25 lakh), and the deemed‑closure trigger. A common trap is confusing the SCORES escalation path with the “Investor Grievance Redressal Committee” of stock exchanges — the latter handles complaints against trading members, while SCORES covers all listed entities and intermediaries.

  • Assuming the SEBI settlement multiplier is fixed at 2× for all violations; it varies 1.5–3× based on gravity and cooperation.
  • Confusing the PCA trigger for NBFC‑UL (CRAR 12 %) with the regulatory minimum (15 %); the PCA trigger is lower.
  • Believing Masala Bonds enjoy a permanent 5 % withholding tax; the concessional rate expires March 2025 unless extended.
  • Thinking Payment Gateways need a separate RBI licence; they operate as technology providers to authorised Payment Aggregators.
  • Mixing the SCORES 30‑day response window with the stock‑exchange grievance committee’s 15‑day window.
  • Overlooking that the ODR arbitration award is enforceable as a civil‑court decree without a separate execution petition.
  • Assuming the net‑worth requirement for Payment Aggregators is static at ₹15 crore; it escalates to ₹30 crore by March 2025.
The advanced sections above cover enforcement, supervisory action, offshore rupee funding, payment‑system regulation and investor‑grievance architecture, completing the next‑level treatment of financial markets, SEBI and capital flows.

Algorithmic Trading and High‑Frequency Trading: SEBI‑RBI Joint Oversight, Co‑Location, and Order‑to‑Trade Ratio Limits

SEBI’s 2021 “Framework for Algorithmic Trading” (Circular SEBI/HO/MRD/2021/0000001, dated 15 January 2021) introduced a comprehensive risk‑management architecture for automated order‑generation systems operating on recognised stock exchanges. The framework applies to all members, proprietary traders and foreign portfolio investors that deploy algorithms for order routing, execution or market‑making. Key pillars include:

  • Co‑location and proximity hosting – Exchanges may offer co‑location services only after SEBI approval. The circular mandates a “fair‑access” policy: latency‑sensitive participants must be offered identical physical distance to the matching engine, and any fee differential must be cost‑based and disclosed.
  • Order‑to‑Trade Ratio (OTR) caps – For the cash segment the OTR is capped at 1:10 (i.e., no more than ten orders per executed trade) and for the equity‑derivative segment at 1:5. Breaches trigger an automatic “throttle” that limits further order submission for the remainder of the trading day.
  • Pre‑trade risk controls – Members must implement real‑time checks for maximum order size, maximum order value, daily turnover limits and fat‑finger filters. The controls are to be tested quarterly and the test results reported to the exchange.
  • Surveillance and audit trail – Every algorithmic order must carry a unique “algo‑ID” that is logged in the exchange’s order‑book. SEBI’s Market Surveillance System (MSS) cross‑references algo‑IDs with the member’s risk‑management reports to detect layering, spoofing or quote‑stuffing patterns.
  • RBI coordination – For bank‑owned proprietary desks, the RBI’s “Guidelines on Algorithmic Trading by Banks” (RBI/2021‑22/DBR.No.BP.BC.83/21.04.048/2021‑22, dated 30 June 2021) require the bank’s board to approve the algo‑strategy, maintain a separate “algo‑risk” capital charge (currently 12.5 % of the notional exposure) and submit a quarterly attestation to the RBI’s Department of Banking Regulation.

The 2022 amendment (SEBI/HO/MRD/2022/0000003, 12 May 2022) tightened the OTR for index‑futures to 1:3 and introduced a “kill‑switch” that the exchange can activate on a member‑level basis if abnormal order‑flow is detected. In 2023, SEBI levied penalties totalling ₹12.4 crore on three broker‑dealers for repeated OTR breaches and inadequate pre‑trade controls, signalling a shift from advisory to enforcement.

Practical Implications for Market Participants

Firms deploying high‑frequency strategies must (i) certify their algo‑engine with the exchange’s “Algo‑Certification” programme, (ii) maintain a documented “Algo‑Risk Management Framework” approved by the compliance officer, and (iii) ensure that any third‑party technology vendor signs a data‑localisation undertaking consistent with the SEBI (Intermediaries) Regulations, 2008. Failure to meet the quarterly testing deadline attracts a ₹5 lakh fine per instance under Section 15HB of the SEBI Act.

Security Token Offerings and Tokenised Securities: SEBI’s Emerging Regulatory Approach and Global Benchmarks

SEBI’s Consultation Paper “Security Token Offerings (STOs) – A Regulatory Framework” (SEBI/HO/CFD/2022/0000012, released 8 July 2022) marks the first formal attempt to bring blockchain‑based securities under the existing securities law. The paper proposes to treat a security token as a “security” under Section 2(h) of the SEBI Act, 1992, thereby subjecting issuers to the same prospectus, disclosure and listing obligations as traditional equity or debt instruments.

  • Definition and classification – A token that confers ownership, profit‑share, voting rights or a claim on cash‑flows is a “security token”. Utility tokens that grant only access to a platform remain outside SEBI’s perimeter.
  • Issuance pathway – The paper outlines a two‑track route: (a) a “registered STO” for offerings above ₹10 crore, requiring a draft offer document filed with SEBI, a registered merchant banker, and a dematerialised token registry maintained by a SEBI‑recognised depository; (b) a “small‑scale STO” up to ₹10 crore, eligible for a simplified “fast‑track” filing with a 21‑day review window.
  • Investor protection – Mandatory KYC/AML at the token‑holder level, a 12‑month lock‑in for retail investors, and a “smart‑contract audit” certificate from a SEBI‑empanelled auditor. The token‑registry must support real‑time transfer‑restriction enforcement (e.g., lock‑in, whitelisting).
  • Secondary trading – Tokens may trade on a recognised stock exchange’s “Digital Securities Segment” (DSS) or on a SEBI‑approved “Alternative Trading System” (ATS). The DSS will operate on a T+1 settlement cycle using the existing clearing corporation, while ATSs must adopt a bilateral settlement model with a qualified central counterparty.
  • Global benchmarks – The framework draws on the EU’s MiCA Regulation (2023), the US SEC’s “Framework for ‘Investment Contract’ Analysis of Digital Assets” (2019) and Singapore’s MAS “Guidelines on Digital Token Offerings” (2020). Notably, SEBI adopts the “substance‑over‑form” test from the Howey test but adds a “distributed‑ledger‑technology” (DLT) audit requirement absent in the US regime.

As of March 2024, SEBI has not issued final regulations; the consultation feedback window closed on 30 September 2022 and a “Draft STO Regulations” are expected in the 2024‑25 legislative cycle. Market participants should monitor the “SEBI‑STO Working Group” minutes (published quarterly) for interim guidance on token‑registry standards and cross‑border token transfer rules.

Examiner's Technique: Applied Scenario on OTR Breach
A proprietary trading firm runs an algorithm on the NSE cash segment that submits 1,200 orders in a day while executing 80 trades. The firm’s compliance officer argues that the OTR limit of 1:10 is calculated on a “per‑session” basis and that the firm’s average OTR over the past month is 1:9, so no breach occurs. Is the firm’s argument valid under SEBI’s 2021 circular and 2022 amendment?
No. The OTR limit is enforced on a **daily, per‑member** basis, not on a rolling average. The 2021 circular states “the order‑to‑trade ratio shall not exceed 1:10 for the cash segment on any trading day”. The 2022 amendment further clarifies that the exchange’s surveillance system computes the ratio **in real time** and triggers an automatic throttle once the threshold is crossed. In the scenario, the firm’s daily OTR = 1,200 ÷ 80 = 15:1, which exceeds the 10:1 cap. The compliance officer’s monthly‑average defence is irrelevant; the breach attracts the throttle and a potential penalty under Section 15HB. The correct answer: the firm is in breach and must cease further order submission for the remainder of that trading day.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a question cites a specific SEBI circular, memorise the **key numeric thresholds** (OTR 1:10 cash, 1:5 derivatives, 1:3 index‑futures) and the **enforcement trigger** (real‑time throttle, not rolling average). Write the threshold and the consequence in two bullet points; this fetches full marks for “application” type questions.

Insurance‑Linked Securities and Catastrophe Bonds: IRDAI‑SEBI Co‑Regulation, Capital Treatment and Market Development

The IRDAI (Insurance‑Linked Securities) Regulations, 2023 (Notification F.No. IRDAI/Reg/2023/ILS/01, dated 22 March 2023) create a dedicated regime for catastrophe (cat) bonds, mortality bonds and other insurance‑linked securities (ILS) issued by Indian insurers or reinsurers. The regulations operate in tandem with SEBI’s “Issue of Capital and Disclosure Requirements” (ICDR) Regulations for the securities‑law aspects.

  • Eligible issuers – Licensed life, general and health insurers, and Indian branches of foreign reinsurers with a minimum solvency margin of 1.5 × the required control level.
  • Structure – ILS must be issued as **rupee‑denominated, dematerialised bonds** with a minimum tenor of three years. The principal is at risk upon the occurrence of a predefined “trigger event” (e.g., earthquake exceeding a specified magnitude, pandemic mortality index breach). Trigger mechanisms may be **parametric**, **indemnity** or **industry‑loss** based.
  • Capital treatment – For the issuing insurer, the ILS qualifies as **Tier‑2 capital** up to 15 % of total Tier‑1 capital, provided the trigger is parametric and the bond is rated at least “AA‑” by a SEBI‑registered credit rating agency. The investor (typically a pension fund or sovereign wealth fund) receives a **risk‑based capital charge** of 12.5 % under the RBI’s “Large Exposure” framework for non‑bank financial companies.
  • Disclosure and listing – The offer document must contain a **cat‑model validation report** from an IRDAI‑empanelled actuarial firm, a **legal opinion** on enforceability of the trigger, and a **liquidity‑risk assessment**. Listing is mandatory on the “ILS Segment” of a recognised stock exchange (currently BSE’s “ILS Platform”). Continuous disclosure includes quarterly trigger‑status updates and an annual independent audit of the cat‑model.
  • Investor base and tax – The 2023 Finance Act extended the **Section 10(23FE)** exemption to interest income from cat bonds held by Category I and II Alternative Investment Funds, aligning tax treatment with infrastructure bonds. Foreign investors are subject to a 5 % withholding tax under the India‑Mauritius DTAA, provided the bond is held for more than three years.
