As of December 2023, two cat bonds have been listed: a ₹1,200 crore earthquake‑trigger bond by a leading general insurer (rated AA) and a ₹800 crore pandemic‑mortality bond by a life insurer (rated AA‑). The IRDAI‑SEBI joint working group meets quarterly to review model‑risk standards and to explore a **re‑insurance‑linked securitisation** pipeline for flood and cyclone risks.

Rupee Vostro Accounts and Cross‑Border Settlement: RBI’s Framework for Internationalising the Rupee in Trade Finance

RBI’s “Rupee Vostro Accounts for International Trade Settlement” circular (RBI/2022‑23/DBR.No.BP.BC.45/21.04.048/2022‑23, dated 11 July 2022) permits authorised dealer (AD) banks to open **rupee‑denominated vostro accounts** for non‑resident banks from partner jurisdictions. The framework aims to reduce reliance on the US dollar for bilateral trade and to deepen the offshore rupee market.

  • Eligible counterparties – Central banks or commercial banks from countries with which India has a **bilateral trade agreement** or a **currency swap arrangement** (e.g., Russia, UAE, Sri Lanka, Mauritius). As of March 2024, 18 jurisdictions have been notified.
  • Account mechanics – The non‑resident bank holds a **vostro account** in the AD bank’s books, denominated in INR. The AD bank maintains a corresponding **nostro account** in the foreign bank’s currency for settlement of the counterparty’s rupee obligations. All credits and debits are reported to RBI via the **R‑Returns** (daily) and **F‑Returns** (monthly).
  • Permitted transactions – (a) **Import/export invoicing** in INR; (b) **Trade‑credit** (buyer’s credit, supplier’s credit) denominated in INR; (c) **Investment** in Indian government securities (G‑Secs) and corporate bonds by the non‑resident bank, subject to the **Fully Accessible Route (FAR)** limits; (d) **Remittance** of dividends, interest and royalty payments in INR.
  • Risk controls – AD banks must assign a **country‑risk limit** (max 15 % of the bank’s Tier‑1 capital per jurisdiction) and a **counterparty‑risk limit** (max 5 % of Tier‑1 capital per non‑resident bank). The vostro balance must be **marked‑to‑market** daily using the RBI reference rate; any adverse movement beyond 2 % triggers a margin call.
  • Reporting and compliance – AD banks submit a **“Vostro Account Utilisation Report”** (Form V‑AUR) quarterly to the RBI’s Department of Regulation. The report captures transaction volumes, currency composition, and any breaches of the country‑risk limits. Non‑compliance attracts a penalty of ₹10 lakh per breach under Section 47A of the Banking Regulation Act, 1949.

By end‑2023, the aggregate rupee vostro balance across AD banks stood at ₹2.3 lakh crore, with Russia and the UAE accounting for 68 % of the total. The RBI’s 2024 “Review of Rupee Internationalisation” (Press Release 2024‑03‑15) indicated a roadmap to expand the framework to **services trade** (software, consulting) and to allow **non‑bank financial companies** to act as settlement agents, subject to a separate licensing regime.

Large Exposure and Concentration Risk Limits for NBFCs: RBI’s Scale‑Based Framework, Inter‑Connectedness Metrics and Supervisory Reporting

The RBI’s “Scale‑Based Regulation (SBR) for NBFCs” (RBI/2022‑23/DBR.No.BP.BC.112/21.04.048/2022‑23, dated 22 October 2022) introduced a **large‑exposure framework (LEF)** calibrated to the four‑layer NBFC classification (Base, Middle, Upper, Top). The LEF replaces the erstwhile single‑limit regime and aligns with the Basel Committee’s “Large Exposures” standards.

  • Exposure definition – “Exposure” includes on‑balance‑sheet credit (loans, advances, debt securities), off‑balance‑sheet commitments (guarantees, letters of credit), derivative counterparty credit risk (current exposure method) and **equity investments** in other financial entities. Intra‑group exposures are aggregated at the **consolidated NBFC‑group level**.
  • Limit tiers –
      • **NBFC‑Base & Middle**: Single‑counterparty limit 15 % of Tier‑1 capital; group‑counterparty limit 25 % of Tier‑1 capital.
      • **NBFC‑Upper (NBFC‑UL)**: Single‑counterparty limit 20 % of Tier‑1 capital; group‑counterparty limit 25 % of Tier‑1 capital.
      • **NBFC‑Top (systemically important)**: Single‑counterparty limit 15 % of Tier‑1 capital; group‑counterparty limit 20 % of Tier‑1 capital, with an **additional “inter‑connectedness” surcharge** of 5 % for exposures to other NBFC‑Top entities.
  • Inter‑connectedness metric – RBI computes a **Network‑Based Systemic Importance Score (NBSIS)** quarterly using bilateral exposure data from the **Central Repository of Information on Large Credits (CRILC)**. An NBFC‑Top whose NBSIS exceeds the 75th percentile must hold an extra **capital conservation buffer of 0.5 %** and submit a **“Concentration Risk Management Plan”** within 30 days.
  • Supervisory reporting – All NBFC‑UL and NBFC‑Top entities file **Form LE‑01 (Large Exposure Return)** monthly, detailing counterparty‑wise exposure, credit‑risk mitigation (collateral, netting agreements) and any breaches. The return is due by the 10th of the following month. Breaches attract a **prompt corrective action (PCA)** trigger: the NBFC must reduce the excess exposure within 90 days or face restrictions on fresh lending and dividend distribution.
  • Exemptions – Exposures to **sovereign (central/state government)**, **central bank**, **multilateral development banks** and **qualifying central counterparties (CCPs)** are exempt, provided they meet the “zero‑risk‑weight” criteria under the RBI’s capital adequacy framework.

In the first half of FY 2023‑24, RBI identified 12 NBFC‑ULs and 3 NBFC‑Tops that breached the single‑counterparty limit. Enforcement actions included a **₹25 crore penalty** on a large NBFC‑UL for a 23 % exposure to a single real‑estate developer and a **directive to unwind** the position within 60 days. The LEF is expected to be refined in the 2024‑25 review cycle, with a proposal to introduce a **dynamic limit** linked to the NBFC’s **credit‑risk‑weighted assets (CRWA)** growth rate.

  • Confusing the OTR daily cap with a rolling‑average limit – the throttle is triggered on the same trading day.
  • Assuming security tokens are automatically exempt from prospectus requirements – they are treated as securities under the SEBI Act.
  • Treating cat‑bond principal as risk‑free for the issuer’s capital adequacy – only parametric triggers with a minimum rating qualify for Tier‑2 treatment.
  • Believing rupee vostro accounts can be opened for any non‑resident bank – only banks from notified partner jurisdictions are eligible.
  • Applying the Base‑layer large‑exposure limit (15 %) to an NBFC‑Top – the Top layer has a stricter 15 % single‑counterparty limit plus an inter‑connectedness surcharge.
  • Overlooking the mandatory monthly LE‑01 filing deadline (10th of the following month) – late filing is a PCA trigger.
  • Mixing the 5 % withholding tax on Masala Bonds (which expires March 2025) with the 5 % rate on cat‑bond interest for foreign investors – the latter is treaty‑based and continues beyond 2025.

These advanced sections complete the next‑level treatment of financial markets, SEBI and capital flows.

SEBI's Emerging Framework for Sustainability‑Linked Bonds and Transition Bonds: Eligibility, Disclosure and Verification

SEBI introduced a dedicated framework for Sustainability‑Linked Bonds (SLBs) through its circular SEBI/HO/DDHS/CIR/P/2022/00000001 dated 15 June 2022, later refined in the “SLB Guidelines” of March 2023. Unlike green bonds, SLBs do not earmark proceeds for specific projects; instead, the issuer commits to predefined Key Performance Indicators (KPIs) and Sustainability Performance Targets (SPTs) that trigger a step‑up or step‑down in the coupon. The framework mandates that KPIs be material, measurable, and externally verifiable, and that SPTs be ambitious yet achievable, calibrated against a recognised baseline (e.g., Science‑Based Targets initiative). Issuers must obtain a Second‑Party Opinion (SPO) from an approved verifier before issuance and publish an annual SLB report detailing KPI performance, verified by the same or another independent verifier. The coupon adjustment mechanism is capped at 25 bps per trigger event to prevent excessive volatility. Transition Bonds, which finance the decarbonisation of high‑carbon assets, are not yet covered by a separate SEBI regulation; however, the 2023 consultation paper on “Transition Finance” proposes extending the SLB architecture to transition‑labelled instruments, requiring a credible transition plan aligned with the International Capital Market Association (ICMA) Transition Finance Principles. Until a formal rule is notified, issuers of transition‑labelled bonds rely on the existing green‑bond guidelines (SEBI/HO/DDHS/CIR/P/2021/00000001) and voluntary adherence to the Climate Bonds Initiative taxonomy. The SLB framework also integrates with the Business Responsibility and Sustainability Reporting (BRSR) core, requiring listed issuers to map SLB KPIs to BRSR indicators, thereby creating a unified ESG disclosure ecosystem.

RBI's Co‑Lending Model for Banks and NBFCs: Risk‑Sharing Mechanics, Credit Enhancement and Supervisory Reporting

The Reserve Bank of India operationalised the Co‑Lending Model (CLM) through the “Co‑Lending Model (CLM) for Banks and NBFCs” master direction (RBI/2020‑21/DBR.No.BP.BC.63/21.04.048/2020‑21) dated 5 November 2020, with subsequent amendments in 2021 and 2022. Under CLM, a bank and an NBFC jointly originate a loan, with the bank typically providing 80 % of the credit and the NBFC 20 %, though the ratio may vary between 70:30 and 90:10 subject to board approval. The model requires a legally binding co‑lending agreement specifying risk‑sharing, credit‑decision authority, and recovery responsibilities. The NBFC acts as the originating and servicing entity, while the bank retains the final credit approval. Credit enhancement is achieved through the NBFC’s first‑loss piece (the 20 % share) and, where agreed, a partial guarantee from the NBFC to the bank. Both entities must maintain independent credit‑risk assessment processes; the bank’s credit committee must approve each co‑lended exposure. RBI mandates that the aggregate co‑lended portfolio of a bank does not exceed 5 % of its net‑owned funds, and for an NBFC the co‑lended book cannot surpass 10 % of its Tier‑1 capital. Supervisory reporting is effected through the “Co‑Lending Return (CLR‑01)” filed quarterly within 21 days of quarter‑end, detailing counterparty‑wise exposure, risk‑weight, and any breaches of the prescribed limits. The 2022 amendment introduced a “stress‑testing” requirement: banks must simulate a 200 bps increase in NBFC‑level NPAs and assess capital adequacy impact. Non‑compliance attracts supervisory action under the Prompt Corrective Action (PCA) framework for banks and the Scale‑Based Regulation (SBR) for NBFCs.

Examiner's Twist
A question asks: “In the RBI’s Co‑Lending Model, the NBFC’s share is treated as first‑loss capital for the bank. Explain the regulatory rationale and the quantitative limit on the bank’s aggregate co‑lended portfolio.”
The NBFC’s 20 % share absorbs initial losses, protecting the bank’s 80 % senior tranche and aligning incentives for prudent origination. RBI caps the bank’s total co‑lended exposure at 5 % of its net‑owned funds to prevent concentration risk and ensure the model remains a supplement, not a substitute, for direct lending. The NBFC’s own exposure is limited to 10 % of its Tier‑1 capital under the Scale‑Based Regulation. Both limits are monitored via the quarterly CLR‑01 return.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a question mixes CLM with PCA or SBR triggers, first identify the entity (bank vs NBFC) and then apply the respective limit (5 % of net‑owned funds for banks, 10 % of Tier‑1 for NBFCs). Remember the 200 bps stress‑test requirement introduced in 2022 – it is a frequent “recent amendment” trap.

Large Exposure Framework for Scheduled Commercial Banks under Basel III: Counterparty Limits, Concentration Surcharge and Reporting Obligations

RBI’s “Large Exposure Framework (LEF) for Banks” (RBI/2019‑20/DBR.No.BP.BC.83/21.04.048/2019‑20) dated 31 December 2019 aligns Indian banks with the Basel Committee’s “Large Exposures” standard (BCBS 2014). The framework defines “exposure” comprehensively: on‑balance‑sheet credit (loans, advances, debt securities), off‑balance‑sheet items (guarantees, letters of credit, undrawn commitments), derivative counterparty credit risk (current exposure method), and equity investments in other financial entities. The single‑counterparty limit is set at 20 % of the bank’s Tier‑1 capital, while the group‑counterparty limit (including connected entities) is 25 % of Tier‑1 capital. For exposures to other Global Systemically Important Banks (G‑SIBs) or Domestic Systemically Important Banks (D‑SIBs), an additional concentration surcharge of 5 % of Tier‑1 capital applies, effectively tightening the single‑counterparty ceiling to 15 % for such counterparties. Intra‑group exposures are aggregated at the consolidated banking‑group level. Exemptions are granted for sovereign (central/state government), central bank, multilateral development banks, and qualifying central counterparties (CCPs) that meet the zero‑risk‑weight criteria under RBI’s capital adequacy norms. Banks must submit the “Large Exposure Return (LER‑01)” monthly, due by the 10th of the following month, detailing counterparty‑wise exposure, credit‑risk mitigation (collateral, netting agreements), and any limit breaches. A breach triggers a supervisory “Prompt Corrective Action” (PCA) – the bank must reduce the excess exposure within 90 days or face restrictions on fresh lending, dividend distribution, and branch expansion. The 2023 review proposed a dynamic limit linked to the bank’s credit‑risk‑weighted assets (CRWA) growth rate, but this has not yet been notified.

Regulatory Treatment of Outsourcing of Financial Services by Banks, NBFCs and Payment System Operators: RBI's 2023 Master Direction

RBI issued the “Master Direction – Outsourcing of Financial Services by Banks, NBFCs and Payment System Operators” (RBI/2023‑24/DBR.No.BP.BC.112/21.04.048/2023‑24) on 12 April 2023, superseding the 2017 guidelines. The direction applies to all scheduled commercial banks, NBFCs (including Housing Finance Companies), and authorised Payment System Operators (PSOs). It defines “outsourcing” as the use of a third‑party service provider to perform activities that the regulated entity would otherwise perform itself, excluding mere procurement of goods. The framework mandates a board‑approved outsourcing policy covering risk assessment, due‑diligence, contract management, and business‑continuity planning. Critical activities – core banking, credit underwriting, KYC/AML compliance, treasury operations, and settlement – may only be outsourced after a “materiality assessment” and with prior RBI intimation. Contracts must contain explicit clauses on data confidentiality, audit rights, sub‑contracting restrictions, and termination‑transition provisions. The regulated entity remains fully responsible for the outsourced activity’s regulatory compliance. A “Outsourcing Risk Register” must be maintained and reviewed quarterly. Reporting requirements include an annual “Outsourcing Return (OSR‑01)” filed within 30 days of the financial year‑end, detailing each outsourcing arrangement, risk rating, and any incidents. Non‑compliance attracts penalties under the Banking Regulation Act, 1949 (for banks), the RBI Act, 1934 (for NBFCs), and the Payment and Settlement Systems Act, 2007 (for PSOs). The 2023 direction also introduces a “concentration limit”: no single service provider may account for more than 30 % of the total outsourced critical‑activity spend of a regulated entity.

Central Counterparty Interoperability and Margin Harmonisation in India: SEBI‑RBI Joint Framework for Clearing Corporations

SEBI and RBI jointly released the “Framework for Interoperability of Central Counterparties (CCPs) and Margin Harmonisation” (SEBI/HO/MRD/MRD‑CCP/CIR/P/2022/00000001 and RBI/2022‑23/DBR.No.BP.BC.95/21.04.048/2022‑23) on 30 September 2022. The framework enables the three recognised CCPs – NSE Clearing Limited (NCL), Indian Clearing Corporation Limited (ICCL), and MCX Clearing Corporation Limited (MCXCCL) – to clear each other’s trades, thereby reducing fragmentation of collateral pools and systemic risk. Interoperability is achieved through a “Common Collateral Management System (CCMS)” that accepts eligible securities (government securities, high‑quality corporate bonds, cash) across CCPs, with a uniform haircut schedule prescribed by SEBI. Margin harmonisation aligns the Initial Margin (IM) methodology: all CCPs must adopt the “Standard Portfolio Analysis of Risk (SPAN)” model with identical risk‑parameter inputs (volatility scans, correlation matrices) for equity, currency, and interest‑rate derivatives. Variation Margin (VM) is settled on a T+0 basis using the Real‑Time Gross Settlement (RTGS) system. The framework mandates a “Default Fund” contribution calibrated to each CCP’s cleared volume, with a minimum of 0.5 % of the notional cleared. Cross‑CCP default management procedures are coordinated through a “Joint Default Management Committee” comprising representatives from SEBI, RBI, and the three CCPs. Reporting obligations include a daily “Interoperability Margin Report (IMR‑01)” submitted to both regulators by 18:00 IST, and a quarterly “CCP Resilience Assessment” covering stress‑test results, liquidity coverage, and cyber‑risk posture. The 2023 amendment extended interoperability to OTC interest‑rate swaps cleared under the “Regulatory Framework for Over‑the‑Counter Derivatives” (SEBI/HO/MRD/MRD‑OTC/CIR/P/2021/00000001), requiring bilateral novation to a CCP within 30 days of trade execution.

  • Confusing the SLB coupon‑step‑up cap (25 bps) with the green‑bond “greenium” – they are distinct pricing mechanisms.
  • Assuming the Co‑Lending Model permits the NBFC to hold the senior tranche – the bank always holds the senior 80 % share.
  • Applying the bank‑level single‑counterparty large‑exposure limit (20 % of Tier‑1) to NBFCs – NBFCs follow the Scale‑Based Regulation limits (15 %/20 %/25 %).
  • Treating outsourcing of core banking as a simple vendor contract – the 2023 Master Direction requires materiality assessment and prior RBI intimation.
  • Believing CCP interoperability eliminates the need for separate default funds – each CCP maintains its own default fund with a minimum 0.5 % contribution.
  • Overlooking the mandatory monthly LER‑01 filing deadline (10th of the following month) – late filing triggers PCA for banks.
  • Awareness of these traps sharpens answer precision in the examination.

SEBI Takeover Code: Open Offer Mechanics, Creeping Acquisition, and Competing Offer Dynamics

The SEBI (Substantial Acquisition of Shares and Takeovers) Regulations, 2011 ("Takeover Code") govern the acquisition of shares and voting rights in listed Indian companies. An open offer is triggered when an acquirer, along with persons acting in concert (PACs), acquires shares or voting rights that take their aggregate holding to 25 % or more of the total voting capital, or when an acquirer holding between 25 % and 75 % acquires additional shares exceeding 5 % of the voting capital in any financial year (the "creeping acquisition" limit). The open offer must be for at least 26 % of the total voting capital at a price not lower than the highest of: (i) the highest negotiated price per share for any acquisition in the 52 weeks preceding the public announcement; (ii) the volume-weighted average price (VWAP) for 60 trading days preceding the public announcement; (iii) the highest price paid by the acquirer or PACs for any acquisition in the 26 weeks preceding the public announcement; and (iv) the VWAP for 10 trading days preceding the public announcement. For infrequently traded shares, the price is determined under Regulation 8(2) using parameters such as return on net worth, book value, and earnings per share.

The creeping acquisition exemption allows an acquirer holding 25 %–75 % to acquire up to 5 % of voting capital per financial year without triggering an open offer, provided the acquisition does not result in the acquirer's holding exceeding 75 %. This 5 % limit is computed on the total voting capital as on the first day of the financial year. Acquisitions beyond 5 % require an open offer for the additional shares acquired. The 2020 amendment introduced a "voluntary open offer" framework: an acquirer holding less than 25 % may make a voluntary open offer for at least 26 % of voting capital, subject to the same pricing and procedural requirements. Competing offers may be announced within 15 working days of the detailed public statement (DPS) of the first offer. The competing offer must be for at least the same number of shares and at a price at least equal to the first offer. The first acquirer may revise the offer upward within 3 working days of the competing offer's DPS. This revision cycle continues until no further competing offers or revisions are made. The offer process, from public announcement to completion of payment, is governed by strict timelines: DPS within 5 working days of public announcement, draft letter of offer filed with SEBI within 5 working days of DPS, SEBI observations within 15 working days, dispatch of letter of offer within 7 working days of SEBI observations, tendering period of 10 working days, and payment within 10 working days of closure. The acquirer must deposit 100 % of the maximum consideration in an escrow account (cash, bank guarantee, or approved securities) before the DPS. For offers exceeding ₹1,000 crore, a minimum 1 % of the consideration must be in cash. The 2022 amendment mandated that the acquirer and PACs disclose their shareholding and voting rights on a quarterly basis to the stock exchanges and the target company, enhancing transparency during the offer period. Landmark cases such as SEBI v. Sterlite Industries (2013) clarified that indirect acquisitions through subsidiaries trigger the open offer obligation, while SEBI v. DLF Ltd (2019) reinforced that PAC determination is fact-specific and not limited to formal agreements.

Delisting of Equity Shares: Reverse Book Building, Exit Price Discovery, and Recent Amendments

The SEBI (Delisting of Equity Shares) Regulations, 2021 replaced the 2009 framework and introduced significant changes to the voluntary delisting process. A promoter or acquirer seeking to delist a company must make a public announcement after obtaining board approval and shareholder approval via a special resolution passed through postal ballot or e-voting, where votes cast by public shareholders in favour must be at least two times the votes cast against. The exit price is discovered through a reverse book building (RBB) process conducted on the stock exchange's acquisition window. The floor price is the higher of: (i) the highest negotiated price for any acquisition by the promoter/PACs in the 52 weeks preceding the board meeting; (ii) the VWAP for 60 trading days preceding the board meeting; (iii) the VWAP for 10 trading days preceding the board meeting; and (iv) for infrequently traded shares, the price determined under Regulation 15(2) of the ICDR Regulations. The RBB bidding period is 5 working days, during which public shareholders tender shares at or above the floor price. The discovered price is the price at which the promoter's holding reaches 90 % of the total issued shares (the "delisting threshold"). If the discovered price is accepted by the promoter, the delisting succeeds; if rejected, the promoter cannot make a fresh delisting attempt for 12 months. The 2022 amendment introduced a "counter-offer" mechanism: if the promoter rejects the discovered price, public shareholders holding at least 25 % of the public shareholding may make a counter-offer to acquire the promoter's shares at the discovered price within 15 working days. If the counter-offer succeeds, the promoter must sell; if it fails, the delisting attempt lapses. The amendment also mandated that the promoter deposit 100 % of the estimated consideration (based on the floor price and total public shareholding) in an escrow account before the RBB opens. For companies listed on multiple exchanges, delisting from all exchanges is required; delisting from one exchange while remaining listed on others is not permitted under the voluntary delisting framework. Compulsory delisting by stock exchanges for non-compliance with listing agreement clauses follows a separate process under the Securities Contracts (Regulation) Act, 1956, where the exchange appoints an independent valuer and provides an exit window at the determined fair value. The Vedanta Ltd delisting attempt (2020) highlighted the tension between promoter expectations and market-discovered prices, while the Hexaware Technologies delisting (2021) demonstrated the RBB mechanism's price discovery efficacy. The 2023 amendment clarified that shares held by custodians against depository receipts are excluded from the public shareholding computation for the 90 % threshold, aligning with the ICDR framework.

Examiner's Trap: Creeping Acquisition vs. Voluntary Open Offer
A listed company has a promoter holding 30 % of voting capital. In FY 2023-24, the promoter acquires 4 % through open market purchases. In FY 2024-25, the promoter acquires another 4 %. Does an open offer trigger in either year? Separately, if the promoter held 20 % and wished to acquire 10 % via a single block deal, could a voluntary open offer be made instead of a mandatory one?
In FY 2023-24, the promoter holds 30 % (between 25 %–75 %). The creeping acquisition limit is 5 % of total voting capital per financial year. A 4 % acquisition is within the limit — no open offer triggers. In FY 2024-25, the promoter now holds 34 %. Another 4 % acquisition is again within the 5 % annual limit — no open offer triggers. However, if the promoter acquired 6 % in a single year, the excess 1 % would trigger an open offer for that 1 % (or the promoter could make an open offer for 26 % under the voluntary route). For the second scenario: a promoter at 20 % acquiring 10 % would cross the 25 % threshold, triggering a mandatory open offer for at least 26 % of voting capital. A voluntary open offer under Regulation 6 is only available to acquirers holding < 25 % who wish to acquire shares that would take them to ≥ 25 %. Once the 25 % threshold is crossed, the mandatory offer provisions (Regulation 3/4) apply; the voluntary route cannot be used to circumvent the mandatory 26 % offer size. The acquirer must make an open offer for at least 26 % at the Regulation 8 price.
Exam-Day Strategy
Memorise the four pricing benchmarks for mandatory open offers (Regulation 8) and the floor price benchmarks for delisting (Regulation 15) as distinct sets — they overlap but are not identical. The creeping acquisition limit (5 % per financial year) applies only between 25 %–75 %; below 25 %, any acquisition crossing 25 % triggers a mandatory offer; above 75 %, no further acquisition is permitted without a delisting or public shareholding increase. For delisting, the 90 % promoter holding threshold is computed post-RBB acceptance; the counter-offer mechanism (2022) is a favourite examiner twist — know the 25 % public shareholding trigger and 15-day window. In numerical questions, always compute the 5 % creeping limit on the voting capital as on 1 April of the financial year, not on the current holding.

Buyback of Securities: Tender Offer vs Open Market Methods, Tax Treatment, and 2023 Regulatory Changes

The SEBI (Buy-back of Securities) Regulations, 2018 govern buybacks by listed companies. Two methods are permitted: (i) tender offer route, where the company makes a public announcement, files a draft letter of offer with SEBI, and purchases shares from shareholders on a proportionate basis; and (ii) open market route, where the company buys shares through the stock exchange order book over a maximum period of 6 months. The buyback size is capped at 25 % of the aggregate of paid-up capital and free reserves (the "25 % test"), and the debt-equity ratio post-buyback must not exceed 2:1. The maximum buyback price is fixed upfront in the tender offer route; in the open market route, the company specifies a maximum price and the buyback is executed at prevailing market prices not exceeding that maximum. The 2023 amendment (SEBI/LAD-NRO/GN/2023/142) introduced critical changes: (a) the tender offer route now mandates that at least 75 % of the buyback size be reserved for retail shareholders (holding shares up to ₹2 lakh), with the remaining 25 % for non-retail shareholders; (b) the open market route is restricted to a maximum of 15 % of the paid-up capital and free reserves (down from 25 %), effectively nudging companies toward the tender offer route for larger buybacks; (c) the buyback period for the open market route is reduced from 12 months to 6 months; (d) companies must utilise at least 50 % of the amount earmarked for buyback within the first half of the buyback period (open market) or complete the tender offer within the prescribed timeline; (e) the escrow requirement is enhanced: 100 % of the buyback consideration for offers up to ₹1,000 crore, and 100 % of ₹1,000 crore plus 25 % of the excess for offers above ₹1,000 crore. Tax treatment: under Section 115QA of the Income-tax Act, 1961, the company pays a buyback tax of 20 % (plus surcharge and cess) on the distributed income (buyback price minus issue price). This is a final tax; shareholders are exempt from capital gains tax under Section 10(34A). For shares issued before 1 July 2019, the issue price is the actual issue price; for shares issued on or after 1 July 2019, the issue price is deemed to be the fair market value as on 31 January 2018 (grandfathering clause under Section 55). The 2023 amendment also mandated that the company's board certify that the buyback will not render the company insolvent and that the audited accounts used for the 25 % test are not older than 6 months from the date of the board resolution. The TCS (2017, 2018, 2020) and Wipro (2019, 2020) buybacks via the tender offer route demonstrated the proportionate acceptance mechanism, while Infosys (2019) used the open market route. The 2023 changes aim to improve retail participation and reduce the open market route's potential for price manipulation.

Participatory Notes (P-Notes): Regulatory Evolution, Beneficial Ownership Norms, and Impact on Capital Flows

Participatory Notes (P-Notes) are offshore derivative instruments (ODIs) issued by SEBI-registered Foreign Portfolio Investors (FPIs) to overseas investors who wish to invest in Indian securities without registering directly with SEBI. The FPI acts as the legal holder of the underlying Indian securities, while the ODI subscriber holds the economic interest. The regulatory journey began with SEBI's 2004 circular permitting FIIs to issue ODIs, followed by the 2007 ban on fresh issuance (lifted in 2008 with stricter norms), the 2011 KYC and reporting requirements, and the 2017 mandate for ODI subscribers to comply with the Prevention of Money Laundering Act (PMLA) and submit beneficial ownership details. The SEBI (Foreign Portfolio Investors) Regulations, 2019 consolidated the framework: Category I FPIs (government entities, central banks, sovereign wealth funds) may issue ODIs only to subscribers who are regulated entities in their home jurisdiction; Category II FPIs (pension funds, university funds, etc.) may issue ODIs to subscribers meeting KYC norms; Category III FPIs (all others) face the strictest ODI issuance conditions. The 2020 amendment mandated that every ODI subscriber must obtain a Legal Entity Identifier (LEI) and submit a declaration of beneficial ownership as per the Companies (Significant Beneficial Owners) Rules, 2018, identifying natural persons holding ≥ 10 % (for companies) or ≥ 15 % (for partnerships/trusts) of the subscriber. The FPI issuing ODIs must report monthly to the designated depository participant (DDP) the details of each ODI: subscriber name, jurisdiction, LEI, beneficial owners, underlying securities, and notional value. SEBI also imposed a "subscription fee" of USD 1,000 per ODI subscriber per annum, payable by the FPI, to fund regulatory oversight. The 2022 circular required FPIs to conduct enhanced due diligence on ODI subscribers from high-risk jurisdictions (as per FATF lists) and to obtain a certificate from the subscriber's auditor confirming compliance with home-country AML/CFT laws. P-Notes have historically accounted for 4–6 % of total FPI assets under custody (AUC) in India, peaking at ~55 % in 2007 before declining post-2011 regulations. The ODI route remains significant for hedge funds and proprietary trading desks seeking anonymity and operational ease. However, the tightening of beneficial ownership disclosure and the introduction of the LEI requirement have increased compliance costs, contributing to a gradual shift toward direct FPI registration. The SEBI v. Cayman Islands-based ODI subscribers (2018) enforcement action, where SEBI barred several ODI subscribers for opaque ownership structures, underscored the regulator's focus on transparency. The 2023 budget proposal to tax ODI income at the FPI level (rather than the subscriber level) was not enacted, but the Finance Act, 2023 clarified that capital gains from ODI transfers are taxable in India under the India-Mauritius and India-Singapore tax treaties' limitation of benefits clauses, aligning ODI taxation with direct FPI investment.

Differential Voting Rights (DVR) and Superior Voting Rights (SVR) Shares: SEBI's Listing Framework and Corporate Governance Implications

The Companies Act, 2013 (Section 43) permits companies to issue equity shares with differential rights as to dividend, voting, or otherwise, subject to rules prescribed under the Companies (Share Capital and Debentures) Rules, 2014. SEBI's ICDR Regulations, 2018 (Regulation 29) and LODR Regulations, 2015 (Regulation 31A) provide the listing framework for DVR and SVR shares. DVR shares carry fewer voting rights per share (typically 1/10th or 1/100th) but may offer higher dividends. SVR shares carry higher voting rights per share (typically 10:1 or 20:1) and are permitted only for "new age" technology companies meeting specific criteria. For DVR shares: the issue must be authorised by the articles of association and a special resolution; the DVR shares cannot exceed 25 % of the total post-issue paid-up equity capital; the company must have distributable profits for at least three preceding years; and the DVR shares must be listed on a recognised stock exchange. The 2020 amendment to the ICDR Regulations introduced the SVR framework for "innovators" — companies in technology, data analytics, intellectual property, or biotechnology sectors, with a pre-issue market capitalisation of at least ₹1,000 crore (for IPOs) or ₹500 crore (for further issues), and where the promoters hold at least 20 % of the post-issue equity capital. SVR shares may be issued only to promoters/founders, carry voting rights up to 10:1 (or 20:1 with shareholder approval), and must be converted to ordinary shares upon transfer to non-promoters or after 5 years from listing (whichever is earlier). The conversion ratio is fixed at issuance. Both DVR and SVR shares are subject to the Minimum Public Shareholding (MPS) norm of 25 % (computed on total voting rights, not share count). The 2021 amendment to LODR Regulations mandated that DVR/SVR shareholdings be disclosed separately in the shareholding pattern, and that any reclassification of promoter DVR/SVR shares to public category requires stock exchange approval. Corporate governance concerns include: (i) entrenchment of promoter control via SVR shares, reducing accountability; (ii) misalignment of economic and voting interests in DVR structures; (iii) potential for tunnelling in DVR companies where promoters control voting but public shareholders bear economic risk. The Tata Motors DVR (issued 2008, delisted 2022) and Future Enterprises DVR (2015) are prominent DVR cases; Zomato (2021) and Nykaa (2021) explored SVR structures but ultimately listed with single-class shares due to institutional investor pressure. The 2023 SEBI consultation paper on "Dual Class Share Structures" proposed extending SVR eligibility to non-promoter key managerial personnel and reducing the sunset period from 5 years to 3 years, but no final regulation has been issued as of 2024. The framework reflects a balance between enabling founder-led innovation and protecting minority shareholders through conversion triggers, disclosure, and MPS computation on voting rights.

  • Confusing the creeping acquisition limit (5 % per financial year for 25 %–75 % holders) with the mandatory offer trigger (crossing 25 % from below) — they operate in different holding bands.
  • Assuming the delisting discovered price is binding on the promoter — the promoter may reject it, but then faces a 12-month cooling-off period and a potential counter-offer from public shareholders holding 25 % of public shareholding.
  • Treating the buyback tax (Section 115QA) as a shareholder-level capital gains tax — it is a company-level tax on distributed income; shareholders are exempt under Section 10(34A).
  • Believing P-Notes are issued by SEBI directly — they are issued by registered FPIs to overseas subscribers; SEBI regulates the FPI's ODI issuance activity.
  • Assuming SVR shares can be freely transferred — they must convert to ordinary shares upon transfer to non-promoters or after the sunset period (5 years), whichever is earlier.
  • Overlooking that the 25 % MPS norm for DVR/SVR companies is computed on total voting rights, not on the number of shares outstanding — this affects the public float calculation significantly.
  • Mixing up the tender offer buyback reservation (75 % for retail) with the open market route limit (15 % of paid-up capital and free reserves) — the 2023 amendment created distinct caps for each method.

Framework for Issuance of Social Impact Bonds and Development Impact Bonds in India

Social Impact Bonds (SIBs) and Development Impact Bonds (DIBs) are outcome‑based financing instruments where private investors fund social programmes and are repaid by an outcome payer — typically a government agency or philanthropic foundation — only if pre‑agreed impact metrics are achieved. In India, the first SIB was launched in 2015 by the Rajasthan government in partnership with Educate Girls, targeting enrolment and learning outcomes for out‑of‑school girls. The structure used a Special Purpose Vehicle (SPV) registered as a Category I Alternative Investment Fund (AIF) under SEBI (AIF) Regulations, 2012, allowing the SPV to raise capital from accredited investors while enjoying pass‑through tax treatment. DIBs differ in that the outcome payer is usually a development finance institution (e.g., the World Bank or USAID) rather than a domestic government; the 2018 “Maternal Health DIB” in Uttar Pradesh, backed by the Children’s Investment Fund Foundation, exemplifies this model.

Key regulatory touchpoints include: (i) SEBI (AIF) Regulations — the SPV must be registered as a Category I or II AIF, with a minimum corpus of ₹20 crore and a maximum of 1,000 investors; (ii) RBI’s External Commercial Borrowing (ECB) framework — if foreign capital is used, the SPV must comply with the ECB master direction, including end‑use restrictions and reporting via the AD Category‑I bank; (iii) the Contract Act, 1872 and the Specific Relief Act, 1963 — governing the outcome‑payment agreements and enforcement of performance milestones; (iv) the Companies Act, 2013 — the SPV is typically a private limited company with a board comprising representatives of investors, the service provider, and an independent director to mitigate conflict of interest.

Risk allocation is the central design challenge. Investors bear implementation risk (service delivery) and outcome risk (metric achievement). To mitigate, contracts often include: (a) a “cap” on investor returns (e.g., 12‑15 % IRR) to align incentives; (b) a “floor” guaranteeing a minimum return if outcomes are partially met; (c) independent verification by a third‑party evaluator (often a university or consultancy) using pre‑specified methodologies such as randomised controlled trials or quasi‑experimental designs. The 2021 SEBI consultation paper on “Impact Investing” suggested a dedicated “Impact Fund” category under AIF regulations with relaxed reporting, but no final rule has been notified as of 2024.

From an examiner’s perspective, the distinction between SIBs and DIBs, the role of AIF registration, and the interplay of SEBI, RBI, and contract law are frequent test points. Candidates should be able to trace the cash‑flow: investor → SPV → service provider → outcome verification → outcome payer → investor. A common twist is to ask whether SIBs qualify as “securities” under the Securities Contracts (Regulation) Act, 1956 — they do, because the SPV issues units to investors, bringing them under SEBI’s purview.

Regulatory Treatment of Perpetual Bonds and Additional Tier 1 Capital Instruments under Basel III

Perpetual bonds and Additional Tier 1 (AT1) capital instruments are loss‑absorbing, non‑cumulative, unsecured debt that count towards a bank’s Tier 1 capital under the Basel III framework as implemented by the Reserve Bank of India (RBI). The RBI’s “Master Circular – Basel III Capital Regulations” (July 2015, updated 2022) prescribes that AT1 instruments must be perpetual (no maturity), have a discretionary non‑cumulative coupon, and contain a principal write‑down or conversion trigger linked to the bank’s Common Equity Tier 1 (CET1) ratio falling below 5.5 % (or 6.125 % including the Capital Conservation Buffer). The coupon may be cancelled at the issuer’s discretion without constituting an event of default.

Key design features mandated by RBI: (i) the instrument must be issued at par; (ii) a call option is permitted only after five years and with prior RBI approval; (iii) the coupon reset mechanism must be linked to a benchmark (e.g., 10‑year G‑sec yield plus a spread) and cannot be step‑up; (iv) the write‑down/conversion trigger must be automatic and irreversible; (v) the instrument must be subordinated to all other creditors except equity holders. For NBFCs classified as “Upper Layer” under the Scale‑Based Regulation (SBR), RBI’s 2022 “NBFC‑Liquidity Risk Management” circular allows perpetual debt to be counted as Tier 1 capital subject to identical loss‑absorption clauses, but the total perpetual debt cannot exceed 15 % of the NBFC’s Tier 1 capital.

Tax treatment: interest on AT1 bonds is deductible for the issuer under Section 36(1)(iii) of the Income‑Tax Act, 1961, while investors treat coupon receipts as interest income taxable at slab rates. However, if the bond is written down, the loss is treated as a capital loss for the investor. The 2020 Finance Act clarified that write‑downs triggered by regulatory action are not “transfer” under Section 2(47), avoiding capital gains complications.

Market practice: Since 2016, major public sector banks (SBI, PNB, Bank of Baroda) have issued AT1 bonds aggregating over ₹1.2 lakh crore. The 2022 “Yes Bank” AT1 write‑down (₹8,415 crore) after the RBI‑mandated reconstruction highlighted the real‑world activation of the CET1 trigger. For examiners, the distinction between AT1 and Tier 2 instruments (which have a minimum five‑year maturity and no CET1 trigger) is a staple. A frequent trap is to assume that AT1 coupons are mandatory — they are fully discretionary, and non‑payment does not constitute default.

Technique: Mapping Regulatory Triggers to Cash‑Flow Outcomes
A bank issues an AT1 perpetual bond with a CET1 trigger at 5.5 %. In Year 3 the bank’s CET1 falls to 5.2 % due to loan losses. The bond’s terms provide for a 50 % principal write‑down and conversion of the remaining 50 % into equity at a fixed conversion price. Explain the sequence of events from trigger breach to investor outcome, citing the relevant RBI circular provisions.
1. Breach detection: The bank’s quarterly CET1 computation (per RBI Master Circular, Annex 4) shows 5.2 % < 5.5 %. 2. Automatic trigger: The bond’s indenture, drafted per RBI’s “loss‑absorption clause” (Para 5.2.3 of the 2022 Master Circular), activates without board discretion. 3. Write‑down: 50 % of the outstanding principal is permanently cancelled; the bank’s CET1 rises by the written‑down amount (since the liability disappears). 4. Conversion: The remaining 50 % is converted into common equity at the pre‑agreed conversion price (usually the market price at issuance). The bank issues new shares to bondholders, increasing CET1 further. 5. Investor outcome: Bondholders receive equity shares; coupon payments cease on the written‑down portion and continue discretionary on the converted equity (dividends). 6. Reporting: The bank discloses the trigger event, write‑down amount, and conversion ratio in its next quarterly financial statements and to RBI under the “Capital Adequacy” reporting format (Form C‑1). This sequence demonstrates the instrument’s going‑concern loss‑absorption capacity, a core Basel III objective.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a question asks to “distinguish” AT1 from Tier 2, structure the answer in a three‑column table: Feature | AT1 (Perpetual) | Tier 2 (Dated). Fill rows for maturity, coupon discretion, trigger, subordination, and capital classification. This visual format scores full marks for clarity and completeness.

SEBI's Framework for Warrants, Convertible Debentures and Preferential Allotments: Pricing, Lock‑in and Disclosure

The SEBI (Issue of Capital and Disclosure Requirements) Regulations, 2018 (ICDR) govern the issuance of warrants, fully/partially convertible debentures (FCDs/PCDs), and preferential allotments by listed companies. Regulation 164 prescribes the pricing floor: the higher of (a) the 90‑trading‑day volume‑weighted average price (VWAP) preceding the relevant date, or (b) the 10‑trading‑day VWAP preceding the relevant date. The “relevant date” is the date thirty days prior to the shareholders’ meeting for preferential issues, and the date of board approval for warrant/FCD issues. This dual‑VWAP floor prevents promoter‑driven under‑pricing.

Lock‑in requirements differ by instrument and allottee category. For preferential allotments to promoters/promoter group: 3 years from the date of trading approval (Regulation 167). For non‑promoter allottees (including QIBs, mutual funds, and strategic investors): 1 year. Warrants and convertible debentures carry a lock‑in on the underlying equity shares upon conversion: 1 year for non‑promoters, 3 years for promoters, calculated from the date of allotment of the resultant shares. The 2022 amendment introduced a “cool‑off” period of six months for any subsequent preferential issue by the same company, curbing serial dilution.

Disclosure obligations are extensive. The issuer must file a “Preferential Issue Document” with the stock exchanges containing: (i) the object of the issue; (ii) the identity of the proposed allottees and their pre‑ and post‑issue shareholding; (iii) a valuation report from a registered valuer (for unlisted companies) or a merchant banker’s fairness opinion (for listed companies); (iv) the pricing methodology and the VWAP calculations; (v) the lock‑in details and the undertaking that the allottees will not sell during the lock‑in. The 2023 SEBI circular (SEBI/HO/CFD/DIL2/CIR/P/2023/12) mandated that the valuation report be uploaded on the company’s website and the stock exchange’s portal at least five working days before the shareholders’ meeting.

Conversion mechanics: Warrants typically have an exercise period of 18 months from allotment, extendable by another 18 months with shareholder approval. The conversion price is fixed at issuance (the warrant exercise price) and cannot be reset. For FCDs/PCDs, the conversion ratio is defined in the debenture trust deed; the conversion price must be at least the ICDR floor price on the date of conversion. The 2021 “Zomato” preferential issue to Ant Financial illustrated the interplay of pricing floor, lock‑in, and the requirement for a special resolution (75 % majority) when the issue exceeds 5 % of the post‑issue capital.

Examiner twists often involve: (a) confusing the 90‑day/10‑day VWAP floor with the “average of weekly high‑low” method used for open offers; (b) assuming that lock‑in applies to the warrant instrument itself rather than the resultant shares; (c) overlooking that the pricing floor for conversion of FCDs is re‑computed on the conversion date, not the issuance date. A precise answer cites Regulation 164, 167, and the 2022/2023 amendments.

Commodity Derivatives Market Regulation: SEBI Oversight, Position Limits and Warehouse Receipt Financing

Following the merger of the Forward Markets Commission (FMC) into SEBI in September 2015, the SEBI (Commodity Derivatives) Regulations, 2018 provide the unified regulatory framework for commodity futures and options in India. The regulations cover agricultural commodities (e.g., chana, soybean, cotton), non‑agricultural commodities (gold, silver, crude oil, base metals), and indices. SEBI designates recognised commodity exchanges (MCX, NCDEX, ICEX, BSE, NSE) as “Commodity Derivatives Exchanges” and mandates a net‑worth requirement of ₹100 crore for clearing members.

Position limits are three‑tiered: (i) Client‑level limit — 5 % of open interest or ₹500 crore, whichever is lower, for agricultural contracts; 10 % or ₹1,000 crore for non‑agricultural contracts. (ii) Member‑level limit — 15 % of open interest or ₹1,500 crore (agri) / ₹3,000 crore (non‑agri). (iii) Market‑wide limit — 25 % of open interest for any single contract, monitored daily by the exchange’s surveillance system. The 2020 SEBI circular (SEBI/HO/CDMRD/DNPMP/CIR/P/2020/125) introduced “position limit relaxation” for hedgers who submit a verified hedging declaration, allowing up to 20 % of open interest subject to audit.

Warehouse receipt financing is enabled through the Warehousing Development and Regulatory Authority (WDRA) electronic Negotiable Warehouse Receipts (eNWRs). SEBI’s 2019 “Framework for Warehouse Receipt Financing” permits banks and NBFCs to lend against eNWRs issued by WDRA‑accredited warehouses. The eNWR is a dematerialised, transferable title document; the lender registers a pledge on the eNWR in the WDRA repository. The 2021 amendment allowed “partial pledge” of a single eNWR, enhancing liquidity for farmers and traders. The interest rate on such loans is typically linked to the MCLR plus a spread of 1.5‑2.5 %.

Surveillance and enforcement: SEBI operates the “Commodity Market Surveillance System” (CMSS) which integrates trade data from all exchanges in real time. The 2022 “Market Abuse” circular extended the insider trading prohibition (under SEBI (Prohibition of Insider Trading) Regulations, 2015) to commodity derivatives, defining “unpublished price sensitive information” (UPSI) to include crop‑size estimates, government procurement decisions, and import/export policy changes. Penalties for violation can reach ₹25 crore or three times the profit made, whichever is higher.

For UPSC aspirants, the key differentiators from equity derivatives are: (a) the dual regulator history (FMC → SEBI) and the continuing role of WDRA for physical delivery infrastructure; (b) the hedger‑specific position limit relaxation; (c) the mandatory physical delivery option for most agricultural contracts (cash‑settled only for a few indices). A typical question asks to “explain the role of eNWR in reducing post‑harvest distress sales” — the answer should trace the chain: farmer deposits produce → receives eNWR → pledges eNWR to bank → obtains loan at harvest‑time prices → sells later at higher prices.

Sovereign Gold Bonds, Gold Monetisation Scheme and Gold ETFs: Design, Taxation and Investor Participation

The Government of India, through the Reserve Bank of India, launched the Sovereign Gold Bond (SGB) scheme in November 2015 to provide a financial alternative to physical gold. SGBs are government securities denominated in grams of gold, with a tenor of eight years and an early‑exit option after the fifth year on interest payment dates. The issue price is fixed at the simple average of the closing price of 999‑purity gold published by the India Bullion and Jewellers Association (IBJA) for the last three business days preceding the subscription period. The coupon is 2.5 % per annum on the nominal value, paid semi‑annually. Capital gains on redemption at maturity are exempt from tax under Section 47(vii) of the Income‑Tax Act, 1961; however, interest income is taxable at the investor’s slab rate. TDS is not deducted on interest.

The Gold Monetisation Scheme (GMS), also launched in 2015, allows resident individuals, HUFs, trusts, and companies to deposit gold (minimum 30 grams, 995‑purity) with designated banks. Deposits can be short‑term (1‑3 years), medium‑term (5‑7 years), or long‑term (12‑15 years). The bank pays interest in gold (for short‑term) or in rupees (for medium/long‑term) at rates notified by the RBI (e.g., 0.5‑1 % p.a. in gold for short‑term). On maturity, the depositor can receive either gold or the rupee equivalent. Interest earned is exempt from capital gains tax but taxable as “income from other sources” at slab rates.

Gold Exchange Traded Funds (Gold ETFs) are open‑ended mutual fund schemes listed on stock exchanges, each unit representing 1 gram of gold (or 0.5 gram for some funds). They are regulated under SEBI (Mutual Funds) Regulations, 1996. The expense ratio is capped at 1 % of daily net assets. Gold ETFs track the domestic spot price of gold (LBMA AM fixing converted to INR) and provide intraday liquidity. Taxation: long‑term capital gains (holding >36 months) are taxed at 20 % with indexation; short‑term gains are taxed at slab rates. No securities transaction tax (STT) applies on purchase/sale of ETF units on the exchange.

Investor participation trends: As of March 2024, cumulative SGB issuances exceed ₹45,000 crore (≈ 1,200 tonnes), with retail investors accounting for ~60 % of subscriptions. GMS deposits have remained modest (~ 30 tonnes) due to low interest rates and the preference for physical possession. Gold ETF assets under management crossed ₹20,000 crore in 2023, driven by institutional allocation to gold as a portfolio diversifier. The 2022 Budget clarified that SGBs held in demat form are eligible for “margin pledge” with clearing corporations, enhancing their utility as collateral.

Examination focus areas: (i) comparative tax treatment — SGB maturity exemption vs. Gold ETF LTCG with indexation vs. GMS interest taxability; (ii) the role of IBJA pricing in SGB issue price; (iii) the distinction between “gold‑denominated” interest (GMS short‑term) and “rupee‑denominated” interest (SGB, GMS medium/long‑term); (iv) the eligibility of SGBs for statutory liquidity ratio (SLR) purposes — they are classified as “Government Securities” and count towards SLR for banks. A common trap is to state that SGB interest is tax‑free; only the capital gain on redemption is exempt.

  • Confusing the 90‑day/10‑day VWAP floor for preferential issues with the 60‑day/10‑day VWAP used for open offers under the Takeover Regulations.
  • Assuming AT1 perpetual bonds have a mandatory coupon — the coupon is fully discretionary and non‑payment is not an event of default.
  • Treating the lock‑in on warrants as applying to the warrant instrument itself rather than the equity shares arising on conversion.
  • Believing that commodity derivatives position limits are uniform across agricultural and non‑agricultural contracts — they differ by a factor of two.
  • Stating that Sovereign Gold Bond interest is exempt from tax — only the capital gain on redemption is exempt; interest is taxable at slab rates.
  • Overlooking that the Gold Monetisation Scheme short‑term deposits earn interest in gold, not rupees, affecting tax characterisation.
  • Mixing up the conversion price reset rules for FCDs (re‑computed on conversion date) with the fixed exercise price of warrants.
The advanced sections above integrate regulatory mechanics, market practice, and examiner‑focused nuances to equip aspirants for high‑difficulty questions on financial market instruments and their governance.

External Commercial Borrowings Framework: Track Classification, End‑Use Restrictions and Hedging Obligations

The Reserve Bank of India’s External Commercial Borrowings (ECB) framework, consolidated under the Foreign Exchange Management (Borrowing and Lending) Regulations, 2018 and the Master Direction dated 26 March 2019 (updated periodically), classifies ECBs into three tracks based on tenor, end‑use and borrower eligibility. Track I comprises medium‑term foreign currency denominated borrowings with a minimum average maturity of three years (five years for manufacturing companies) and a maximum all‑in cost ceiling of 450 basis points over the 6‑month SOFR/LIBOR/ARR benchmark. Permitted end‑uses include capital expenditure, working capital (subject to a minimum average maturity of ten years), and repayment of rupee loans availed domestically for capital expenditure. Track II covers long‑term foreign currency borrowings with a minimum average maturity of ten years, primarily for infrastructure and green projects, with an all‑in cost ceiling of 350 basis points. Track III consists of rupee‑denominated borrowings (masala bonds) with a minimum maturity of three years and no end‑use restrictions beyond the negative list (real estate, capital market speculation, working capital for general corporate purposes, and on‑lending to entities for prohibited activities).

All ECBs above USD 20 million equivalent require mandatory hedging of 70 % of the principal and coupon exposure for Track I and Track II borrowings, with the hedge tenor matching the residual maturity of the ECB. The hedging can be undertaken through permitted derivative instruments (forwards, options, swaps) with authorised dealer Category‑I banks. Borrowers must report the hedge position quarterly in the ECB‑2 return. The framework also prescribes a prudential limit: the outstanding ECB liability of a corporate cannot exceed 6.5 times its net owned funds (NOF) for Track I and 7.5 times for Track II, subject to an overall cap of USD 750 million or equivalent per financial year under the automatic route. Borrowings beyond this limit or for end‑uses not permitted under the automatic route require prior RBI approval. The negative list explicitly prohibits utilisation for real estate activities, investment in capital markets, equity investment, working capital (except as specified), general corporate purposes (except for Track III), repayment of rupee loans (except for Track I), and on‑lending to entities for any of the above prohibited activities. The 2022 amendment introduced a “green ECB” window under Track II with a reduced all‑in cost ceiling of 250 basis points for projects certified under the RBI’s green taxonomy, provided the proceeds are exclusively deployed in eligible green categories (renewable energy, energy efficiency, pollution prevention, sustainable agriculture, green buildings, clean transportation).

Conversion of ECB into equity is permitted under the automatic route subject to sectoral FDI caps, pricing guidelines (fair valuation by a SEBI‑registered merchant banker or chartered accountant), and reporting in the ECB‑2 return within seven working days of conversion. The conversion price for listed companies cannot be lower than the higher of the 90‑day or 10‑day volume weighted average price (VWAP) preceding the board resolution; for unlisted companies, fair valuation applies. Prepayment of ECB is allowed without prior RBI approval provided the minimum average maturity has been complied with, and the prepayment is reported in the ECB‑2 return. The framework also permits partial conversion and prepayment. A critical compliance nuance: the all‑in cost includes arranger fees, upfront fees, commitment fees, and any other charges payable to the lender, but excludes withholding tax payable in India. Breach of end‑use, hedging, or reporting requirements attracts penal action under FEMA, including compounding proceedings and potential restriction on future ECB access.

Takeover Code Mechanics: Open Offer Triggers, Creeping Acquisition and Competing Offer Dynamics

The SEBI (Substantial Acquisition of Shares and Takeovers) Regulations, 2011 (Takeover Regulations) govern the acquisition of shares and voting rights in listed companies, mandating an open offer when specific thresholds are breached. The primary trigger under Regulation 3(1) is the acquisition of shares or voting rights entitling the acquirer to exercise 25 % or more of the voting rights in the target company. A secondary trigger under Regulation 3(2) applies when an acquirer, already holding 25 % or more but less than the maximum permissible non‑public shareholding (75 % for most companies, 90 % for PSUs), acquires additional shares or voting rights exceeding 5 % in a financial year (the “creeping acquisition” limit). The creeping limit is computed on a financial year basis (April–March) and resets annually; acquisitions through open market purchases, preferential allotments, rights issues, and conversion of convertible securities all count towards the 5 % threshold. An open offer must be for at least 26 % of the total voting capital of the target, at a price not lower than the highest of: (a) the highest negotiated price per share for any acquisition under the agreement triggering the offer; (b) the volume weighted average price (VWAP) for 60 trading days preceding the public announcement (PA); (c) the highest price paid by the acquirer or persons acting in concert (PACs) during the 52 weeks preceding the PA; (d) the VWAP for 10 trading days preceding the PA (for frequently traded shares) or fair value determined by a merchant banker (for infrequently traded shares).

The open offer process follows a strict timeline: the public announcement (PA) must be made within one working day of the agreement or decision to acquire; the detailed public statement (DPS) within five working days of the PA; the draft letter of offer (DLOF) filed with SEBI within five working days of the DPS; SEBI observations issued within 15 working days; the letter of offer (LOF) dispatched to shareholders within seven working days of receiving SEBI observations; the tendering period opens within 12 working days of the LOF dispatch and remains open for 10 working days; and payment to shareholders who tendered must be completed within 10 working days of the tendering period closure. The acquirer must deposit 100 % of the maximum consideration payable under the open offer in an escrow account (cash, bank guarantee, or approved securities) before the PA. For offers exceeding ₹500 crore, at least 25 % must be in cash. Competing offers can be announced within 15 working days of the DPS of the first offer, subject to a minimum offer price 5 % higher than the first offer price. The competing offer follows a compressed timeline: DPS within five working days, DLOF within five working days, and the tendering period aligns with the first offer’s closure date. If a competing offer succeeds, the first acquirer’s escrow is released only after the competing acquirer fulfils payment obligations. The “put option” for residual shareholders: if post‑offer public shareholding falls below 10 %, the acquirer must provide an exit window at the offer price for one year. A landmark interpretation in SEBI v. Kishore Biyani (2022) clarified that indirect acquisition through a scheme of arrangement triggering change in control attracts the open offer obligation even if no fresh shares are acquired, reinforcing the “control” test over the “share acquisition” test.

Examiner's trap
An acquirer holds 24 % in a listed company. On 15 April 2024, it acquires 2 % via open market. On 10 May 2024, it acquires another 2 % via preferential allotment. On 20 June 2024, it acquires 1.5 % via conversion of warrants. Does an open offer trigger? Calculate the creeping acquisition limit applicability.
No open offer triggers. The acquirer crosses 25 % only after the 20 June acquisition (24 + 2 + 2 + 1.5 = 29.5 %). The first trigger is Regulation 3(1) at 25 %, which occurs on 20 June. The creeping limit under Regulation 3(2) applies only when the acquirer already holds ≥25 % at the start of the financial year. Since the acquirer held 24 % on 1 April 2024, the 5 % creeping limit does not apply for FY 2024‑25. The open offer obligation arises solely under Regulation 3(1) upon crossing 25 % on 20 June, requiring an offer for 26 % at the highest of the prescribed pricing parameters. A common error is to aggregate all acquisitions in the financial year and apply the 5 % creeping limit; the creeping limit is relevant only if the pre‑acquisition holding was already ≥25 %.
Exam‑day strategy
Memorise the two distinct triggers (25 % absolute and 5 % creeping) and their pricing formulas (60‑day/10‑day VWAP for open offer vs. 90‑day/10‑day VWAP for preferential issue). In scenario questions, first identify the pre‑acquisition holding on 1 April of the financial year to determine which regulation applies. Always check whether the acquirer is a PAC — concert party aggregation can trigger the offer earlier than individual holdings suggest.

Buyback of Securities and Delisting Framework: SEBI Regulations, Process and Recent Amendments

The SEBI (Buy‑Back of Securities) Regulations, 2018 (Buyback Regulations) and the SEBI (Delisting of Equity Shares) Regulations, 2021 (Delisting Regulations) provide the statutory architecture for capital reduction and exit mechanisms. A buyback can be undertaken through a tender offer (minimum 15 % of paid‑up capital and free reserves) or open market purchases (maximum 15 % of paid‑up capital and free reserves) via stock exchanges. The buyback price cannot exceed 25 % above the volume weighted average market price (VWAP) for the 2 weeks preceding the board resolution for tender offers, and for open market buybacks, the daily purchase price cannot exceed the highest of the VWAP for the 2 weeks preceding the purchase date or the last traded price. The company must utilise at least 50 % of the amount earmarked for buyback; the escrow requirement is 25 % of the buyback consideration up to ₹100 crore and 10 % thereafter. The buyback must be completed within 12 months (tender) or 6 months (open market) from the board resolution. Post‑buyback, the debt‑equity ratio cannot exceed 2:1 (except for NBFCs and housing finance companies where sectoral regulators prescribe norms). The 2022 amendment introduced a “buyback through stock exchange” mechanism for listed companies where the buyback is executed solely through the order matching system, with a minimum 15 % utilisation of the earmarked amount and a maximum 15 % of paid‑up capital and free reserves, aligning the utilisation threshold with the tender route.

Delisting under the 2021 Regulations follows a reverse book building (RBB) process for voluntary delisting. The acquirer (promoter/promoter group) must make a public announcement (PA) after board and shareholder approval (special resolution with 2/3 majority of public shareholders voting in favour). The floor price is the higher of: (a) the highest price paid by the acquirer/PACs in the 52 weeks preceding the PA; (b) the VWAP for 90 trading days preceding the PA; (c) the VWAP for 10 trading days preceding the PA. The RBB bidding period is 5 working days. The discovered price is the price at which the maximum number of shares are tendered. The acquirer can accept the discovered price or make a counter‑offer at a higher price. If the post‑offer promoter shareholding reaches 90 % (or 75 % for PSUs), delisting succeeds. The acquirer must provide an exit window for remaining shareholders for one year at the discovered/counter‑offer price. Compulsory delisting by stock exchanges for non‑compliance follows a separate track: the exchange appoints an independent valuer, and the promoter must buy out public shareholders at the fair value determined. The 2023 amendment to the Delisting Regulations introduced a “fixed price delisting” option for companies where the promoter holds ≥90 % (or ≥75 % for PSUs) and the public shareholding is ≤10 % (or ≤25 % for PSUs), bypassing RBB. The fixed price is the higher of the 90‑day VWAP or the 10‑day VWAP preceding the PA, with a mandatory 25 % premium. This route requires a special resolution and SEBI approval but eliminates price discovery uncertainty.

A critical interplay exists between buyback and delisting: a company cannot announce a buyback and a delisting offer simultaneously. SEBI’s informal guidance clarifies that if a buyback is underway, a delisting PA can be made only after the buyback completion or withdrawal. Conversely, if a delisting offer fails, a buyback can be announced after 6 months. The tax treatment differs: buyback proceeds are taxed as dividend income in the hands of shareholders (Section 115QA, 20 % buyback tax on distributed income for the company, abolished for listed companies from FY 2020‑21; shareholders pay capital gains on the difference between buyback price and cost of acquisition). Delisting proceeds are taxed as capital gains (long‑term if held >12 months, 10 % above ₹1 lakh without indexation; short‑term at slab rates). The 2024 Budget proposed aligning buyback taxation with dividend taxation for unlisted companies, but listed company buybacks remain subject to capital gains in the hands of shareholders.

Portfolio Management Services Regulation: SEBI PMS Regulations 2020, Discretionary vs Non‑Discretionary and AIF‑PMS Arbitrage

The SEBI (Portfolio Managers) Regulations, 2020 (PMS Regulations) replaced the 1993 framework, raising the entry barrier and aligning disclosure, governance and investor protection standards with the AIF regime. A portfolio manager (PM) must have a net worth of ₹5 crore (up from ₹2 crore), a principal officer with a professional qualification (CFA, CA, CS, or MBA Finance) and 10 years’ experience, and at least two key personnel with 5 years’ experience. The minimum investment per client is ₹50 lakh (up from ₹25 lakh), and the PM cannot accept funds from more than 1,000 clients across all strategies. Discretionary PMS (DPMS) grants the PM full investment authority; non‑discretionary PMS (NDPMS) requires client approval for each transaction; advisory PMS (APMS) provides only recommendations. The Regulations mandate a written agreement (PMS Agreement) specifying the investment approach, risk factors, fee structure (management fee capped at 2.5 % p.a. of AUM for DPMS, 1.5 % for NDPMS/APMS; performance fee permitted only for DPMS with a high‑water mark and hurdle rate), valuation methodology (daily NAV for DPMS, weekly for NDPMS/APMS), and reporting frequency (monthly portfolio statement, quarterly performance report benchmarked against a SEBI‑approved index). The PM must maintain a client‑level segregation of assets with a custodian (mandatory for AUM > ₹500 crore) and cannot pool client funds.

The “AIF‑PMS arbitrage” arises because Category III AIFs (hedge funds) and DPMS both employ leverage, short selling, and derivative strategies, but AIFs face a higher minimum investment (₹1 crore), a 100‑investor cap, and mandatory 3‑year lock‑in for close‑ended funds, while DPMS offers daily/weekly liquidity, no lock‑in, and a lower ticket size (₹50 lakh). SEBI’s 2022 circular on “large value orders” requires DPMS managers to disclose order‑level details (ISIN, quantity, price, timestamp) to the custodian for audit trail, addressing concerns about front‑running. The Regulations also prohibit PMs from investing client funds in unlisted equity (except up to 25 % of AUM in unlisted securities rated investment grade or guaranteed by a sovereign), commodities, or real estate. The 2023 amendment introduced a “model PMS agreement” to standardise clauses on termination, force majeure, and dispute resolution (arbitration at Mumbai). A key compliance nuance: the PM’s own proprietary trades must be executed through a separate broker account, and the PM must maintain a “Chinese wall” between the proprietary desk and the PMS desk, with a compliance officer certifying quarterly that no conflicted trades occurred. The Regulations also require the PM to appoint a compliance officer (not the principal officer) and conduct an annual internal audit by a practicing CA/CS, with the report submitted to SEBI within 3 months of the financial year end.

Co‑Lending Model and Bank‑NBFC Partnership Framework: RBI Guidelines, Risk Sharing and Operational Mechanics

The Reserve Bank of India’s Co‑Lending Model (CLM), introduced via the circular dated 5 November 2020 (updated 24 February 2022), enables banks and NBFCs (including HFCs) to jointly extend credit to priority sector borrowers, leveraging the NBFC’s origination reach and the bank’s low‑cost funds. Under CLM, the bank and NBFC enter into a master agreement specifying the risk‑sharing ratio (minimum 20:80 bank:NBFC for priority sector loans; the NBFC must retain at least 20 % of the credit risk on its books throughout the loan tenor). The loan is originated, processed, and serviced by the NBFC, while the bank contributes its share of the disbursement directly to the borrower’s account (or through the NBFC’s escrow account). The interest rate charged to the borrower is a blended rate: the bank’s cost of funds plus a spread, and the NBFC’s cost of funds plus a spread, weighted by their respective shares. The NBFC acts as the single point of contact for the borrower for all servicing, recovery, and grievance redressal. The bank’s exposure is treated as a direct exposure to the borrower for prudential norms (asset classification, provisioning, large exposure limits), while the NBFC’s exposure follows its own regulatory framework (IRACP for NBFCs under Scale‑Based Regulation).

The CLM mandates that the NBFC’s board approves a co‑lending policy covering borrower eligibility, credit appraisal standards, risk‑sharing methodology, and escalation mechanisms. The bank must conduct independent due diligence on a sample basis (at least 10 % of the portfolio quarterly) and retain the right to reject any loan proposal. The master agreement must include a “first loss” clause: the NBFC absorbs the first 20 % of losses (or a higher percentage as agreed) before the bank’s share is impacted. This aligns incentives and ensures skin‑in‑the‑game. The 2022 update permitted co‑lending for non‑priority sector loans (subject to a minimum 20:80 risk share) and allowed multiple banks to co‑lend with a single NBFC under a common master agreement (multi‑lender CLM). The framework also clarified that the NBFC can assign its share of the loan to another NBFC or securitise it, provided the bank’s consent is obtained and the risk‑sharing ratio is maintained. For priority sector classification, the loan qualifies as priority sector for the bank only if the end‑use meets the PSL categories (agriculture, MSME, housing, education, etc.) and the NBFC’s share is originated by an NBFC‑MFI or an NBFC‑UL (upper layer) meeting the “last mile” criteria. The bank’s share of the loan is risk‑weighted at 20 % (for PSL) or 100 % (non‑PSL) under Basel III, while the NBFC’s share attracts risk weights per its own capital adequacy framework. A critical operational requirement: the tripartite agreement (borrower‑bank‑NBFC) must be executed at origination, and the borrower’s consent for data sharing between the bank and NBFC must be obtained under the Account Aggregator framework. The RBI’s 2023 supervisory letter highlighted weaknesses in credit appraisal outsourcing, requiring banks to validate the NBFC’s credit scoring models annually and to monitor early warning signals (EWS) at the portfolio level.

  • Confusing the ECB all‑in cost ceiling (450 bps for Track I, 350 bps for Track II) with the spread over the benchmark rate — the ceiling includes all fees except withholding tax.
  • Assuming the creeping acquisition limit of 5 % applies from the first share acquired — it applies only when the acquirer already holds ≥25 % at the start of the financial year.
  • Stating that the buyback price ceiling is 25 % above the 60‑day VWAP — it is 25 % above the 2‑week VWAP for tender offers and the higher of 2‑week VWAP or last traded price for open market buybacks.
  • Believing that delisting floor price uses the 60‑day/10‑day VWAP — it uses the 90‑day/10‑day VWAP and the highest price paid in 52 weeks.
  • Treating PMS and AIF Category III as interchangeable for leverage and liquidity — PMS has a ₹50 lakh ticket, no lock‑in, and daily NAV; AIF Category III has a ₹1 crore ticket, 100‑investor cap, and typically a 3‑year lock‑in for close‑ended funds.
  • Assuming the bank’s share in co‑lending is always 20 % — the minimum is 20 % for priority sector; for non‑priority sector the ratio is freely negotiated but the NBFC must retain at least 20 % risk share.
  • Overlooking that the NBFC must absorb the first 20 % of losses in CLM — this “first loss” clause is mandatory and distinct from the 20 % risk retention requirement.

SEBI's Regulatory Framework for Proxy Advisors: Registration, Code of Conduct, and Conflict Management

The Securities and Exchange Board of India (Proxy Advisors) Regulations, 2014, notified on 28 August 2014, brought proxy advisory firms under a mandatory registration regime, making India one of the few jurisdictions with a dedicated statutory framework for this activity. A proxy advisor is defined as any person who provides advice to institutional investors or shareholders on the exercise of voting rights in a company, including recommendations on corporate governance matters, board appointments, executive compensation, and related-party transactions. The regulations require a minimum net worth of ₹50 lakh, a professional team with at least two persons having relevant experience of five years, and infrastructure capable of independent research. The registration is valid for five years and renewable. The code of conduct mandates a "comply or explain" approach: proxy advisors must disclose their voting recommendation policies, methodologies, and conflict-of-interest policies on their websites. They are required to maintain an arm's-length relationship between research and business development teams, and any material conflict — such as providing consulting services to the subject company — must be disclosed alongside the voting recommendation. The 2019 amendments, effective 1 February 2020, introduced stricter disclosure norms: proxy advisors must now share their draft reports with the subject company at least 24 hours before finalising recommendations, allowing the company to flag factual inaccuracies. The advisor must consider such feedback and disclose whether it was incorporated. A grievance redressal mechanism is mandatory, with a designated compliance officer and a monthly reporting obligation to SEBI on complaints received and resolved. The regulations also require proxy advisors to retain records for a minimum of five years.
