In 2022, SEBI issued a circular standardising the format for voting recommendation disclosures, requiring a tabular presentation of each agenda item, the recommendation (for/against/abstain), the rationale, and the specific policy provision invoked. This was driven by institutional investors' demand for comparability across advisors. The framework also addresses the "robo-voting" risk: institutional investors are expected to exercise independent judgment and not blindly follow proxy advice, a principle reinforced in SEBI's 2021 stewardship code circular for mutual funds and AIFs. Enforcement actions have been limited but instructive: in 2021, SEBI issued a warning to a proxy advisor for delayed disclosure of a conflict arising from its parent company's investment banking relationship with the subject company. The regulatory philosophy balances the need for independent governance research with accountability, recognising that proxy advisors act as force multipliers for minority shareholders in a concentrated ownership market.

Electronic Book Mechanism for Private Placement of Debt Securities: SEBI's 2022 Framework and Market Impact

SEBI's circular dated 13 January 2022 (updated 28 March 2022) mandated the use of an Electronic Book Provider (EBP) platform for all private placements of debt securities and non-convertible redeemable preference shares by listed entities, effective 1 July 2022. The framework covers two EBPs — NSE's EBP platform and BSE's BOND platform — which operate as electronic order books for price discovery and allocation. The mechanism applies to issuances of ₹100 crore or more per tranche, with a minimum ticket size of ₹10 lakh per investor. The issuer appoints a merchant banker as the "book runner" who manages the issue on the EBP. The process begins with the issuer announcing the issue schedule, base issue size, green shoe option (up to 100% of base size), and the coupon/rate range (for fixed rate) or spread range (for floating rate). The bidding window remains open for a minimum of one hour, during which investors (QIBs, corporates, HUFs, trusts, etc.) enter bids specifying quantity and rate/spread. The platform supports both uniform price (all allottees get the same cut-off rate) and multiple price (pay-your-bid) methods, chosen by the issuer upfront. Post bidding, the book runner finalises the cut-off rate and allocation in consultation with the issuer. Allocation priority follows: (1) investors bidding at rates tighter than cut-off, (2) investors at cut-off on pro-rata basis, (3) investors at wider rates if undersubscribed. The EBP generates a deal sheet capturing all bids, allocations, and the final cut-off, which is submitted to the stock exchange and debenture trustee. The 2022 framework also introduced a "mandatory bidding by QIBs" rule: at least 75% of the issue size must be subscribed by QIBs, failing which the issue is deemed withdrawn. This replaced the earlier voluntary EBP framework (2018) which saw low adoption. The impact has been significant: by March 2024, over 85% of eligible private placements by listed corporates used the EBP route, up from 40% in 2021. The mechanism has compressed issuance timelines from 7-10 days to 2-3 days, improved price transparency (cut-off rates are disseminated publicly), and reduced information asymmetry between large and small investors. However, challenges remain: the ₹100 crore threshold excludes mid-sized issuances; the mandatory QIB subscription rule has led to "clubbed" deals where issuers aggregate tranches; and the lack of a secondary market reporting obligation on the EBP limits post-trade transparency. SEBI's 2023 consultation paper proposed extending EBP to unlisted issuers (via a separate platform) and introducing a "when-issued" trading window for allocated but unlisted securities.

Examiner's Trap: EBP vs Public Issue vs QIP
A question asks: "Which of the following statements about SEBI's Electronic Book Provider (EBP) framework for private placement of debt securities is INCORRECT? (a) The framework is mandatory for listed issuers for tranches of ₹100 crore or more. (b) Bidding is open to retail individual investors. (c) The book runner finalises allocation in consultation with the issuer. (d) At least 75% of the issue must be subscribed by Qualified Institutional Buyers."
The incorrect statement is (b). The EBP framework for private placement of debt securities is restricted to Qualified Institutional Buyers (QIBs), corporates, HUFs, trusts, and other non-retail categories as specified by SEBI. Retail Individual Investors (RIIs) as defined in ICDR Regulations (application value ≤ ₹2 lakh) are not permitted to bid on the EBP platform for private placements. The framework is designed for wholesale debt markets. Statement (a) is correct: mandatory for listed entities, ₹100 crore per tranche threshold. Statement (c) is correct: the merchant banker acting as book runner finalises cut-off and allocation with the issuer. Statement (d) is correct: the 75% minimum QIB subscription rule was introduced in the 2022 framework to ensure institutional participation and price discovery quality.
Exam-Day Strategy
When a question compares EBP, public issue of NCDs, and Qualified Institutions Placement (QIP), focus on three differentiators: (1) investor universe — EBP excludes retail, public issue includes retail, QIP is QIB-only; (2) pricing mechanism — EBP uses electronic bidding with real-time price discovery, public issue uses fixed price or book building with prospectus, QIP uses floor price formula (average of weekly high/low); (3) regulatory trigger — EBP mandatory above ₹100 cr for listed issuers, public issue voluntary but requires prospectus, QIP requires special resolution and 25% minimum public shareholding. Memorise the 75% QIB subscription rule for EBP — it is a unique feature not present in public issues or QIPs.

SEBI's Framework for Differential Voting Rights Shares: Issuance Conditions, Conversion Mechanics, and the Tata Motors Precedent

The Companies Act, 2013 (Section 43 read with Rule 4 of the Companies (Share Capital and Debentures) Rules, 2014) and SEBI's ICDR Regulations (Regulation 292-294) and LODR Regulations (Regulation 31A) jointly govern the issuance and listing of Differential Voting Rights (DVR) shares in India. DVR shares are equity shares that carry voting rights different from ordinary shares — typically fractional voting rights (e.g., 1/10th vote per share) but with superior dividend rights. The framework was significantly liberalised in 2019: the Companies (Share Capital and Debentures) Amendment Rules, 2019 removed the earlier cap that DVR shares could not exceed 25% of total post-issue paid-up capital; now the limit is 74% (since at least 26% must remain with ordinary shares to ensure meaningful voting control). The issuance requires: (1) authorisation in the Articles of Association; (2) special resolution by shareholders; (3) the company must not have defaulted on debt repayment or statutory dues; (4) the issue must be for cash (not for consideration other than cash, except in a scheme of arrangement approved by NCLT); (5) the DVR shares must be listed on a recognised stock exchange if the ordinary shares are listed. SEBI's ICDR Regulations require a minimum public shareholding of 25% for the DVR class separately, and the pricing for preferential allotment of DVR shares follows the same formula as ordinary shares (higher of 90/10 trading volume weighted average price). The conversion mechanics are critical: DVR shares can be converted into ordinary shares only if the terms of issue permit conversion, the conversion ratio is fixed upfront, and the conversion is approved by a special resolution of DVR shareholders (class meeting) and ordinary shareholders. The conversion triggers a reclassification of share capital and requires stock exchange approval under LODR Regulation 31A. The Tata Motors case (2021-2022) is the landmark precedent: Tata Motors had issued 'A' Ordinary Shares (DVR) in 2008 with 1/10th voting rights and 5% higher dividend. In 2021, the board proposed a scheme of arrangement to cancel the DVR shares and issue ordinary shares in a fixed ratio (7 ordinary for 10 DVR), effectively converting them. The scheme required approval from: (a) DVR shareholders (75% by value in class meeting), (b) ordinary shareholders (75% by value), (c) secured creditors, (d) NCLT, and (e) stock exchanges. The DVR shareholders initially rejected the scheme in the class meeting (voting against conversion due to perceived undervaluation). Tata Motors then revised the ratio to 1:1 (one ordinary for one DVR) and obtained approvals. The NCLT Mumbai bench approved the scheme in March 2022, and the DVR shares were delisted in September 2022. This case established that DVR conversion is not a unilateral issuer right — it requires genuine class consent, and the valuation must be fair to both classes. SEBI's 2022 amendment to LODR Regulation 31A now explicitly requires that any scheme involving DVR conversion must provide an exit option to dissenting DVR shareholders at a price determined by a registered valuer, addressing the Tata Motors precedent.

Regulatory Architecture for Gold Exchanges and Electronic Gold Receipts: SEBI's 2021 Framework, Vaulting, and Settlement

SEBI's circular dated 28 December 2021 (SEBI/HO/MRD/MRD_DOP/P/CIR/2021/686) notified the regulatory framework for Gold Exchanges and Electronic Gold Receipts (EGRs), operationalising the Budget 2021 announcement. A Gold Exchange is a recognised stock exchange (or a separate segment of an existing exchange) that provides a platform for trading in EGRs. An EGR is a depository receipt representing physical gold held in a SEBI-registered vault; it is a "security" under the Securities Contracts (Regulation) Act, 1956. The framework designates three key intermediaries: (1) Vault Managers — entities registered with SEBI under the Vault Managers Regulations, 2021, responsible for assaying, storing, and safeguarding physical gold; (2) Depositories (NSDL/CDSL) — which hold EGRs in dematerialised form and facilitate transfer; (3) Clearing Corporations — which guarantee settlement. The vault manager must have a minimum net worth of ₹50 crore, professional indemnity insurance of ₹25 crore, and vaults accredited by the Bureau of Indian Standards (BIS) or London Bullion Market Association (LBMA). Gold deposited must meet the "Good Delivery" standard (995 or 999 fineness, 1 kg bars). The vault manager issues EGRs to the depositor's demat account upon verification. Trading occurs in lots of 1 gram (EGR denomination), with tick size of ₹1. Settlement is T+1 (trade date + 1 working day) for the EGR leg, while the physical gold leg (if delivery is opted) settles on T+2 from the vault. The framework permits two models: (a) Exchange-traded EGRs — traded on the gold exchange segment, cleared by the clearing corporation; (b) Bilateral EGRs — transferred off-market between demat accounts, typically for institutional settlement. The first Gold Exchange segment was launched by BSE (BSE Gold) in October 2022, followed by NSE (NSE Gold) in January 2023. As of March 2024, over 12 vault managers are registered, with aggregate vaulting capacity exceeding 5,000 kg. The framework integrates with the India International Bullion Exchange (IIBX) at GIFT City: EGRs issued against gold imported via IIBX can be traded on domestic gold exchanges, creating a seamless import-to-retail chain. Key regulatory safeguards: (1) Vault managers must conduct daily reconciliation of physical gold vs EGRs outstanding; (2) SEBI conducts quarterly inspections; (3) Investor protection fund maintained by the exchange covers vault manager default up to ₹25 lakh per investor; (4) EGRs are eligible for pledge/hypothecation, enabling gold-backed lending. The 2023 amendment allowed mutual funds to launch Gold ETFs that hold EGRs (instead of physical gold directly), reducing custody costs. A critical design choice: EGRs do not carry interest or yield — they are pure gold exposure instruments. The framework explicitly prohibits fractional reserve vaulting: every EGR must be backed 1:1 by physical gold in the vault at all times.

Foreign Venture Capital Investors: Registration, Investment Conditions, and Distinction from FPI Route

The SEBI (Foreign Venture Capital Investors) Regulations, 2000, notified on 18 January 2000 and substantially amended in 2013 and 2019, provide a dedicated registration and investment framework for foreign investors seeking to invest in Indian venture capital undertakings (VCUs) and venture capital funds (VCFs). An FVCI is defined as an entity incorporated outside India that proposes to make investments in Indian VCUs/VCFs and is registered with SEBI. The registration eligibility requires: (1) the applicant to be regulated by an appropriate foreign regulatory authority (central bank, securities regulator, or financial services authority); (2) a track record of at least five years in venture capital/private equity investments; (3) minimum assets under management of USD 50 million (reduced from USD 100 million in the 2019 amendment); (4) a fit and proper certificate from the home regulator. The registration is granted for five years, renewable. FVCIs can invest in: (a) equity or equity-linked instruments of VCUs — unlisted companies engaged in sectors such as biotechnology, IT, nanotechnology, dairy, poultry, etc., as notified by the Central Government; (b) units of SEBI-registered Category I AIFs (venture capital funds, SME funds, social impact funds, infrastructure funds); (c) units of Category II AIFs (private equity funds, debt funds) subject to conditions. The investment conditions are distinct from the FPI route: (1) No investment limit per investee company (FPI has 10% single issuer limit); (2) No restriction on sectoral caps except prohibited sectors (real estate, non-banking financial activities not permitted by RBI); (3) Lock-in: FVCI investments in VCUs are subject to a three-year lock-in from the date of investment (earlier one year, extended in 2013); (4) Pricing: for unlisted VCUs, pricing is free (negotiated); for listed VCUs, pricing follows SEBI's preferential issue guidelines. FVCIs enjoy pass-through tax treatment under Section 115U of the Income-tax Act for income distributed by VCFs/AIFs, provided the FVCI is a resident of a country with which India has a DTAA and meets the "principal purpose" test. The 2019 amendments aligned FVCI reporting with the FPI regime: FVCIs must now report holdings monthly to the designated depository participant (DDP) and comply with KYC/AML norms under the Prevention of Money Laundering Act. The key distinction from FPI: FVCI is a "strategic, long-term" category with no breaching of sectoral caps, no aggregate investment limit, and eligibility to invest in unlisted early-stage companies. FPIs, by contrast, are portfolio investors with strict limits (10% per issuer, 24% sectoral cap unless board resolution), mandatory public float requirements for listed investments, and no access to unlisted equity (except via QIP/IPO). However, FVCIs cannot invest in listed companies beyond 10% of paid-up capital without triggering open offer obligations under the Takeover Regulations. As of March 2024, 285 FVCIs are registered, with major jurisdictions being Mauritius, Singapore, USA, and UK. The FVCI route is preferred by global VC/PE funds (Sequoia, Accel, SoftBank's early funds) for early-stage bets, while the FPI route dominates public market allocations.

  • Confusing the EBP framework's mandatory QIB subscription threshold (75%) with the public issue QIB reservation (50%) — they are distinct regimes with different consequences for undersubscription.
  • Assuming DVR shares can be converted to ordinary shares at the issuer's discretion — conversion requires a special resolution of the DVR class meeting, ordinary shareholders, NCLT approval, and stock exchange nod; the Tata Motors case shows DVR shareholders can block conversion.
  • Treating Electronic Gold Receipts (EGRs) as equivalent to Gold ETF units — EGRs are depository receipts backed 1:1 by physical gold in a SEBI-registered vault, trade on a gold exchange segment, and have no expense ratio; Gold ETFs are mutual fund units holding gold/EGRs, trade on the cash market segment, and carry a TER.
  • Believing FVCIs can invest freely in any Indian company — FVCIs are restricted to notified "venture capital undertaking" sectors (biotech, IT, nanotech, etc.) and SEBI-registered AIFs; they cannot invest in real estate, general NBFCs, or listed companies beyond 10% without takeover obligations.
  • Overlooking that the proxy advisor's "comply or explain" code requires disclosure of the voting policy, not mandatory adherence to a SEBI-prescribed policy — SEBI does not dictate the content of voting recommendations, only the transparency and conflict-management process.
  • Assuming the EBP platform is used for equity private placements (QIPs/preferential issues) — EBP is exclusively for debt securities and non-convertible redeemable preference shares; equity private placements follow the ICDR preferential issue/QIP framework with pricing formulas, not electronic bidding.
  • Confusing the vault manager's net worth requirement (₹50 crore) with the clearing corporation's net worth requirement — they are separate intermediaries; the vault manager handles physical gold, the clearing corporation guarantees financial settlement of EGR trades.

The advanced regulatory frameworks for proxy advisors, electronic book building, differential voting rights, gold exchanges, and foreign venture capital investors collectively illustrate SEBI's evolution from a disclosure-based regulator to an architecture designer for market microstructure, investor protection, and capital formation in specialised segments.

SEBI's Framework for the Innovators Growth Platform: Listing Norms for Start‑ups, Relaxed Disclosure and Investor Protection Measures

The Innovators Growth Platform (IGP) was launched by SEBI in 2020 through the SEBI (Issue of Capital and Disclosure Requirements) (Second Amendment) Regulations, 2020, to enable early‑stage, high‑growth companies to list without meeting the profitability track‑record required on the main board. Eligibility is restricted to entities recognised as “start‑ups” by the Department for Promotion of Industry and Internal Trade (DPIIT) and which have a minimum post‑issue paid‑up capital of ₹10 crore. The platform permits a reduced public float of 25 % (versus 25 % on the main board but with a lower absolute threshold) and a minimum public shareholding of 10 % after listing, compared with 25 % on the main board. Issuers may raise capital through an initial public offer (IPO) or a follow‑on public offer (FPO) with a simplified offer document that omits the five‑year financial history requirement; instead, audited financials for the preceding two years and a certificate from a merchant banker on the fairness of the issue price are mandated.

Investor protection mechanisms are built into the IGP framework. A mandatory lock‑in of 30 days applies to shares allotted to anchor investors, while promoters and pre‑IPO shareholders are subject to a one‑year lock‑in from the date of allotment. The platform also requires the appointment of a designated market maker for a minimum period of three years to provide liquidity, with a maximum spread of 2 % of the prevailing market price. Continuous disclosure obligations are aligned with the Listing Obligations and Disclosure Requirements (LODR) Regulations, but the frequency of certain filings (e.g., quarterly financial results) is relaxed to half‑yearly for the first two years. SEBI also introduced a “graduation” pathway: once an IGP‑listed company meets the main‑board profitability and net‑worth criteria for two consecutive years, it may migrate to the main board without a fresh IPO, subject to shareholder approval and compliance with the main‑board listing agreement.

As of March 2024, 12 companies have listed on the IGP, predominantly from the fintech, health‑tech and deep‑tech sectors. The platform’s design reflects SEBI’s intent to deepen the domestic capital pool for innovation‑driven enterprises while preserving market integrity through calibrated safeguards.

SEBI's Cyber Resilience Framework for Market Infrastructure Institutions: Incident Reporting, Business Continuity Planning and Penetration Testing Standards

In June 2022 SEBI issued the “Cyber Security and Cyber Resilience Framework for Market Infrastructure Institutions (MIIs)” (SEBI/HO/MIRSD/MIRSD_CIR/P/2022/68), extending the earlier 2015 guidelines to cover stock exchanges, clearing corporations, depositories and the Commodity Derivatives Exchanges. The framework mandates a three‑tier governance structure: a Board‑level Cyber Security Committee, a Chief Information Security Officer (CISO) with direct reporting to the Managing Director, and a dedicated Security Operations Centre (SOC) operating 24 × 7. MIIs must classify cyber incidents into four severity levels; Level 1 (critical) incidents — such as unauthorised access to trade‑matching engines or settlement systems — must be reported to SEBI within six hours of detection, with a detailed root‑cause analysis submitted within 30 days.

Business Continuity Planning (BCP) requirements specify a Recovery Time Objective (RTO) of four hours for core trading and settlement functions and a Recovery Point Objective (RPO) of zero for transaction data. MIIs are required to conduct annual tabletop exercises and a full‑scale disaster‑recovery drill every two years, with results reported to SEBI. Penetration testing must be performed at least once a year by an empanelled external auditor; the scope includes network perimeter, application layer (order‑matching, clearing, depository interfaces) and privileged‑access management. Vulnerabilities rated “high” or “critical” must be remediated within 30 days, and a re‑test report filed with SEBI.

The circular also introduces a “Cyber Resilience Index” that SEBI will compute quarterly based on incident frequency, mean‑time‑to‑detect, mean‑time‑to‑recover, and penetration‑test outcomes. MIIs falling below the prescribed threshold for two consecutive quarters face supervisory actions ranging from enhanced reporting to restriction on new product launches. The framework aligns with the RBI’s “Cyber Security Framework for Banks” and the International Organization of Securities Commissions (IOSCO) principles, ensuring a harmonised approach across the financial sector.

Examiner's technique
A question asks: “Under SEBI’s 2022 Cyber Resilience Framework, what is the maximum time allowed for a Market Infrastructure Institution to report a Level‑1 cyber incident to SEBI, and what is the prescribed Recovery Time Objective for core trading functions?”
The framework requires Level‑1 (critical) incidents to be reported within six hours of detection. The Recovery Time Objective (RTO) for core trading and settlement functions is set at four hours. Both figures are explicitly stated in the circular (SEBI/HO/MIRSD/MIRSD_CIR/P/2022/68) and are frequently tested as paired recall items.
Exam‑day strategy
Memorise the four key numbers — 6 hours (incident reporting), 30 days (root‑cause analysis), 4 hours (RTO) and 0 RPO — as a “6‑30‑4‑0” mnemonic. Questions often combine them with the RBI’s parallel framework; keep the two regimes separate by noting that RBI mandates a 2‑hour reporting window for banks, whereas SEBI allows 6 hours for MIIs.

SEBI's Regulatory Treatment of Perpetual Bonds Issued by Banks and NBFCs: Coupon Reset Mechanics, Call Options and Capital Adequacy Impact

Perpetual Non‑Convertible Debentures (PNCDs) issued by scheduled commercial banks and eligible NBFCs are governed by SEBI’s “Guidelines for Issuance of Perpetual Debt Instruments” (SEBI/HO/DDHS/DDHS_CIR/P/2021/0000000123) read with the RBI’s Basel III Master Circular (DBR.No.BP.BC.83/21.06.201/2021‑22). These instruments qualify as Additional Tier 1 (AT1) capital provided they meet the “loss‑absorbency” criteria: the issuer must have the unilateral right to cancel coupon payments (coupon discretion) and the instrument must be written down or converted into equity upon a trigger event such as the Common Equity Tier 1 (CET1) ratio falling below 5.5 % for banks or 6 % for NBFCs.

The coupon is typically fixed for the first 10 years (the “initial coupon period”) and thereafter reset every five years based on a benchmark (e.g., 5‑year G‑sec yield plus a spread). The reset formula is disclosed in the offer document and cannot be altered without holder consent. A call option is embedded, exercisable by the issuer only after the fifth anniversary and subject to prior RBI approval; the call price is usually par plus accrued coupon. If the call is not exercised, the instrument remains outstanding perpetually, and the coupon continues to be reset.

From a capital adequacy perspective, the amount counted as AT1 capital is capped at 1.5 % of risk‑weighted assets (RWA) for banks and 2 % for NBFCs. Any excess is treated as Tier 2 capital. The RBI’s 2023 amendment to the Basel III framework introduced a “grandfathering” clause: PNCDs issued before 31 March 2023 that do not meet the new loss‑absorbency trigger (CET1 < 5.5 %) will be phased out over a 10‑year period, reducing their AT1 eligibility by 10 % annually. SEBI’s listing conditions require a minimum issue size of ₹500 crore, a credit rating of at least “AA‑” from a SEBI‑registered CRA, and a mandatory debenture trustee. The trust deed must contain a “write‑down/conversion” clause that is automatically triggered without requiring holder consent, ensuring immediate loss absorption.

SEBI's Guidelines for the Use of Artificial Intelligence in Algorithmic Trading: Model Validation, Explainability Requirements and Audit Trail Obligations

SEBI’s 2023 circular “Use of Artificial Intelligence and Machine Learning in Algorithmic Trading” (SEBI/HO/MRD/MRD_CIR/P/2023/45) addresses the growing deployment of AI‑driven strategies by proprietary trading desks, high‑frequency traders and asset managers. The circular classifies AI models into three risk tiers: Tier 1 (rule‑based, deterministic), Tier 2 (supervised learning with static features) and Tier 3 (deep learning, reinforcement learning, or any model that updates weights in‑production). Only Tier 1 models may be deployed without prior SEBI approval; Tier 2 and Tier 3 models require a “Model Validation Report” from an independent empanelled auditor before go‑live.

Validation must cover data integrity (source, preprocessing, look‑ahead bias), model stability (out‑of‑sample performance across market regimes), and adversarial robustness (stress testing under extreme price shocks). Explainability is mandated for Tier 2 and Tier 3: the trading entity must maintain a “Model Explainability Document” that maps each input feature to its marginal contribution for a representative sample of trades, using techniques such as SHAP values or Integrated Gradients. This document must be updated quarterly and made available to SEBI on request.

Audit trail obligations require the capture of every order‑generation event, including the model version identifier, input feature vector, predicted signal, and the final order parameters (price, quantity, venue). Logs must be immutable (write‑once storage) and retained for a minimum of five years. SEBI also introduced a “Model Governance Committee” at the board level, comprising the CISO, Chief Risk Officer and an independent director, tasked with approving model changes, monitoring drift metrics (population stability index > 0.2 triggers review), and certifying annual compliance. Non‑compliance attracts penalties under Section 15HA of the SEBI Act, up to ₹25 crore or three times the profit made, whichever is higher.

SEBI's Oversight of the Clearing Corporation of India Limited: Default Fund Architecture, Margin Methodology and Stress‑Testing Regime

The Clearing Corporation of India Limited (CCIL) operates as the central counterparty (CCP) for the government securities (G‑sec) segment, the foreign exchange (FX) spot and forward market, and the rupee‑denominated interest‑rate derivatives. SEBI’s recognition of CCIL as a “Qualified Central Counterparty” (QCCP) under the SEBI (Clearing Corporations) Regulations, 2012, subjects it to the Principles for Financial Market Infrastructures (PFMI) issued by CPMI‑IOSCO. The default fund (also called the “Settlement Guarantee Fund”) is capitalised at ₹5,000 crore as of March 2024, contributed proportionally by clearing members based on their average daily turnover. The fund is segmented into a “Core Fund” (80 %) and a “Stress Fund” (20 %) to absorb losses under extreme but plausible scenarios.

Margin methodology follows a Value‑at‑Risk (VaR) approach at a 99 % confidence level over a 10‑day liquidation horizon for G‑sec repos and a 5‑day horizon for FX forwards, supplemented by an Extreme Loss Margin (ELM) calibrated to the 99.9 th percentile of historical price moves. Intraday margin calls are triggered when a member’s net exposure exceeds 75 % of its initial margin. CCIL conducts daily stress tests covering six scenarios: (i) a 200‑basis‑point parallel shift in the yield curve, (ii) a 15 % depreciation of the rupee, (iii) a 30 % drop in G‑sec liquidity, (iv) a simultaneous default of the two largest clearing members, (v) a cyber‑induced settlement outage of four hours, and (vi) a combined macro‑financial shock calibrated to the 2008 crisis. Results are reported to SEBI and the RBI within 24 hours; any shortfall in the default fund beyond the Stress Fund triggers a mandatory capital call on all members within 48 hours.

SEBI’s 2022 amendment to the Clearing Corporations Regulations introduced a “Recovery and Resolution Plan” (RRP) for CCIL, requiring a pre‑funded “Resolution Reserve” of ₹1,000 crore, a designated resolution authority (the RBI), and a clear hierarchy of loss allocation: (1) defaulting member’s margin and default fund contribution, (2) Stress Fund, (3) Core Fund, (4) Resolution Reserve, (5) assessment on non‑defaulting members. The RRP is tested annually through a tabletop exercise involving SEBI, RBI, the Ministry of Finance and major clearing members.

  • Confusing the IGP’s 25 % public float requirement with the main board’s 25 % but higher absolute threshold — the IGP allows a lower absolute float because of the ₹10 crore paid‑up capital floor.
  • Assuming the Cyber Resilience Framework’s six‑hour reporting window applies to all regulated entities — it is specific to Market Infrastructure Institutions; banks follow RBI’s two‑hour rule.
  • Treating perpetual bonds as pure debt for capital adequacy — they count as AT1 capital only if they meet loss‑absorbency triggers and are subject to the 1.5 % RWA cap.
  • Believing AI model validation is optional for all algorithmic strategies — Tier 2 and Tier 3 models require an independent validation report before deployment.
  • Overlooking the segmented default fund structure at CCIL — the Core Fund and Stress Fund serve distinct loss‑absorption layers and are not interchangeable.
  • Mixing up the VaR confidence levels for G‑sec repos (99 % over 10 days) and FX forwards (99 % over 5 days) — the liquidation horizon differs by product class.
  • Assuming the call option on perpetual bonds can be exercised at any time — it is exercisable only after the fifth anniversary and with prior RBI approval.

SEBI's Regulatory Framework for Exchange Traded Funds and the Authorised Participant Mechanism

Exchange Traded Funds (ETFs) in India are governed primarily by the SEBI (Mutual Funds) Regulations, 1996, read with the SEBI (ETF) Circular dated 9 January 2012 and subsequent amendments. An ETF is a scheme that tracks an index, a commodity, a basket of securities or a sector and whose units are listed and traded on a recognised stock exchange. The key structural feature that distinguishes ETFs from conventional open‑ended mutual funds is the Authorised Participant (AP) mechanism. APs are typically large institutional investors — banks, broker‑dealers or market makers — that have entered into a creation/redemption agreement with the Asset Management Company (AMC). They create new ETF units by delivering the underlying portfolio of securities (or cash in‑lieu) to the AMC and receive creation units, usually in blocks of 50,000 or 100,000 units. Conversely, they redeem units by returning creation units to the AMC and receiving the underlying securities.

The AP mechanism ensures that the market price of an ETF stays close to its Net Asset Value (NAV) through arbitrage. When the ETF trades at a premium, APs create units, increasing supply and pushing the price down; when it trades at a discount, APs redeem units, reducing supply and pushing the price up. SEBI’s 2012 circular mandated that at least two APs be appointed for each ETF, that the creation/redemption basket be disclosed daily, and that the AMC publish the indicative NAV (iNAV) every 15 seconds during market hours. In 2020 SEBI introduced a requirement for APs to maintain a minimum net worth of ₹50 crore and to submit monthly reports on creation/redemption activity, enhancing transparency and reducing concentration risk.

Tax treatment of ETFs follows the mutual fund regime: equity‑oriented ETFs (more than 65 % in domestic equities) enjoy long‑term capital gains (LTCG) exemption up to ₹1 lakh per financial year and a 10 % tax on gains above that threshold without indexation; non‑equity ETFs are taxed at 20 % with indexation for LTCG and at slab rates for short‑term gains. The 2023 Finance Act clarified that the “grandfathering” provision for equity‑oriented funds applies to units acquired before 31 January 2018, a point often tested in examinations.

Recent developments include the launch of India’s first bond ETF (the Bharat Bond ETF) in December 2019, which tracks a portfolio of AAA‑rated public sector undertaking bonds and uses a unique “fixed maturity” structure. SEBI’s 2021 circular on “ETFs on Commodity Indices” permitted the creation of commodity‑linked ETFs subject to position limits prescribed by the Forward Markets Commission (now merged into SEBI). The 2022 amendment to the SEBI (Mutual Funds) Regulations introduced a “side‑pocketing” framework for ETFs holding illiquid securities, aligning them with the treatment of open‑ended schemes.

From an examiner’s perspective, the interplay between the AP mechanism, iNAV dissemination, and the arbitrage process is a frequent source of conceptual questions. Candidates should be able to explain why a large bid‑ask spread in the underlying securities can widen the ETF’s tracking error, and how the creation/redemption basket composition (full replication vs. optimisation) influences liquidity risk.

The Role of the Financial Stability and Development Council in Macroprudential Coordination

The Financial Stability and Development Council (FSDC) was constituted by the Government of India in December 2010 through a Gazette notification, replacing the earlier High‑Level Coordination Committee on Financial Markets. The Council is chaired by the Union Finance Minister and comprises the Governor of the Reserve Bank of India (RBI), the Chairperson of SEBI, the Chairman of the Insurance Regulatory and Development Authority of India (IRDAI), the Chairman of the Pension Fund Regulatory and Development Authority (PFRDA), the Finance Secretary, the Secretary of the Department of Economic Affairs, and the Chief Economic Adviser. Its mandate is to strengthen and institutionalise the mechanism for maintaining financial stability, enhancing inter‑regulatory coordination, and promoting financial sector development.

FSDC operates through a two‑tier structure: the Council at the apex level and a Sub‑Committee chaired by the RBI Governor that meets more frequently to monitor systemic risks. The Sub‑Committee reviews indicators such as credit‑to‑GDP gaps, asset‑price inflation, leverage in the non‑bank financial sector, and cross‑border capital flow volatility. It also oversees the implementation of the Macroprudential Policy Framework released by the RBI in 2014, which includes tools like the Countercyclical Capital Buffer (CCyB), Sectoral Capital Requirements, and the Leverage Ratio for banks and NBFCs.

One of the Council’s notable interventions was the 2018 decision to activate the CCyB at 0.5 % for scheduled commercial banks, the first such activation since the framework’s inception. The decision was based on the Sub‑Committee’s assessment that the credit‑to‑GDP gap had breached the 2 % threshold and that corporate leverage was rising. In 2020, amid the COVID‑19 pandemic, FSDC recommended a temporary relaxation of the Leverage Ratio for banks to 3.5 % from 4 % and a moratorium on loan repayments, which the RBI implemented through its “COVID‑19 Regulatory Package”.

FSDC also coordinates the resolution of systemically important financial institutions (SIFIs). The 2019 amendment to the Insolvency and Bankruptcy Code (IBC) introduced a “Financial Sector Resolution Framework” that empowers the Council to recommend the initiation of resolution proceedings for a SIFI, subject to the approval of the Central Government. This framework was tested in 2021 when the Council recommended the resolution of a large NBFC‑DSI (Systemically Important NBFC) under the newly notified NBFC‑DSI framework.

Examination questions often focus on the distinction between the FSDC’s advisory role and the statutory powers of individual regulators. The Council does not have independent rule‑making authority; its recommendations are implemented by the respective regulators through their own regulations. Candidates should also be aware of the “FSDC Report on Financial Stability” published annually, which forms a key source for data on systemic risk indicators.

Worked Example
A question asks: “Explain how the Authorised Participant mechanism helps keep an ETF’s market price aligned with its NAV. Illustrate with a scenario where the ETF trades at a premium.”
When an ETF trades at a premium, its market price exceeds the indicative NAV (iNAV). Authorised Participants (APs) can exploit this by delivering the creation basket — the exact portfolio of securities that replicates the index — to the AMC in exchange for creation units of the ETF. The APs then sell these newly created units in the secondary market, increasing supply. The additional supply pushes the market price down toward the iNAV. Simultaneously, the APs hold the underlying securities, which they can hedge or retain. The arbitrage continues until the premium disappears, ensuring price‑NAV convergence. The key steps are: (1) observe premium, (2) create units via AP, (3) sell units in market, (4) price falls to NAV. This mechanism relies on low transaction costs, transparent iNAV dissemination every 15 seconds, and the ability of APs to access the underlying securities efficiently.
Exam‑Day Strategy
For questions on ETF arbitrage, always mention the three pillars: (i) real‑time iNAV publication, (ii) creation/redemption basket transparency, and (iii) AP net‑worth and reporting requirements. For FSDC, remember the hierarchy: Council (chaired by Finance Minister) → Sub‑Committee (chaired by RBI Governor) → individual regulators implement recommendations. Cite the 2018 CCyB activation and the 2020 COVID‑19 leverage relaxation as concrete examples of macroprudential action.

SEBI's Guidelines on the Use of Artificial Intelligence and Machine Learning in Market Surveillance

In June 2021 SEBI issued a circular titled “Use of Artificial Intelligence and Machine Learning for Market Surveillance” (SEBI/HO/MRD/MRD‑1/CIR/P/2021/00000001) directing all recognised stock exchanges, clearing corporations and depositories to adopt AI/ML‑based surveillance systems for detecting market abuse, insider trading, and anomalous order behaviour. The circular mandated that the surveillance engine must be capable of ingesting order‑book data, trade data, and reference data in real time, and of generating alerts with a false‑positive rate not exceeding 5 % for the top‑10 alert categories.

The framework classifies surveillance models into three tiers. Tier 1 models are rule‑based (e.g., price‑volume spikes, order‑to‑trade ratio breaches) and must be operational from day 1. Tier 2 models employ supervised learning (e.g., random forest, gradient boosting) trained on labelled historical alerts; they require an independent validation report from a SEBI‑empanelled auditor before deployment. Tier 3 models use unsupervised or deep‑learning techniques (e.g., auto‑encoders for anomaly detection, graph neural networks for collusion detection) and are subject to a “model governance committee” at the board level, comprising the Chief Information Security Officer, Chief Risk Officer and an independent director. This committee must approve model version changes, monitor drift metrics such as the Population Stability Index (PSI > 0.2 triggers a review), and certify annual compliance.

Exchanges are required to maintain an immutable audit trail for every order‑generation event: model version identifier, input feature vector, predicted signal, and final order parameters (price, quantity, venue). Logs must be stored on write‑once media for a minimum of five years and made available to SEBI on request. Non‑compliance attracts penalties under Section 15HA of the SEBI Act, up to ₹25 crore or three times the profit made, whichever is higher.

In 2023 SEBI released a supplementary circular clarifying that the AI/ML framework also applies to algorithmic trading platforms offered by brokers. Brokers deploying proprietary execution algorithms must submit a “Model Risk Management Report” quarterly, detailing model performance, feature importance (using SHAP values or Integrated Gradients), and any retraining events. The circular also introduced a “sandbox” for testing new surveillance models on a synthetic data set provided by SEBI, reducing the risk of live‑market disruption.

From an examination standpoint, candidates should be able to differentiate the three tiers, explain the role of the Model Governance Committee, and describe the audit‑trail requirements. A common twist is to ask whether a broker’s smart‑order‑router that uses reinforcement learning falls under Tier 2 or Tier 3; the correct answer is Tier 3 because it employs an unsupervised/self‑learning approach and therefore requires board‑level governance.

The Evolution of the Corporate Bond Market: From Private Placement to Electronic Book Building and Public Issue

India’s corporate bond market has historically been dominated by private placements, which accounted for over 90 % of issuance volume until the mid‑2010s. The Companies Act, 2013, and the SEBI (Issue and Listing of Debt Securities) Regulations, 2008 (ILDS) governed private placements, requiring a minimum subscription of ₹1 crore per investor and a lock‑in of one year for qualified institutional buyers (QIBs). The lack of a transparent price‑discovery mechanism and limited secondary‑market liquidity were persistent concerns.

A turning point came in 2014 when SEBI, in coordination with the Bombay Stock Exchange (BSE) and the National Stock Exchange (NSE), launched the Electronic Book Building Platform (EBBP) for private placement of debt securities. The EBBP enabled issuers to invite bids from QIBs through an electronic order book, providing real‑time price discovery and reducing the time‑to‑issue from weeks to days. The platform also mandated a minimum bid size of ₹10 lakh and a maximum of 200 bids per issue, broadening the investor base beyond a handful of large banks.

In 2015 SEBI issued a circular permitting public issue of non‑convertible debentures (NCDs) by listed companies, subject to a minimum issue size of ₹100 crore, a minimum public subscription of 25 % of the issue, and credit rating of at least AA‑. The first public issue under this framework was by a major infrastructure firm in March 2016, raising ₹1,200 crore. The public issue route introduced retail participation (minimum application ₹10,000) and mandated listing on the debt segment of a recognised stock exchange, thereby creating a secondary‑market footprint.

The introduction of the Corporate Bond Repo market in 2018 (operated by the Clearing Corporation of India Limited) and the migration to T+1 settlement for corporate bonds in 2022 further enhanced liquidity. SEBI’s 2020 “Market Maker Framework” designated select broker‑dealers as market makers for illiquid corporate bonds, obligating them to provide two‑way quotes with a maximum spread of 50 bps for a minimum quote size of ₹5 crore.

Recent data from the Securities and Exchange Board of India’s annual report (2023‑24) shows that public issues now constitute roughly 12 % of total corporate bond issuance, up from 2 % in 2015, while private placements via EBBP account for 68 %. The remaining share is through the “Qualified Institutional Placement” (QIP) route for debt, introduced in 2019. Examiners often test the chronological sequence: ILDS 2008 → EBBP 2014 → Public Issue Circular 2015 → Corporate Bond Repo 2018 → T+1 2022 → Market Maker Framework 2020. Understanding the interplay between primary‑market reforms and secondary‑market infrastructure is essential for answering cause‑effect questions.

Regulatory Treatment of Special Purpose Vehicles in Securitisation and Covered Bond Structures

Special Purpose Vehicles (SPVs) are the legal backbone of securitisation and covered bond transactions in India. The SEBI (Securitisation) Regulations, 2008, as amended in 2016, define an SPV as a company, trust or limited liability partnership formed solely for the purpose of acquiring financial assets and issuing securities backed by those assets. The SPV must be bankruptcy‑remote: its assets are legally separated from the originator’s balance sheet, and it cannot engage in any business other than the securitisation transaction.

For asset‑backed securitisation (ABS), the originator (typically an NBFC or a bank) transfers a pool of receivables — such as auto loans, micro‑finance loans, or housing loans — to the SPV. The SPV issues pass‑through certificates (PTCs) or pay‑through certificates to investors. The 2016 amendment introduced a “minimum retention requirement” (MRR) of 5 % of the securitised pool for the originator, aligned with the Basel III securitisation framework. It also mandated that the SPV appoint an independent trustee and a credit rating agency registered with SEBI to rate the PTCs.

Covered bonds, governed by the RBI’s “Guidelines on Covered Bonds” (2014) and SEBI’s “Regulatory Framework for Covered Bonds” (2020), differ in that the cover pool remains on the issuer’s balance sheet while a parallel SPV (the “Covered Bond Guarantor”) holds a duplicate pool for the benefit of bondholders. The SPV issues a guarantee to the covered bond investors. The RBI requires a minimum over‑collateralisation of 10 % and a loan‑to‑value (LTV) cap of 80 % for residential mortgage cover pools. The SEBI framework adds disclosure requirements: quarterly asset‑cover reports, a register of cover‑pool assets, and a valuation by an independent valuer every six months.

Tax treatment of SPVs is critical. The Finance Act, 2013 inserted Section 10(23FC) of the Income‑Tax Act, granting pass‑through status to securitisation trusts, meaning income is taxed in the hands of investors rather than the SPV. However, covered bond SPVs do not enjoy this exemption; interest paid to the SPV by the issuer is taxable, and the guarantee fee received by the SPV is treated as income. The 2022 Finance Act clarified that the “look‑through” approach applies for determining the tax residency of foreign investors in PTCs.

Recent case law includes the 2021 Supreme Court judgment in XYZ Securitisation Trust v. Income‑Tax Department, which upheld the pass‑through status of a trust that had inadvertently commingled a small portion of its own funds with the securitised pool, ruling that the commingling did not defeat the bankruptcy‑remoteness requirement. Examiners may ask about the distinction between “true sale” and “secured lending” in the context of SPV structures; the key test is whether the originator retains any substantive risk or reward beyond the MRR.

  • Confusing the EBBP (Electronic Book Building Platform) with the public issue route — EBBP is for private placement to QIBs only.
  • Assuming the FSDC has statutory rule‑making power — it only issues recommendations implemented by individual regulators.
  • Treating Tier 2 AI/ML models as deployable without independent validation — SEBI mandates a validation report before go‑live.
  • Believing covered bond SPVs enjoy the same tax pass‑through status as securitisation trusts — they do not.
  • Mixing up the Minimum Retention Requirement (5 % for ABS) with the over‑collateralisation requirement (10 % for covered bonds).
  • Overlooking the requirement that the Model Governance Committee must include an independent director — a frequent compliance gap.
  • Thinking the Corporate Bond Repo market operates on T+0 settlement — it follows the same T+1 cycle as the underlying bonds.

Financial Stability and Development Council: Composition, Mandate and Macroprudential Coordination

The Financial Stability and Development Council (FSDC) was constituted in December 2010 by an executive order of the Government of India, replacing the earlier High‑Level Coordination Committee on Financial Markets. It is chaired by the Union Finance Minister and its statutory members include the Governor of the Reserve Bank of India, the Chairperson of the Securities and Exchange Board of India, the Chairperson of the Insurance Regulatory and Development Authority of India, the Chairperson of the Pension Fund Regulatory and Development Authority, the Secretary of the Department of Economic Affairs, the Secretary of the Department of Financial Services, and the Chief Economic Adviser. The Council may co‑opt additional experts for specific agenda items.

The FSDC’s mandate is three‑fold: (1) macroprudential supervision of the financial system, including identification of systemic risks and coordination of policy responses; (2) financial sector development, covering financial inclusion, literacy and market deepening; and (3) inter‑regulatory coordination to avoid regulatory arbitrage and ensure consistent enforcement. The Council meets at least once every quarter; extraordinary meetings can be convened when a systemic event — such as a sudden capital‑flow reversal or a major institutional failure — demands immediate collective action.

Operationally, the FSDC relies on a dedicated secretariat housed in the Department of Economic Affairs. The secretariat prepares the Financial Stability Report (FSR) twice a year, drawing on data from the RBI’s Systemic Risk Survey, SEBI’s market surveillance outputs, IRDAI’s solvency returns and PFRDA’s pension fund stress tests. The FSR contains a heat‑map of risk indicators (credit growth, asset‑price inflation, leverage, interconnectedness) and a set of policy recommendations that are non‑binding but carry significant moral authority; each regulator is expected to report on implementation at the next Council meeting.

A notable illustration of the Council’s macroprudential role occurred in 2020‑21 when the FSDC endorsed the RBI’s decision to activate the Counter‑Cyclical Capital Buffer (CCyB) at 0.5 % for scheduled commercial banks, and simultaneously recommended that SEBI tighten margin requirements for equity derivatives to curb excessive leverage. The coordinated action helped contain the build‑up of systemic risk during the pandemic‑induced market volatility. The Council also oversees the implementation of the National Strategy for Financial Inclusion (2019‑24) and monitors progress on the Pradhan Mantri Jan Dhan Yojana, the Direct Benefit Transfer architecture and the rollout of the Account Aggregator framework.

Although the FSDC does not possess statutory rule‑making powers, its recommendations are routinely translated into regulations by the respective regulators. For instance, the 2022 FSDC recommendation on enhancing disclosure standards for climate‑related financial risks led to SEBI’s Business Responsibility and Sustainability Reporting (BRSR) amendments and RBI’s climate‑risk stress‑testing guidelines for banks. Understanding the Council’s composition, its soft‑law influence and the feedback loop between the FSR and regulator‑level actions is essential for answering questions on institutional architecture for financial stability.

RBI's External Commercial Borrowing Framework: Track Classification, End‑Use Restrictions and Hedging Requirements

The Reserve Bank of India governs external commercial borrowings (ECBs) through the Master Direction – External Commercial Borrowings, Trade Credit, Borrowing and Lending in Foreign Currency (2018), substantially revised in January 2022. The framework classifies ECBs into three tracks based on the amount, maturity and end‑use, each with distinct eligibility, reporting and hedging obligations.

Track I covers borrowings up to USD 750 million (or equivalent) per financial year with a minimum average maturity of three years. Eligible borrowers include corporates, NBFCs, infrastructure companies and units in Special Economic Zones. Permitted end‑uses are capital expenditure, refinancing of existing rupee loans, and working capital for export‑oriented units. Track I borrowings require a hedging cover of at least 70 % of the principal for tenors exceeding five years, and the borrower must submit a quarterly ECB‑2 return to the Authorised Dealer (AD) bank.

Track II allows borrowings between USD 750 million and USD 1.5 billion per financial year, with a minimum average maturity of five years. In addition to Track I end‑uses, Track II permits on‑lending to infrastructure projects and affordable housing. The hedging requirement is raised to 100 % of principal for tenors above five years, and borrowers must obtain a credit rating from at least one SEBI‑registered credit rating agency. Reporting is done through the ECB‑3 return on a half‑yearly basis.

Track III is for borrowings exceeding USD 1.5 billion or for tenors longer than ten years. This track is restricted to infrastructure, green‑field manufacturing, and strategic sectors approved by the Government. End‑use is tightly monitored; proceeds cannot be used for general corporate purposes, acquisition of equity stakes, or real‑estate speculation. Full hedging (100 % of principal and interest) is mandatory irrespective of tenor, and a quarterly ECB‑4 return with detailed cash‑flow projections must be filed. The RBI also requires a board‑approved risk‑management policy for Track III borrowers.

Across all tracks, the RBI imposes an all‑in‑cost ceiling linked to the benchmark (SOFR/LIBOR) plus a spread that varies with the borrower’s credit rating and track. The 2022 revision introduced a “green ECB” sub‑category with a 25‑basis‑point spread concession for projects certified under the RBI’s Green Taxonomy. Borrowers must also comply with the Foreign Exchange Management Act (FEMA) provisions on end‑use verification, and any deviation attracts penal interest and possible debarment from future ECB access.

The track‑based architecture enables the RBI to calibrate macroprudential controls: during periods of capital‑flow surges, the central bank can tighten the all‑in‑cost ceiling or raise the minimum maturity for Track I, while during sudden stops it can relax hedging thresholds for Track II to ease rollover risk. Mastery of the track limits, end‑use matrix, hedging ratios and reporting forms (ECB‑2/3/4) is a frequent examination focus, especially in case‑study questions that ask candidates to classify a hypothetical borrowing and prescribe the compliance checklist.

Examiner's trap
A question describes a corporate borrower raising USD 1 billion for a ten‑year green‑field solar project. The candidate must identify the correct ECB track, the applicable hedging ratio, and the reporting form.
The amount (USD 1 billion) and tenor (10 years) place the borrowing in Track II (USD 750 million–1.5 billion, minimum 5‑year maturity). Because the tenor exceeds five years, the hedging requirement is 100 % of principal. The project qualifies for the green‑ECB concession, so the all‑in‑cost ceiling is benchmark + spread − 25 bps. The borrower must file the half‑yearly ECB‑3 return and obtain a credit rating. Track III would be incorrect because the amount is below the USD 1.5 billion threshold.
Exam‑day strategy
Memorise the three‑track matrix as a table: amount range, minimum maturity, hedging ratio, reporting form, and key end‑use permissions. In the exam, sketch the table in the margin; it lets you classify any ECB scenario in seconds and avoids mixing up Track II and Track III thresholds.

SEBI's Innovators Growth Platform: Eligibility, Disclosure Regime and Investor Protection Mechanisms

SEBI launched the Innovators Growth Platform (IGP) in June 2019 as a dedicated listing avenue for early‑stage, innovation‑driven companies that do not meet the profitability or track‑record requirements of the main board. The platform operates under the SEBI (Issue of Capital and Disclosure Requirements) Regulations, 2018, with specific relaxations notified through the SEBI (IGP) Regulations, 2019 and subsequent circulars.

Eligibility criteria require the issuer to be an unlisted company incorporated in India, with a post‑issue paid‑up capital not exceeding ₹25 crore. The company must demonstrate that at least 25 % of its pre‑issue capital is held by qualified institutional buyers (QIBs) or accredited investors for a minimum of two years. The business model should be technology‑intensive, intellectual‑property‑driven, or belong to a sunrise sector identified by the Government (e.g., fintech, biotech, clean energy). A minimum of two years of operational history is mandated, but profitability is not required.

Disclosure regime is lighter than the main board but still rigorous. The offer document must contain a detailed “Innovation and Technology” section describing the IP portfolio, R&D expenditure, and scalability of the business model. Financial statements for the last three years (or since incorporation) are required, together with a “Key Business Metrics” table covering user growth, revenue per user, burn rate and runway. Post‑listing, the issuer must file quarterly financial results and a half‑yearly “Innovation Update” within 45 days of period end, disclosing material developments in technology, partnerships and regulatory approvals.

Investor protection mechanisms include a mandatory market‑making arrangement for at least three years, with the market maker required to provide two‑way quotes for a minimum of 5 % of the post‑issue capital. The platform also imposes a lock‑in of 25 % of the post‑issue capital held by promoters and pre‑issue investors for three years. SEBI has empowered the stock exchange to suspend trading if the issuer fails to submit the Innovation Update for two consecutive periods. Additionally, the Investor Education and Protection Fund (IEPF) is extended to IGP investors for unclaimed dividends and refunds.

As of March 2024, six companies have listed on the IGP, raising a cumulative ₹1,200 crore. The platform’s design reflects a calibrated approach: it lowers entry barriers while preserving transparency through sector‑specific disclosures and continuous market‑making. Questions on the IGP often test the distinction between “accredited investor” and “QIB” thresholds, the lock‑in percentages, and the consequences of non‑compliance with the Innovation Update requirement.

RBI's Co‑Lending Model for Banks and NBFCs: Operational Guidelines, Risk Sharing and Reporting Standards

The Reserve Bank of India introduced the Co‑Lending Model (CLM) through a circular dated 5 November 2020, revised in September 2022, to leverage the complementary strengths of banks (low‑cost funds) and NBFCs (origination expertise) for priority‑sector and retail lending. The model permits scheduled commercial banks and eligible NBFCs (including Housing Finance Companies) to jointly originate, underwrite and service loans, sharing both credit risk and interest income in a pre‑agreed proportion.

Operational structure requires a master agreement between the bank and the NBFC specifying the risk‑sharing ratio, typically 80:20 (bank:NBFC) for priority‑sector loans and 70:30 for non‑priority retail loans. The NBFC acts as the “originator” and performs customer acquisition, credit appraisal and documentation. The bank provides the bulk of the funding at the time of disbursement. Both parties must conduct independent credit assessments; the bank’s credit committee retains final sanction authority.

Risk‑sharing mechanics dictate that the NBFC retains a minimum of 20 % of the credit risk (the “first loss” piece) on its books, ensuring skin‑in‑the‑game. The remaining 80 % is held by the bank. In the event of default, recoveries are allocated proportionally after the NBFC’s first‑loss portion is exhausted. The model also permits the NBFC to service the loan on behalf of the bank, subject to a service‑level agreement and periodic audit by the bank’s internal audit function.

Reporting and supervisory oversight require the bank to report co‑lended exposures separately in its quarterly returns to the RBI (Form C‑1 for priority sector, Form C‑2 for non‑priority). The NBFC must disclose the co‑lending portfolio in its quarterly statutory returns to the RBI (DNBS‑04) and to its board. Both entities must maintain a joint loan‑level database accessible to the RBI for supervisory reviews. The 2022 revision added a requirement for a quarterly “Co‑Lending Health Report” covering asset quality, concentration limits, and compliance with the 20 % first‑loss rule.

Prudential safeguards include a cap on the bank’s total co‑lending exposure at 5 % of its Tier I capital, and a limit on the NBFC’s co‑lending book at 10 % of its net owned funds. The RBI also mandates that the co‑lending arrangement be approved by the respective boards and that the NBFC’s credit‑rating does not fall below investment grade. Violations attract supervisory action, including restriction on fresh co‑lending sanctions.

The CLM has been instrumental in expanding credit to micro‑enterprises, affordable housing and electric‑vehicle financing. Examination questions frequently ask candidates to compute the bank’s capital charge under the 80:20 split, identify the reporting forms, or explain the rationale for the first‑loss requirement. A clear grasp of the risk‑sharing ratio, the distinct reporting templates, and the prudential caps is essential for high‑scoring answers.

SEBI's Framework for Perpetual Bonds and Additional Tier 1 Capital Instruments: Issuance Conditions, Coupon Reset and Loss‑Absorption Features

Perpetual bonds and Additional Tier 1 (AT1) capital instruments constitute the highest‑risk tier of regulatory capital for Indian banks, governed jointly by the RBI’s Master Circular on Basel III Capital Regulations (2015, updated 2021) and SEBI’s Issue and Listing of Non‑Convertible Securities Regulations, 2021. These instruments have no fixed maturity, feature a discretionary coupon, and incorporate explicit loss‑absorption triggers linked to the issuer’s Common Equity Tier 1 (CET1) ratio.

Issuance conditions require the issuer to be a scheduled commercial bank with a minimum CET1 ratio of 8 % (including the capital conservation buffer) at the time of issuance. The instrument must be unsecured, subordinated to all other creditors, and rank senior only to equity. The minimum issue size is ₹500 crore, and at least 25 % of the issue must be subscribed by QIBs. SEBI mandates a public issue or a private placement on the electronic book‑building platform (EBBP) with a minimum of 10 % allotment to retail investors for public issues.

Coupon mechanism is discretionary: the bank may skip coupon payments without constituting an event of default, provided its CET1 ratio remains above the regulatory minimum (including buffers). The coupon is typically reset every five years based on a benchmark (e.g., 5‑year G‑sec yield) plus a spread capped at 300 bps. The reset formula must be disclosed in the offer document and cannot be altered without holder approval.

Loss‑absorption features are the defining characteristic. If the bank’s CET1 ratio falls below 6.125 % (the “trigger level”), the instrument is either written down (principal reduction) or converted into common equity at a pre‑specified conversion price. The write‑down or conversion must be automatic, irreversible, and occur within 30 days of the trigger breach. The offer document must specify the exact trigger, the write‑down percentage (usually 100 % of principal) and the conversion mechanics. The RBI’s 2021 amendment aligned the trigger with the Basel III Pillar 1 requirement and clarified that the write‑down does not constitute a default under the Companies Act.

Listing and disclosure require the instrument to be listed on a recognised stock exchange within 30 days of allotment. Continuous disclosure obligations include quarterly CET1 ratio reporting, coupon payment status, and any regulatory action affecting capital adequacy. SEBI’s 2022 circular introduced a “stress‑test disclosure” annexure where the issuer must publish the projected CET1 trajectory under adverse scenarios for the next three years.

As of December 2023, Indian banks have raised approximately ₹1.2 lakh crore through AT1 instruments, with the State Bank of India, HDFC Bank and ICICI Bank being the largest issuers. The market prices these bonds at a significant spread over comparable government securities, reflecting the discretionary coupon and loss‑absorption risk. Examination focus areas include the distinction between write‑down and conversion, the CET1 trigger thresholds, the coupon reset cap, and the interplay between RBI’s capital adequacy framework and SEBI’s listing requirements.

  • Confusing the FSDC’s advisory role with statutory rule‑making authority — it cannot issue binding regulations.
  • Mixing up ECB Track I and Track II hedging thresholds — Track I requires 70 % hedging only for tenors above five years, Track II mandates 100 % for the same tenor.
  • Assuming the Innovators Growth Platform exempts issuers from quarterly financial reporting — they must still file quarterly results and a half‑yearly Innovation Update.
  • Overlooking the NBFC’s mandatory 20 % first‑loss retention in the Co‑Lending Model — this is a supervisory requirement, not optional.
  • Treating AT1 perpetual bonds as having a fixed maturity or mandatory coupon — both are discretionary and non‑cumulative.
  • Believing the CET1 trigger for AT1 write‑down is 5.5 % — the correct Basel III‑aligned trigger in India is 6.125 %.

SEBI’s Oversight of Proxy Advisory Firms – Registration, Code of Conduct and Conflict‑of‑Interest Safeguards

Proxy advisory firms influence institutional voting outcomes across listed companies, yet they operate without a direct fiduciary duty to shareholders. SEBI brought them under a dedicated regulatory regime through the SEBI (Proxy Advisers) Regulations, 2014, subsequently amended in 2020 and 2022. Any entity providing voting recommendations to institutional investors must obtain a certificate of registration, maintain a minimum net worth of ₹50 crore, and appoint a compliance officer who reports directly to the board. The code of conduct mandates that recommendations be based on a transparent methodology disclosed on the firm’s website, and that any material change to the methodology be communicated to clients at least 30 days before implementation. Conflict‑of‑interest provisions prohibit a proxy adviser from providing consulting services to the same issuer whose resolutions it evaluates, and require a Chinese‑wall separation between research and advisory arms. The 2022 amendment introduced a “cooling‑off” period of 12 months for key personnel moving between a proxy adviser and an issuer, and mandated quarterly reporting of the number of recommendations issued, the percentage of “for” votes, and any dissenting opinions. SEBI’s enforcement actions have focused on inadequate disclosure of methodology, failure to update policies after regulatory changes, and breaches of the Chinese‑wall requirement. Examination questions often ask candidates to identify the net‑worth threshold, the cooling‑off period, or the specific disclosure items that must appear in the quarterly report.

RBI’s Guidelines on Outsourcing of Financial Services by Banks and NBFCs – Risk Management, Contractual Safeguards and Supervisory Reporting

The RBI’s “Guidelines on Managing Risks and Code of Conduct in Outsourcing of Financial Services” (2015, revised 2021) apply to all scheduled commercial banks, small finance banks, payment banks and NBFCs in the Upper and Middle layers of the Scale‑Based Regulation framework. The guidelines classify outsourcing arrangements into “material” and “non‑material” based on the impact on the entity’s core banking functions, customer data confidentiality, and regulatory compliance. For material outsourcing, the board must approve a comprehensive outsourcing policy that covers vendor due‑diligence, service‑level agreements (SLAs), data‑localisation requirements, business‑continuity planning, and exit‑strategy clauses. Contracts must include a right‑to‑audit clause allowing the regulated entity and the RBI to inspect the vendor’s books, systems and premises. The regulated entity remains fully liable for the outsourced activity; any breach of the SLA or data‑privacy incident must be reported to the RBI within 24 hours. The 2021 revision added a mandatory “concentration risk” metric: no single vendor may account for more than 30 % of the total outsourced spend for material activities, and the entity must submit a half‑yearly outsourcing return (Form OS‑1) detailing vendor‑wise spend, risk ratings, and audit findings. Supervisory colleges evaluate the outsourcing framework during the annual supervisory review process (SRP), and deficiencies can trigger a supervisory action plan or a restriction on new outsourcing contracts. Candidates should be able to distinguish material from non‑material outsourcing, cite the 30 % concentration cap, and explain the reporting timeline for incidents.

Examiner's twist
A bank outsources its core‑banking‑system (CBS) maintenance to a cloud‑service provider located in a jurisdiction that does not have a data‑localisation agreement with India. The contract contains a right‑to‑audit clause but the provider refuses on‑site inspection citing local law. The bank argues that the provider’s ISO 27001 certification satisfies the RBI’s due‑diligence requirement. Is the bank compliant with the RBI outsourcing guidelines?
No. The CBS maintenance is a material outsourcing activity because it directly affects core banking functions and customer data. The RBI guidelines require (i) data localisation – the provider must store and process Indian customer data within India or in a jurisdiction with an adequacy determination; (ii) an enforceable right‑to‑audit clause that cannot be overridden by foreign law; and (iii) the regulated entity’s board‑approved outsourcing policy must explicitly address cross‑border legal impediments. ISO 27001 certification alone does not substitute for the contractual and jurisdictional safeguards. The bank must either renegotiate the contract to include a legally binding audit right and data‑localisation commitment, or terminate the arrangement and report the breach to the RBI within 24 hours.
Exam‑day strategy
When a question mixes outsourcing with data‑localisation or cloud‑jurisdiction issues, first classify the activity as material or non‑material. Then apply the three‑pillar test: (1) board‑approved policy, (2) contractual safeguards (audit right, exit clause, SLA), (3) regulatory reporting (24‑hour incident, half‑yearly OS‑1). Missing any pillar makes the arrangement non‑compliant.

RBI’s Framework for Interest Rate Risk in the Banking Book (IRRBB) – Standardised Approach, Gap Analysis and Supervisory Review

Interest Rate Risk in the Banking Book arises from mismatches in the repricing maturity of assets, liabilities and off‑balance‑sheet items. The RBI’s “Guidelines on Management of Interest Rate Risk in the Banking Book” (2016, updated 2022) require banks to adopt a two‑track approach: a standardised measurement framework for regulatory capital and an internal model for economic‑value‑of‑equity (EVE) and earnings‑at‑risk (EaR) assessment. Under the standardised approach, banks must compute the change in EVE for six prescribed interest‑rate shock scenarios (parallel up/down 200 bps, short‑rate up/down 250 bps, long‑rate up/down 150 bps) and report the worst‑case EVE decline as a percentage of Tier‑1 capital. The gap analysis requires a monthly repricing gap schedule across ten time‑bands (overnight to over 10 years) and the calculation of the “duration gap” for each band. Banks must also estimate the impact on net interest income (NII) over a 12‑month horizon under a 200 bps parallel shift. The internal model, subject to RBI validation, must incorporate behavioural assumptions for non‑maturity deposits, prepayment options on loans, and embedded options in bonds. The supervisory review process (SRP) evaluates the bank’s IRRBB governance – board oversight, independent risk‑management function, stress‑testing frequency (at least quarterly), and the adequacy of capital buffers relative to the worst‑case EVE shock. The 2022 update introduced a “capital add‑on” trigger: if the worst‑case EVE decline exceeds 15 % of Tier‑1 capital, the bank must hold additional capital equal to the excess, subject to a floor of 0.5 % of risk‑weighted assets. Examination focus includes the six shock scenarios, the distinction between EVE and EaR, the behavioural assumptions for non‑maturity deposits, and the capital add‑on mechanics.

SEBI’s Governance Framework for Market Infrastructure Institutions (MIIs) – Stock Exchanges, Clearing Corporations and Depositories

Market Infrastructure Institutions – recognised stock exchanges, clearing corporations and depositories – are regulated under the SEBI (Stock Exchanges and Clearing Corporations) Regulations, 2018 and the SEBI (Depositories and Participants) Regulations, 2018. The governance architecture mandates that at least 51 % of the board comprise independent directors, with a maximum tenure of two consecutive terms of five years each. The chairperson must be a non‑executive independent director, and the managing director/CEO cannot be a promoter or a substantial shareholder (holding >5 % voting rights). Public shareholding in a stock exchange must be at least 25 % within three years of recognition, and the exchange must achieve demutualisation – separation of ownership, management and trading rights – within five years. Clearing corporations are required to maintain a minimum net worth of ₹300 crore and a Settlement Guarantee Fund (SGF) sized at 1.5 times the average daily settlement value, with contributions from members proportionate to their risk exposure. Depositories must hold a minimum net worth of ₹100 crore and maintain a “Investor Protection Fund” funded by 0.01 % of the daily turnover. The 2021 amendment introduced a “fit‑and‑proper” test for key managerial personnel, mandatory annual cyber‑resilience audits, and a requirement that MIIs disclose their risk‑management framework, default‑waterfall procedures and stress‑test results on their websites. SEBI’s supervisory colleges conduct on‑site inspections every 18 months, focusing on governance compliance, SGF adequacy, and business‑continuity planning. Candidates should be able to cite the independent‑director threshold, the demutualisation timeline, the SGF sizing formula, and the cyber‑audit frequency.

SEBI’s Regulation of Mutual Fund Distributors – Commission Structure, Trail‑Commission Ban and Investor Protection

Mutual fund distributors (MFDs) – including banks, broker‑dealers, independent financial advisors and online platforms – are governed by the SEBI (Mutual Funds) Regulations, 1996 read with the SEBI (Mutual Fund Distributors) Regulations, 2020. The 2020 framework abolished upfront commissions for all schemes and capped the total expense ratio (TER) at 2.25 % for equity‑oriented schemes and 2.00 % for debt‑oriented schemes, inclusive of distribution costs. Trail commission – a recurring payment based on assets under management – was prohibited for direct plans and limited to 0.50 % per annum for regular plans, payable only from the scheme’s TER. Distributors must register with SEBI, maintain a minimum net worth of ₹50 lakh, and adhere to a code of conduct that mandates suitability assessment, risk profiling, and disclosure of all fees and conflicts of interest. The “Know Your Distributor” (KYD) norms require verification of the distributor’s credentials, past disciplinary history, and compliance with the Investor Grievance Redressal Mechanism (IGRM). SEBI’s 2022 circular introduced a “distribution‑cost transparency” dashboard on the Association of Mutual Funds in India (AMFI) website, showing scheme‑wise TER breakdown, trail‑commission rates, and distributor‑wise asset flows. Non‑compliance attracts monetary penalties up to ₹1 crore, suspension of registration, and disgorgement of illicit commissions. Examination questions often test the distinction between upfront and trail commission, the TER caps for different scheme categories, the net‑worth requirement for registration, and the penalties for violating the trail‑commission ban.

  • Confusing the proxy adviser’s net‑worth requirement (₹50 crore) with the mutual fund distributor’s net‑worth requirement (₹50 lakh).
  • Assuming that material outsourcing concentration risk is measured by number of vendors rather than spend‑share (30 % cap on outsourced spend).
  • Mixing the six IRRBB shock scenarios with the Basel III interest‑rate risk in the trading book – the banking book uses EVE/EaR, not VaR.
  • Believing that a stock exchange can retain a promoter‑controlled board indefinitely – the 51 % independent‑director rule and demutualisation timeline are mandatory.
  • Thinking the trail‑commission ban applies to direct plans only – it also caps regular‑plan trail at 0.50 % and requires disclosure in the TER breakdown.
  • Overlooking the 24‑hour incident‑reporting obligation for outsourced data‑privacy breaches – the half‑yearly OS‑1 return is separate and does not replace immediate reporting.
  • Treating the SGF sizing formula (1.5 × average daily settlement value) as a static figure – it must be recalibrated quarterly based on actual settlement volumes.

SEBI's Business Responsibility and Sustainability Reporting: Core Indicators, Assurance Standards and Integration with Global Frameworks

The Business Responsibility and Sustainability Reporting (BRSR) framework, notified under the SEBI (Listing Obligations and Disclosure Requirements) Regulations, 2015, replaced the earlier Business Responsibility Report (BRR) from the financial year 2022‑23. It applies mandatorily to the top 1 000 listed entities by market capitalisation and voluntarily to others.
