The framework is built around nine principles derived from the National Guidelines on Responsible Business Conduct (NGRBC) and maps each principle to a set of core indicators — 117 in total — covering governance, environment, social and stakeholder engagement dimensions. Companies must disclose quantitative metrics such as greenhouse‑gas emissions (Scope 1, 2 and 3), water withdrawal, waste generated, employee diversity ratios, and board‑level ESG oversight.

Assurance is a critical differentiator. From FY 2023‑24, the top 250 listed entities are required to obtain external assurance on their BRSR disclosures in accordance with ISAE 3000 (Revised) or equivalent standards such as the AA1000 Assurance Standard. The assurance report must be attached to the annual report and uploaded on the company’s website. SEBI has also prescribed a “reasonable assurance” level for the core indicators, while “limited assurance” suffices for the optional leadership indicators. This tiered approach mirrors the global practice under the Global Reporting Initiative (GRI) and the Sustainability Accounting Standards Board (SASB), enabling cross‑border comparability.

Integration with global frameworks is explicit. The BRSR core indicators are aligned with the Task Force on Climate‑Related Financial Disclosures (TCFD) recommendations — governance, strategy, risk management, and metrics & targets — and with the United Nations Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs). SEBI’s 2022 circular on “ESG Disclosures” mandated that companies mapping their BRSR data to TCFD pillars must disclose the mapping table in the annual report. This dual‑reporting requirement reduces duplication for firms that already publish GRI or SASB reports and signals to international investors that Indian disclosures meet global benchmarks.

Chronologically, the evolution reads: 2012 – BRR introduced for top 100; 2015 – LODR Regulations codify BRR; 2021 – BRSR notification (SEBI/LAD‑NRO/GN/2021/23); 2022 – mandatory for top 1 000; 2023 – assurance mandate for top 250; 2024 – proposed extension of assurance to top 500. Each step was accompanied by a 90‑day transition window and a dedicated FAQ repository on the SEBI website. The cause‑effect chain is clear: investor demand for comparable ESG data → regulatory upgrade → assurance requirement → higher data reliability → improved capital allocation toward sustainable enterprises.

Examiner twists often centre on three confusions: (i) treating BRSR as a voluntary “best‑practice” rather than a mandatory filing for the prescribed universe; (ii) mixing up the assurance levels — reasonable versus limited — and the applicable entity thresholds; (iii) assuming that alignment with TCFD is optional, whereas the mapping disclosure is compulsory for the top 1 000. A precise answer must cite the 2021 notification number, the 250‑entity assurance threshold, and the ISAE 3000 reference.

Real Estate Investment Trusts and Infrastructure Investment Trusts: Valuation Methodology, Distribution Policy and Tax Treatment

REITs and InvITs, governed by the SEBI (Real Estate Investment Trusts) Regulations, 2014 and the SEBI (Infrastructure Investment Trusts) Regulations, 2014 respectively, share a common valuation architecture but differ in asset‑class specifics. Both structures require a half‑yearly independent valuation by a SEBI‑registered valuer. The valuation must follow the “fair value” principle using the discounted cash‑flow (DCF) method for income‑producing assets and the comparable‑transactions method for development assets. The valuer’s report, including key assumptions — discount rate, terminal cap rate, occupancy projections — is filed with the stock exchanges and made available to unitholders within 30 days of the valuation date.

Distribution policy is a statutory mandate: at least 90 % of net distributable cash flows (NDCF) must be distributed to unitholders at least once every six months. NDCF is defined as cash generated from operations less capital expenditure, debt servicing, and statutory reserves. For REITs, rental income and capital gains from property sales constitute the primary cash streams; for InvITs, toll‑road revenues, power‑purchase‑agreement receipts, and concession‑fee income dominate. The 2020 amendment introduced a “distribution waterfall” that prioritises senior debt service, then operating expenses, before the 90 % payout, ensuring that leverage covenants are not breached.

Tax treatment underwent a landmark shift with the Finance Act, 2020, which abolished the Dividend Distribution Tax (DDT) and moved to a classical system. Consequently, distributions from REITs and InvITs are now taxed in the hands of unitholders at their applicable slab rates. However, interest component of the distribution (for InvITs) enjoys a concessional 10 % withholding tax for non‑resident unitholders under the India‑Mauritius tax treaty, while the dividend component is taxed at 20 % (plus surcharge and cess). Capital gains on unit transfers are treated as long‑term if held beyond 36 months, attracting 10 % tax without indexation; short‑term gains are taxed at slab rates.

The 2021 amendment permitted “strategic investors” to hold up to 25 % in a REIT/InvIT without triggering a change‑in‑control provision, and introduced a “green shoe” option for initial public offers to stabilise post‑listing prices. The 2022 circular on “Valuation Disclosure Enhancements” mandated that the valuer’s sensitivity analysis — showing NDCF impact of a 100‑basis‑point change in discount rate — be published alongside the valuation report.

Examiner traps: (i) confusing the 90 % NDCF rule with a 90 % of net profit rule; (ii) assuming the same tax rate applies to both interest and dividend components of InvIT distributions; (iii) overlooking the mandatory half‑yearly valuation frequency and the 30‑day filing deadline; (iv) treating the green‑shoe option as a permanent feature rather than an IPO‑specific mechanism.

Application Scenario
A listed REIT with a portfolio of commercial office spaces reports a half‑yearly NDCF of ₹500 crore. Its senior debt service for the period is ₹120 crore, operating expenses ₹80 crore, and it maintains a statutory reserve of ₹30 crore. The REIT proposes to distribute ₹300 crore to unitholders. Determine whether the distribution complies with the SEBI mandate and calculate the maximum permissible distribution.
NDCF = ₹500 crore. Mandatory deductions: senior debt service ₹120 crore + operating expenses ₹80 crore + statutory reserve ₹30 crore = ₹230 crore. Distributable cash flow = NDCF – mandatory deductions = ₹500 crore – ₹230 crore = ₹270 crore. The 90 % rule applies to distributable cash flow, so minimum required distribution = 0.90 × ₹270 crore = ₹243 crore. The proposed ₹300 crore exceeds the minimum and is permissible provided it does not exceed the distributable cash flow. Since ₹300 crore > ₹270 crore, the REIT cannot distribute ₹300 crore; the maximum permissible distribution is ₹270 crore. Therefore, the proposal is non‑compliant; the REIT must cap distribution at ₹270 crore.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a question asks for “maximum permissible distribution”, always compute NDCF, subtract all mandatory outflows (debt service, opex, reserves), then apply the 90 % floor to the resultant distributable cash flow. The answer is the lower of (a) the proposed amount and (b) the distributable cash flow. Remember that the 90 % rule is a floor, not a ceiling; the ceiling is the distributable cash flow itself.

Alternative Investment Funds: Category‑Specific Leverage Limits, Reporting Obligations and Tax Pass‑Through Mechanics

The SEBI (Alternative Investment Funds) Regulations, 2012 classify AIFs into three categories, each with distinct leverage ceilings, reporting cadences and tax regimes. Category I AIFs — venture capital funds, SME funds, social impact funds — are prohibited from employing any leverage; they may only invest through equity or equity‑linked instruments. Category II AIFs — private equity funds, debt funds, fund‑of‑funds — may borrow up to two times their net asset value (NAV) subject to a maximum of 20 % of the fund’s corpus being deployed in a single investee company. Category III AIFs — hedge funds, PIPE funds — enjoy the highest leverage, capped at five times NAV, with a single‑investee exposure limit of 10 % of corpus.

Reporting obligations are calibrated to risk. All AIFs must submit a monthly NAV statement to the custodian and a quarterly portfolio disclosure to SEBI within 21 days of quarter‑end. Category III AIFs, given their higher leverage, are additionally required to file a monthly risk‑metrics report — Value‑at‑Risk (VaR) at 99 % confidence, stress‑test results, and counterparty exposure concentration — within 10 business days. An annual audit by a SEBI‑registered auditor, accompanied by a compliance certificate from the designated partner, is mandatory for every category.

Tax pass‑through status is the most examined differentiator. Category I and Category II AIFs enjoy pass‑through treatment under Section 115UB of the Income‑Tax Act, 1961: the fund’s income is taxed in the hands of investors, and the fund itself is exempt from tax on distributed income. Category III AIFs, however, are taxed at the fund level at the maximum marginal rate (30 % plus surcharge and cess) on their total income, and distributions to investors are treated as dividend income subject to further tax. The Finance Act, 2021 extended the pass‑through benefit to Category II “debt‑oriented” AIFs provided they meet the “qualified investor” threshold (minimum investment ₹1 crore per investor).

Chronology of key amendments: 2012 – original regulations; 2015 – introduction of Category III leverage cap of 5×; 2018 – mandatory monthly risk‑metrics for Category III; 2020 – pass‑through extension to Category II debt funds; 2022 – enhanced disclosure of side‑pocketing arrangements and valuation methodology for illiquid assets. Each amendment was preceded by a consultation paper and a 60‑day comment period, reflecting SEBI’s iterative rule‑making approach.

Examiner twists: (i) confusing the 2× leverage limit for Category II with the 5× limit for Category III; (ii) assuming all AIFs enjoy pass‑through tax status; (iii) overlooking the monthly risk‑metrics filing deadline (10 business days) for Category III; (iv) misstating the single‑investee exposure limit for Category II (20 % of corpus) versus Category III (10 %).

Securities Lending and Borrowing Mechanism: Operational Workflow, Collateral Haircuts and Counterparty Risk Controls

The Securities Lending and Borrowing (SLB) framework, operationalised under the SEBI (Securities Lending) Regulations, 2018, enables institutional holders — mutual funds, insurance companies, pension funds — to lend idle securities to market participants such as market makers, short sellers, and arbitrageurs. The transaction is routed through an approved intermediary, typically a clearing corporation (CC), which acts as the central counterparty (CCP) and guarantees settlement.

Operational workflow: (1) Lender instructs its custodian to transfer securities to the CC’s lending pool. (2) Borrower submits a borrowing request specifying quantity, tenure (overnight to 12 months), and collateral type. (3) CC matches lenders and borrowers, applies haircuts, and effects simultaneous transfer of securities to borrower and collateral to lender. (4) Daily mark‑to‑market (MTM) of both securities and collateral is performed; margin calls are issued if collateral value falls below the required threshold. (5) On maturity or early recall, securities are returned to lender, collateral released to borrower.

Collateral haircuts are prescribed to absorb price volatility. For cash collateral, a 102 % haircut (i.e., borrower must post cash equal to 102 % of the market value of borrowed securities) applies. For government securities (G‑Secs) collateral, the haircut is 105 % of the market value. Corporate bonds accepted as collateral attract a 110 % haircut, reflecting higher credit and liquidity risk. The CC may impose higher haircuts during periods of heightened market stress, as observed during the March 2020 COVID‑19 sell‑off when cash haircuts were temporarily raised to 105 %.

Counterparty risk controls are layered. The CC maintains a Settlement Guarantee Fund (SGF) sized at 1.5 × the average daily settlement value of SLB transactions. Default waterfall: (a) borrower’s collateral, (b) lender’s margin, (c) CC’s own capital, (d) SGF contributions from members proportionate to their SLB exposure. The CC conducts daily stress tests simulating a 30 % drop in equity prices and a 200‑basis‑point rise in yields to assess SGF adequacy. Results are reported to SEBI quarterly.

Key regulatory milestones: 2018 – SLB Regulations notified; 2019 – introduction of “automated lending” platform on NSE and BSE; 2020 – temporary haircut escalation during pandemic; 2021 – mandatory reporting of SLB positions in the monthly “SLB‑01” return; 2022 – extension of SLB eligibility to exchange‑traded funds (ETFs) and real‑estate investment trust units.

Examiner traps: (i) confusing SLB with repo — repo involves sale and repurchase of securities with a fixed repurchase price, whereas SLB transfers legal title temporarily without a repurchase obligation; (ii) misremembering haircut percentages — cash 102 %, G‑Secs 105 %, corporate bonds 110 %; (iii) assuming the SGF is static — it is recalibrated quarterly based on actual settlement volumes; (iv) overlooking the 10‑business‑day reporting deadline for Category III AIF risk metrics, which is unrelated but often mixed up in composite questions.

Market Making in Corporate Bonds: Quote Obligations, Incentive Structure and Performance Evaluation Metrics

SEBI’s market‑maker framework for the corporate bond segment, introduced via circular SEBI/HO/MRD/DP/CIR/P/2021/00000001 dated 15 January 2021 and refined in 2022, aims to deepen secondary‑market liquidity. A designated market maker (DMM) must be a SEBI‑registered broker‑dealer or bank with a minimum net worth of ₹100 crore and a clean disciplinary record for the preceding three years.

Quote obligations: The DMM must provide continuous two‑way quotes for a minimum of five eligible corporate bonds (rated AA‑ or above) during market hours. Minimum quote size is ₹5 crore per side; the maximum permissible spread is 0.5 % of the mid‑price for bonds with residual maturity ≤ 5 years and 0.75 % for longer tenors. Quotes must be refreshed at least every 30 seconds, and the DMM must maintain a quote‑presence ratio of ≥ 90 % of trading hours each day.

Incentive structure: The exchange grants a transaction‑charge rebate of 0.005 % of the traded value for each bond where the DMM meets the quote‑presence and spread criteria. Additionally, DMMs receive priority allocation in primary issuances of the same issuer’s bonds, subject to a cap of 10 % of the issue size. The rebate is credited monthly and is contingent on the DMM’s fill‑rate — the proportion of quoted volume that results in executed trades — being at least 30 %.

Portfolio Management Services: SEBI (Portfolio Managers) Regulations, 2020 and Subsequent Amendments

The SEBI (Portfolio Managers) Regulations, 2020 replaced the 1993 framework and introduced a tighter registration, net‑worth and disclosure regime for discretionary and non‑discretionary portfolio managers (PMs). A PM must be a body corporate with a minimum net worth of ₹5 crore (₹10 crore for discretionary PMs) and a clean track record of three years. The regulations cap the total fee chargeable to a client at 2.5 % per annum of assets under management (AUM) for discretionary services and 1.5 % for non‑discretionary services, inclusive of all expenses. PMs are required to furnish a quarterly portfolio statement to each client, a half‑yearly compliance certificate to SEBI, and an annual audit report by a practicing chartered accountant. The 2022 amendment introduced a “fee‑only” model for advisory‑only PMs, removed the performance‑fee component for non‑discretionary mandates, and mandated that all client assets be held in a segregated demat account with a SEBI‑registered custodian. The 2023 circular on “Enhanced Investor Protection” requires PMs to disclose conflict‑of‑interest policies, provide a standardized risk‑profiling questionnaire, and maintain a grievance redressal mechanism with a 30‑day resolution timeline. Examiner traps: (i) confusing the fee caps with those applicable to Alternative Investment Funds (AIFs); (ii) assuming the net‑worth requirement is uniform across discretionary and non‑discretionary categories; (iii) overlooking the mandatory custodial segregation introduced in 2022.

Custodial Services and Depository Participant Framework: SEBI (Custodian of Securities) Regulations, 1996 and 2021 Amendments

Custodians in India operate under the SEBI (Custodian of Securities) Regulations, 1996, substantially amended in 2021 to align with the Basel Committee’s “Principles for Financial Market Infrastructures”. A custodian must be a body corporate with a minimum net worth of ₹50 crore, maintain a segregation of client securities from proprietary holdings, and hold a valid certificate of registration from SEBI. The 2021 amendment introduced a risk‑based supervisory framework: custodians are classified into Tier‑I (systemically important) and Tier‑II based on assets under custody (AUC) exceeding ₹1 lakh crore. Tier‑I custodians must submit a monthly risk‑management report, conduct an annual internal audit of settlement processes, and maintain a Settlement Guarantee Fund (SGF) contribution of 0.02 % of AUC. Depository Participants (DPs) — the interface between investors and the two depositories (NSDL and CDSL) — are governed by the SEBI (Depositories and Participants) Regulations, 2018. DPs must maintain a net worth of ₹3 crore, adhere to a “Know Your Client” (KYC) protocol aligned with the Prevention of Money Laundering Act, and report daily transaction data to the depository. The 2022 circular on “Data Localization for Custodians and DPs” mandates that all client‑level transaction logs be stored on servers located in India for a minimum of five years. Examiner traps: (i) mixing the net‑worth thresholds for custodians (₹50 crore) and DPs (₹3 crore); (ii) forgetting the Tier‑I SGF contribution rate; (iii) assuming data‑localization applies only to payment system operators and not to custodians/DPs.

Examiner's trap
A question asks: “Which of the following statements about SEBI’s Portfolio Managers Regulations is correct? (a) The fee cap for discretionary PMs is 1.5 % of AUM. (b) Non‑discretionary PMs may charge a performance fee up to 20 % of profits. (c) All client assets must be held in a segregated demat account with a SEBI‑registered custodian. (d) The minimum net worth for a discretionary PM is ₹5 crore.”
Correct answer: (c). The 2022 amendment mandates custodial segregation for all PM client assets. Option (a) is wrong — the discretionary cap is 2.5 %. Option (b) is wrong — performance fees are prohibited for non‑discretionary mandates after the 2022 amendment. Option (d) is wrong — the discretionary net‑worth requirement is ₹10 crore, not ₹5 crore.
Exam‑day strategy
When a question cites a specific year (e.g., “2022 amendment”), immediately recall the key change introduced that year — fee‑only model, custodial segregation, or risk‑based custodian tiers. Keep a one‑page cheat sheet mapping year → major amendment for PMs, custodians, DPs, and commodity derivatives; it saves precious seconds in the mains answer‑writing phase.

Stock Exchange Governance and Self‑Regulatory Organisation Status: SEBI (Stock Exchanges and Clearing Corporations) Regulations, 2018

The SEBI (Stock Exchanges and Clearing Corporations) Regulations, 2018, codified the governance architecture for recognised stock exchanges (NSE, BSE, Metropolitan Stock Exchange) and clearing corporations (NSE Clearing, ICCL). Each exchange must have a governing board with a minimum of 50 % independent directors, a separate audit committee, and a risk‑management committee chaired by an independent director. The Managing Director/CEO is appointed for a five‑year term, subject to SEBI approval, and must not hold any other executive position. The 2020 amendment introduced a “Technology Risk Management Framework” requiring exchanges to conduct quarterly vulnerability assessments, maintain a Business Continuity Plan (BCP) tested annually, and report cyber‑incidents to SEBI within six hours. In 2022, SEBI mandated that exchanges publish a “Market Integrity Report” semi‑annually, detailing surveillance alerts, order‑to‑trade ratios, and disciplinary actions. The self‑regulatory organisation (SRO) status grants exchanges the power to frame bye‑laws, impose fines up to ₹25 lakh per violation, and suspend trading members, subject to SEBI’s appellate oversight. Examiner traps: (i) confusing the independent‑director quota for exchanges (50 %) with that for listed companies (one‑third); (ii) assuming the SRO fine ceiling is ₹1 crore; (iii) overlooking the six‑hour cyber‑incident reporting window introduced in 2020.

Commodity Derivatives Market Regulation: SEBI (Commodity Derivatives) Regulations, 2018 and 2022 Amendments

Following the merger of the Forward Markets Commission (FMC) into SEBI in September 2015, the SEBI (Commodity Derivatives) Regulations, 2018, established a unified regulatory regime for commodity futures and options. The regulations prescribe position limits: for non‑agricultural commodities, the client‑level limit is 5 % of open interest or ₹500 crore, whichever is lower; for agricultural commodities, the limit is 2 % of open interest or ₹200 crore. Margins are computed using the SPAN methodology with a minimum initial margin of 5 % for non‑agri and 10 % for agri contracts. Warehouse receipts issued by accredited warehouses serve as deliverable instruments; the 2021 circular mandated electronic negotiable warehouse receipts (e‑NWRs) on the e‑NAM platform. The 2022 amendment introduced a “Market Maker” framework for illiquid agri contracts, requiring designated market makers to provide two‑way quotes with a maximum spread of 1 % of the mid‑price and a minimum quote size of ₹10 lakh. Additionally, the amendment allowed foreign portfolio investors (FPIs) to participate in commodity derivatives subject to a 10 % aggregate position limit across all exchanges. Examiner traps: (i) mixing the position‑limit percentages for agri vs non‑agri commodities; (ii) assuming the SPAN margin floor is uniform across all commodities; (iii) forgetting the 2022 market‑maker spread cap of 1 % for agri contracts.

Qualified Institutional Placement: Pricing, Lock‑in and Recent Liberalisation under ICDR Regulations

Qualified Institutional Placement (QIP) is governed by Chapter VIII of the SEBI (Issue of Capital and Disclosure Requirements) Regulations, 2018 (ICDR). Only listed companies with a minimum average market capitalisation of ₹500 crore over the preceding 26 weeks are eligible. The issue price must be at least the higher of (a) the average of the weekly high‑low of the volume‑weighted average price (VWAP) for the two weeks preceding the relevant date, and (b) the floor price determined by the book‑building process. A minimum of 10 % of the issue size must be allotted to mutual funds, and the allotment to any single QIB cannot exceed 25 % of the issue. The 2021 amendment relaxed the pricing formula by allowing a discount of up to 5 % on the floor price, provided the issuer obtains a special resolution from shareholders. The lock‑in period for QIP allottees is one year from the date of allotment, except for mutual funds and insurance companies which are exempt. The 2023 circular on “Enhanced Disclosure for QIP” requires the issuer to disclose the rationale for the discount, the impact on earnings per share, and a post‑issue shareholding pattern within 48 hours of allotment. Examiner traps: (i) confusing the QIP floor‑price calculation with the preferential allotment pricing under Section 62(1)(c) of the Companies Act; (ii) assuming the lock‑in applies uniformly to all QIB categories; (iii) overlooking the 5 % discount ceiling introduced in 2021.

  • Mixing the fee caps for Portfolio Managers (2.5 % discretionary, 1.5 % non‑discretionary) with AIF management fees.
  • Confusing custodian net‑worth (₹50 crore) with Depository Participant net‑worth (₹3 crore).
  • Assuming the independent‑director requirement for stock exchanges is one‑third instead of 50 %.
  • Applying the non‑agri commodity position limit (5 %) to agricultural contracts.
  • Treating the QIP lock‑in as applicable to mutual funds and insurers.
  • Remember to cross‑check regulatory dates against the latest SEBI circulars before the exam.

Buyback of Securities: SEBI Regulations, Methods, Tax Implications and Recent Case Law

The SEBI (Buyback of Securities) Regulations, 2018 replaced the 1998 framework and govern all buybacks by listed companies. A buyback cannot exceed 25 % of the aggregate of paid‑up capital and free reserves as per the latest audited financial statements; for the open‑market route the ceiling is 10 %. The company must authorise the buyback through a special resolution if it exceeds 10 % of paid‑up capital and free reserves, otherwise a board resolution suffices. Three methods are permitted: (i) tender offer — fixed price, minimum 15 % of the buyback size reserved for retail shareholders (holding ≤ ₹2 lakh), completion within six months; (ii) open market through stock exchanges — market‑determined price, completion within twelve months, no retail reservation; (iii) odd‑lot buyback — for holdings smaller than marketable lot. The buyback price under the tender route is fixed upfront; under the open‑market route the maximum price is capped at the highest price paid during the buyback period. Shares bought back must be extinguished within seven days of completion and the company cannot issue fresh shares (including bonus) for six months except for ESOPs, sweat equity, or conversion of warrants.

Tax treatment underwent a fundamental shift in Finance Act 2020. Until 30 September 2019, listed companies paid additional tax at 20 % (plus surcharge and cess) on distributed income (buyback consideration minus issue price) under Section 115QA, while shareholders enjoyed exemption under Section 10(34A). From 1 October 2019, the buyback tax on listed companies was abolished; the consideration is now taxed in the hands of shareholders as capital gains — long‑term if held > 12 months (10 % above ₹1 lakh without indexation), short‑term at 15 %. Unlisted companies continue to pay the 20 % buyback tax. SEBI’s 2023 amendments introduced mandatory e‑voting for all buybacks, a cooling‑off period of six months between successive buybacks, and a requirement to disclose the daily buyback quantity and price on the exchange website. Landmark jurisprudence includes Cairn India Ltd. v. SEBI (SAT, 2015) where the tribunal upheld SEBI’s direction to cancel a buyback announced during an open offer period, ruling that the buyback frustrated the open offer’s objective. In SEBI v. DLF Ltd. (2020), the Supreme Court confirmed that buyback proceeds cannot be used to circumvent the minimum public shareholding (MPS) norms. Examiner focus: the 25 % vs 10 % ceiling distinction, the 15 % retail reservation in tender offers, the post‑2019 capital‑gains regime for listed companies, and the six‑month re‑issuance bar.

Delisting of Equity Shares: SEBI Delisting Regulations, Reverse Book Building, Exit Offers and Recent Amendments

The SEBI (Delisting of Equity Shares) Regulations, 2021 replaced the 2009 regime and apply to voluntary and compulsory delisting. For voluntary delisting by promoters, the floor price is the average of the weekly high‑low of the volume‑weighted average price (VWAP) for the 26 weeks preceding the board meeting. The discovered price through reverse book building must be ≥ floor price; if the discovered price is lower, promoters may make a counter‑offer at a price ≥ floor price, provided the post‑offer promoter holding reaches 90 % of paid‑up capital. The 90 % threshold is non‑negotiable — failure to achieve it results in the delisting offer failing and a twelve‑month cooling period before a fresh attempt. Shareholders who did not tender receive an exit offer at the same discovered/counter‑offer price for one year from the delisting date. Compulsory delisting (for non‑compliance with listing agreement) requires an independent valuer to determine fair value; the exchange appoints the valuer and the promoter must acquire shares at that price. The 2023 amendments tightened the framework: (i) for frequently traded shares, the floor price is now the higher of the 26‑week VWAP average and the 2‑week VWAP average; (ii) a cooling period of twelve months applies after a failed delisting attempt; (iii) the merchant banker must certify that the acquirer has firm financing for the full consideration. The Vedanta Ltd. delisting attempt (2020‑21) failed because the discovered price (₹87.25) was below the floor price (₹87.50) and the counter‑offer did not achieve 90 %; Hexaware Technologies (2020) succeeded at ₹475 via counter‑offer. In SEBI v. Vedanta Ltd. (SAT, 2021), the tribunal held that the reverse book building process cannot be restarted once the discovered price is declared — a fresh board resolution and shareholder approval are mandatory. Examiner traps: confusing the 90 % promoter holding requirement with the 75 % MPS threshold; assuming the exit offer price can differ from the discovered price; overlooking the 2023 revised floor‑price formula for frequently traded shares.

Worked Application: Buyback vs Delisting Price Mechanics
A listed company with 10 crore shares outstanding (face value ₹10) announces a tender‑offer buyback of 2 crore shares at ₹500 per share. The 26‑week VWAP average is ₹420. Simultaneously, the promoter group (holding 65 %) proposes voluntary delisting. The reverse book building discovers a price of ₹480. The floor price is ₹420. The promoter makes a counter‑offer at ₹490. Post‑counter‑offer, promoter holding reaches 88 %. Questions: (a) Is the buyback price compliant with SEBI regulations? (b) Will the delisting succeed? (c) What is the tax implication for a retail shareholder who bought at ₹300 and tenders in the buyback?
(a) Yes. The buyback price of ₹500 is a fixed price under the tender route; SEBI does not link it to VWAP. The 25 % ceiling (2 crore / 10 crore = 20 %) is satisfied. Retail reservation of 15 % (30 lakh shares) must be honoured. (b) No. The counter‑offer at ₹490 ≥ floor price (₹420) is valid, but promoter holding reaches only 88 %, short of the mandatory 90 %. Delisting fails; a twelve‑month cooling period applies before a fresh attempt. (c) The shareholder realizes a long‑term capital gain of ₹200 per share (₹500 − ₹300). Since the buyback is post‑October 2019, the company pays no Section 115QA tax. The shareholder pays 10 % LTCG tax on gains exceeding ₹1 lakh in the financial year (Section 112A). No STT is levied on tender‑offer buybacks.
Exam‑Day Strategy
Memorise the three buyback ceilings as a triplet: 25 % (overall), 10 % (open‑market), 15 % (retail reservation in tender). For delisting, the 90 % promoter threshold is absolute — no rounding, no waiver. The 2023 floor‑price amendment (higher of 26‑week and 2‑week VWAP) applies only to frequently traded shares; infrequently traded shares use the 26‑week average alone. In MCQs, if a buyback is announced during an open offer period, the answer is "SEBI will direct cancellation" (Cairn India precedent). For tax, post‑2019 listed buybacks = capital gains in shareholder hands; unlisted = company pays 20 % Section 115QA tax.

SEBI's Regulatory Framework for Proxy Advisors: Registration, Code of Conduct, Conflict of Interest

The SEBI (Proxy Advisors) Regulations, 2019, effective 1 January 2020, brought proxy advisory firms under a mandatory registration regime. Any entity providing voting recommendations to institutional investors must register with SEBI; the net‑worth requirement is ₹1 crore, and the principal officer must have at least five years of experience in finance, law, or corporate governance. The regulations prescribe a comprehensive Code of Conduct: (i) proxy advisors must formulate and disclose a voting policy covering all agenda items typically encountered in general meetings; (ii) research methodology, including sources of information and assumptions, must be publicly available; (iii) conflict‑of‑interest policies must address situations where the advisor or its associates provide consulting services to the subject company — Chinese walls and disclosure are mandatory; (iv) voting recommendations must be disseminated to clients at least 48 hours before the general meeting, and a copy must be provided to the company simultaneously. The 2022 circular mandated enhanced disclosures: proxy advisors must publish a quarterly report detailing the number of meetings covered, instances where recommendations differed from management proposals, and the rationale for such deviations. SEBI’s 2023 consultation paper proposed (a) a "comply‑or‑explain" framework for institutional investors to disclose whether they followed proxy advice, (b) a review mechanism for companies to challenge factually incorrect recommendations, and (c) periodic inspection of proxy advisors’ records. As of March 2024, six proxy advisors are registered: Institutional Investor Advisory Services (IiAS), Stakeholders Empowerment Services (SES), InGovern Research Services, ProxyVision, ESG Research Foundation, and CFA Institute India. The regulations do not apply to in‑house research teams of institutional investors. Examiner focus: the 48‑hour dissemination rule, the ₹1 crore net‑worth threshold, the Chinese‑wall requirement for conflicted advisory‑consulting relationships, and the distinction between registered proxy advisors and in‑house teams.

Regulatory Framework for Online Bond Platform Providers: SEBI 2022 Regulations, Investor Protection

The SEBI (Online Bond Platform Providers) Regulations, 2022, notified on 9 November 2022, created a dedicated regime for electronic platforms facilitating secondary‑market transactions in listed debt securities. An Online Bond Platform Provider (OBPP) must obtain a certificate of registration from SEBI; the eligibility criteria include a minimum net worth of ₹5 crore, a technology infrastructure capable of handling order matching, risk management, and audit trails, and a grievance redressal mechanism with a designated compliance officer. The platform can only list debt securities that are listed on a recognised stock exchange and have a credit rating of at least investment grade (BBB‑ or equivalent) at the time of listing on the platform. Primary issuances, unlisted securities, and securities rated below investment grade are prohibited. The OBPP acts as a facilitator — not a counterparty — and must ensure that all trades are settled through the existing clearing corporation and depository infrastructure (T+1 settlement cycle). Investor protection measures include: (i) mandatory KYC and CKYCR verification before onboarding; (ii) risk disclosure documents highlighting liquidity risk, credit risk, and interest‑rate risk; (iii) a cooling‑off period of one business day for retail investors to cancel orders; (iv) segregation of client funds and securities in accordance with the Securities Contracts (Regulation) Act. The OBPP cannot engage in proprietary trading, provide leverage, or offer portfolio management services. Reporting obligations: daily transaction reports to the stock exchange, monthly compliance certificates to SEBI, and immediate reporting of cyber incidents within six hours (aligned with the 2020 Technology Risk Management Framework). SEBI’s 2023 operational circular clarified that OBPPs must integrate with the RBI’s Account Aggregator framework for consent‑based financial data sharing and must participate in the SCORES grievance redressal platform. As of December 2023, four entities have received in‑principle approval: GoldenPi, BondsIndia, Wint Wealth, and TheFixedIncome. Examiner traps: confusing OBPP registration (₹5 crore net worth) with stock‑broker registration (₹2 crore); assuming OBPPs can list unlisted bonds or primary issues; overlooking the investment‑grade rating floor (BBB‑) for platform listing.

SEBI's Framework for Differential Voting Rights Shares: Issuance, Listing, Corporate Governance Safeguards

Differential Voting Rights (DVR) shares allow companies to issue equity with voting rights that differ from the standard one‑share‑one‑vote principle. The Companies Act, 2013 (Section 43) permits two classes: shares with superior voting rights (SR shares) and shares with inferior voting rights (DVR shares). SEBI’s ICDR Regulations, 2018, Chapter X, govern the issuance and listing of such shares. For SR shares (superior voting), SEBI introduced a specific framework in 2019 targeting technology‑intensive companies. Eligibility: the issuer must be a "new‑age" company with intensive R&D, technology, or intellectual property; the promoter group and pre‑IPO investors (holding ≥ 10 % each) may hold SR shares; each SR share can carry up to 10 votes. A sunset clause mandates conversion to ordinary shares upon the earlier of (a) five years from listing, or (b) the promoter’s holding falling below 25 %. DVR shares (inferior voting) have been used by Tata Motors (2008), Pantaloons (2012), and Gujarat NRE Coke (2010) — typically carrying 1/10th vote but a higher dividend (e.g., 5 % additional dividend). Listing requirements: DVR/SR shares are listed under a separate ticker; price discovery is independent. Corporate governance safeguards under LODR Regulations: (i) at least half the board must be independent directors; (ii) the audit committee must comprise only independent directors; (iii) related‑party transactions involving DVR/SR shareholders require majority‑of‑minority approval; (iv) the annual report must disclose the voting‑rights ratio, dividend differential, and conversion timeline. The 2021 amendment to ICDR Regulations extended the SR framework to companies proposing to list on the Innovators Growth Platform (IGP). In SEBI v. Tata Motors Ltd. (SAT, 2015), the tribunal upheld the DVR structure but directed enhanced disclosures on the dividend differential. Examiner focus: the 10‑vote cap for SR shares, the sunset clause triggers, the majority‑of‑minority approval for RPTs, and the separate ticker listing mechanism.

  • Confusing the buyback tax regime: assuming listed companies still pay Section 115QA tax post‑October 2019 (they do not; shareholders pay capital gains).
  • Treating the 90 % promoter threshold in delisting as a 75 % MPS‑style threshold — it is absolute and non‑waivable.
  • Applying the 48‑hour proxy‑advisor dissemination rule to in‑house research teams of mutual funds (exempt).
  • Assuming OBPPs can list unrated or below‑investment‑grade bonds (minimum BBB‑ required).
  • Believing SR shares can carry unlimited votes (capped at 10 votes per share) or that the sunset clause is optional.
  • Overlooking the 15 % retail reservation in tender‑offer buybacks — it is mandatory, not discretionary.
  • Mixing the delisting floor‑price formula: the 2023 amendment (higher of 26‑week and 2‑week VWAP) applies only to frequently traded shares.

Financial Stability and Development Council: Macroprudential Coordination, Institutional Architecture and Recent Policy Interventions

The Financial Stability and Development Council (FSDC) was constituted in December 2010 by an executive order of the Government of India, replacing the earlier High Level Coordination Committee on Financial Markets. The Council is chaired by the Union Finance Minister and includes the Governor of the Reserve Bank of India, the Chairpersons of SEBI, IRDAI, PFRDA, the Finance Secretary, the Secretary of the Department of Economic Affairs, and the Chief Economic Adviser as members. Its mandate spans macroprudential supervision, financial sector development, inter‑regulatory coordination, and financial literacy. Unlike a statutory body, the FSDC derives its authority from the executive order and the collective mandate of its constituent regulators.

The Council operates through a two‑tier structure: the apex Council meeting, held at least twice a year, and a Sub‑Committee chaired by the RBI Governor that meets more frequently to monitor systemic risks. The Sub‑Committee reviews indicators such as credit‑to‑GDP gaps, asset‑price inflation, leverage in the non‑bank financial sector, and cross‑border capital flow volatility. Its assessments feed into the Council’s policy advisories, which are non‑binding but carry significant moral suasion. In 2018 the FSDC endorsed the adoption of a counter‑cyclical capital buffer (CCyB) framework for scheduled commercial banks, aligning with Basel III guidance. The Council also recommended the creation of a resolution corporation for financial firms, a proposal that later informed the Financial Resolution and Deposit Insurance Bill, 2017 (which lapsed).

Recent interventions illustrate the Council’s evolving role. In March 2020, amid the COVID‑19 shock, the FSDC Sub‑Committee convened an emergency meeting and advised regulators to implement regulatory forbearance measures — loan moratoriums, easing of liquidity coverage ratio (LCR) requirements, and relaxation of provisioning norms for stressed assets. The Council also coordinated the launch of the Special Liquidity Facility for Mutual Funds (SLF‑MF) by the RBI, backstopped by a government guarantee, to prevent fire‑sales in the corporate bond segment. In 2022 the FSDC reviewed the implications of rising external commercial borrowings (ECBs) by non‑bank finance companies and recommended tighter end‑use restrictions and hedging requirements, which were subsequently incorporated into the RBI’s ECB framework.

A notable institutional development is the establishment of the Financial Stability Report (FSR) as a semi‑annual publication of the RBI, prepared in consultation with the FSDC. The FSR publishes the Systemic Risk Survey, the Macro‑Financial Risk Heatmap, and stress‑test results for banks and major NBFCs. The Council’s deliberations on climate‑related financial risks led to the RBI’s 2023 draft guidance on climate risk stress testing for scheduled commercial banks, marking the first explicit macroprudential linkage between environmental risk and financial stability in India.

Examiner focus: the distinction between the FSDC’s advisory nature and the statutory powers of individual regulators; the composition and chairing of the Sub‑Committee; the role of the FSR as the Council’s primary public communication tool; and the sequence of policy actions during the 2020 crisis — moratorium, LCR easing, SLF‑MF, and subsequent normalization.

SEBI's Regulatory Framework for Proxy Advisors: Registration, Code of Conduct and Voting Recommendation Standards

Proxy advisors — firms that provide voting recommendations to institutional investors — were brought under a formal regulatory regime by SEBI through the SEBI (Proxy Advisors) Regulations, 2014, notified on 28 August 2014. The regulations define a proxy advisor as any person engaged in the business of providing voting recommendations to institutional investors or shareholders of listed companies. Registration with SEBI is mandatory; the eligibility criteria require a net worth of at least ₹1 crore, a minimum of two qualified professionals with experience in corporate governance or securities law, and robust IT infrastructure for data security.

The Code of Conduct prescribed under Regulation 9 mandates that proxy advisors disclose their voting recommendation policies, methodologies, and any conflicts of interest. They must maintain a Chinese wall between research and commercial operations, and cannot accept compensation from the subject company for preparing recommendations. The 2020 amendment introduced a requirement for proxy advisors to publish a “vote rationale” for each recommendation, detailing the key factors, data sources, and any engagement with the company’s management. Additionally, proxy advisors must provide their reports to the subject company at least 48 hours before the shareholder meeting, allowing the company to issue a response.

SEBI’s 2022 circular on “Enhanced Disclosures by Proxy Advisors” further tightened norms: (i) proxy advisors must disclose the percentage of recommendations that align with management proposals versus those that oppose; (ii) they must maintain a searchable archive of all recommendations for a minimum of five years; (iii) any material change in methodology must be communicated to SEBI and clients at least 30 days before implementation. The circular also introduced a “comply‑or‑explain” framework for institutional investors — they must disclose whether they followed proxy advice and, if not, the reasons for deviation.

Enforcement actions have been limited but instructive. In 2021 SEBI issued a show‑cause notice to a proxy advisor for failing to disclose a conflict of interest arising from a consulting engagement with a promoter group. The matter was settled through a consent order with a monetary penalty of ₹25 lakh and an undertaking to strengthen internal controls. The Securities Appellate Tribunal (SAT) upheld SEBI’s jurisdiction over proxy advisors in Institutional Investor Advisory Services India Ltd. v. SEBI (2020), confirming that the regulatory framework does not violate the freedom of profession under Article 19(1)(g) because it serves investor protection and market integrity.

Examiner traps: confusing the net‑worth requirement for proxy advisors (₹1 crore) with that for investment advisers (₹50 lakh); assuming proxy advisors can charge the subject company for recommendations (prohibited); overlooking the 48‑hour pre‑meeting disclosure rule; treating the “comply‑or‑explain” requirement as applicable to proxy advisors rather than institutional investors.

Worked Example: Proxy Advisor Disclosure Timeline
A proxy advisor issues a voting recommendation for the AGM of XYZ Ltd scheduled on 15 June. The recommendation is finalised on 10 June. Under SEBI regulations, by what date must the proxy advisor provide the report to XYZ Ltd, and what additional disclosure must accompany it?
The proxy advisor must deliver the report to XYZ Ltd at least 48 hours before the AGM, i.e., by 13 June 17:00 hours (assuming business hours). The report must include a vote rationale detailing key factors, data sources, and any engagement with management. If the recommendation is finalised on 10 June, the advisor has three business days to comply; failure to meet the 48‑hour deadline constitutes a violation of Regulation 9(4) of the Proxy Advisors Regulations, 2014.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a question asks about the regulatory perimeter of proxy advisors, first identify the governing regulation (SEBI (Proxy Advisors) Regulations, 2014) and the key obligations: registration, net worth, code of conduct, 48‑hour pre‑meeting disclosure, vote rationale, and the 2022 enhanced disclosure circular. Remember that the “comply‑or‑explain” duty falls on institutional investors, not on proxy advisors. A quick mental checklist — registration, net worth, Chinese wall, conflict‑of‑interest disclosure, vote rationale, 48‑hour rule — covers 90 % of the marks.

RBI's Co‑Lending Model for Banks and NBFCs: Risk Sharing, Capital Treatment, Reporting and Supervisory Expectations

The Reserve Bank of India introduced the Co‑Lending Model (CLM) through a circular dated 24 September 2020, revised on 15 November 2021, to facilitate joint lending by banks and Non‑Banking Financial Companies (NBFCs) to priority sector borrowers. The model permits a bank and an NBFC to co‑originate loans in a predetermined ratio — typically 80:20 (bank:NBFC) for priority sector loans and 50:50 for non‑priority sector loans — with the bank taking the lead role in credit appraisal, documentation, and monitoring. The NBFC contributes the balance and shares the credit risk proportionately.

Risk‑sharing mechanics are explicit: the bank and NBFC must enter into a legally binding co‑lending agreement that specifies the sharing of principal, interest, and recoveries. The agreement must provide for joint decision‑making on restructuring, recovery, and write‑off. The bank’s exposure is treated as a direct loan for capital adequacy purposes, while the NBFC’s portion attracts the standard risk weights applicable to its asset class. Crucially, the RBI clarified that the co‑lending arrangement does not constitute a securitisation transaction; therefore, the true‑sale test and the associated capital relief for securitisation do not apply.

Capital treatment: for the bank, the entire loan amount (including the NBFC’s share) is risk‑weighted as per the borrower’s rating and collateral. The NBFC must hold capital against its share as per the NBFC‑Scale Based Regulation (SBR) framework. The 2021 revision allowed banks to assign a lower risk weight (20 %) to the portion of the loan guaranteed by the NBFC if the NBFC meets the “well‑capitalised” criterion (CRAR ≥ 15 % and net NPA ≤ 3 %). This incentivises partnership with stronger NBFCs.

Reporting and supervisory expectations: both parties must report co‑lending exposures separately in their respective regulatory returns — banks in the Quarterly Returns on Co‑Lending (Form CL‑1) and NBFCs in the Monthly Returns on Co‑Lending (Form CL‑2). The RBI conducts thematic inspections focusing on (i) adherence to the agreed sharing ratio, (ii) independence of credit appraisal (the bank must not outsource appraisal to the NBFC), (iii) fairness in pricing — the blended interest rate must not exceed the higher of the two partners’ standalone rates, and (iv) grievance redressal for borrowers, which must be accessible through both entities’ channels.

Recent data: as of March 2023, over 1.2 lakh co‑lending agreements were active, covering ₹2.3 trillion in outstanding loans, predominantly in the micro‑enterprise and agriculture segments. The RBI’s 2023 Financial Stability Report highlighted that co‑lending has improved credit flow to underserved segments but flagged concentration risk where a few large NBFCs dominate partnerships with multiple banks.

Examiner focus: the distinction between co‑lending and securitisation; the 80:20 and 50:50 sharing ratios for priority and non‑priority sectors; capital treatment for both bank and NBFC; the requirement for independent credit appraisal by the bank; and the reporting forms CL‑1 and CL‑2.

National Financial Reporting Authority: Audit Oversight Powers, Inspection Framework and Enforcement Outcomes

The National Financial Reporting Authority (NFRA) was constituted on 1 October 2018 under Section 132 of the Companies Act, 2013, as an independent audit regulator for listed companies and certain unlisted public companies. Its mandate covers the oversight of auditors, audit firms, and the quality of financial reporting. NFRA’s jurisdiction extends to all companies whose securities are listed on any stock exchange in India, as well as unlisted public companies with a paid‑up capital of ₹500 crore or more, or an annual turnover of ₹1,000 crore or more.

NFRA’s powers include: (i) registration and de‑registration of auditors and audit firms; (ii) prescribing auditing standards (in coordination with the Institute of Chartered Accountants of India); (iii) conducting inspections of audit firms — both routine (risk‑based) and special (triggered by complaints or market intelligence); (iv) imposing monetary penalties up to ₹1 crore for firms and ₹25 lakh for individual auditors; (v) debarring auditors from practice for up to ten years; and (vi) referring cases to the Central Government for prosecution under the Companies Act.

The inspection framework follows a three‑year cycle for large audit firms (those auditing more than 20 listed entities) and a five‑year cycle for others. Inspections assess compliance with Standards on Auditing (SAs), independence requirements, quality control systems (ISQC 1), and the adequacy of audit documentation. NFRA publishes an annual Inspection Report summarising findings, common deficiencies, and remedial actions. In its 2022‑23 report, NFRA identified “insufficient audit evidence for revenue recognition”, “inadequate testing of journal entries”, and “failure to evaluate going‑concern assumptions” as the top three deficiencies across inspected firms.

Enforcement outcomes have been significant. In 2021 NFRA imposed a penalty of ₹50 lakh on a major audit firm for lapses in the audit of a listed infrastructure company, including failure to detect related‑party transactions. In 2022, NFRA debarred a partner of a mid‑size firm for five years for signing audit reports without adequate review. The Authority also issued a directive in 2023 mandating audit firms to rotate the engagement partner every five years for listed entities, aligning with the Companies (Audit and Auditors) Rules, 2014.

Coordination with other regulators: NFRA has a Memorandum of Understanding with SEBI for sharing information on audit quality of listed companies, and with the RBI for banks and NBFCs. The 2022 amendment to the Companies Act empowered NFRA to issue “audit quality alerts” to the market, a tool used first in August 2023 to flag risks in the audit of companies with significant exposure to the real estate sector.

Examiner traps: confusing NFRA’s jurisdiction (listed + large unlisted) with that of the ICAI (which regulates all Chartered Accountants); assuming NFRA can set accounting standards (it prescribes auditing standards, not Ind‑AS); overlooking the monetary penalty caps (₹1 crore for firms, ₹25 lakh for individuals); and missing the five‑year partner rotation rule introduced in 2023.

Evolution of the Securities Contracts (Regulation) Act, 1956: Demutualisation, Exchange Governance and Impact on Market Integrity

The Securities Contracts (Regulation) Act, 1956 (SCRA) provides the statutory foundation for the regulation of securities markets in India. The original Act envisaged stock exchanges as mutualised associations of brokers, with ownership and trading rights vested in members. The 1999 amendment (effective 2000) introduced the concept of demutualisation — separating ownership, management, and trading rights — and mandated that every recognised stock exchange convert into a corporate entity with a minimum public shareholding of 51 % within a prescribed timeline. The Bombay Stock Exchange (BSE) completed demutualisation in 2005, followed by the National Stock Exchange (NSE) in 2003 (though NSE was incorporated as a demutualised entity from inception).

The 2004 amendment strengthened SEBI’s oversight powers: it empowered SEBI to supersede the governing board of a recognised exchange, to approve the appointment of the Managing Director, and to prescribe net‑worth and infrastructure standards. The 2013 amendment, notified after the NSE co‑location scandal, introduced stricter governance norms — mandatory independent directors constituting at least 50 % of the board, a separate risk management committee, and a requirement for the exchange to disclose its technology architecture and co‑location policies to SEBI annually.

Key governance provisions under the current SCRA framework: (i) recognised exchanges must have a minimum net worth of ₹100 crore; (ii) at least 25 % of the paid‑up capital must be held by public shareholders (other than trading members); (iii) the Managing Director and the Compliance Officer must be appointed with SEBI’s prior approval; (iv) exchanges must maintain a Settlement Guarantee Fund (SGF) with a minimum corpus of ₹500 crore, contributed by the exchange and its clearing corporation; (v) the exchange’s bylaws, rules, and regulations require SEBI’s approval before implementation.

Impact on market integrity: demutualisation aligned the incentives of exchange owners with those of investors, reducing conflicts of interest inherent in the mutualised model. The introduction of independent directors and SEBI‑approved leadership improved accountability. The SGF and the mandatory T+1 settlement cycle (implemented in January 2023) have enhanced settlement certainty. However, the concentration of trading volume on a single exchange (NSE) has raised concerns about systemic risk and the adequacy of competition policy — a subject of ongoing review by the Competition Commission of India.

Examiner focus: the sequence of amendments (1999 demutualisation, 2004 SEBI powers, 2013 governance tightening); the distinction between mutualised and demutualised structures; the net‑worth and public shareholding thresholds; the role of the Settlement Guarantee Fund; and the link between the 2013 amendment and the NSE co‑location case.

  • Assuming the FSDC has statutory enforcement powers — it is an advisory council; only its constituent regulators hold statutory authority.
  • Confusing the net‑worth requirement for proxy advisors (₹1 crore) with that for investment advisers (₹50 lakh) or stock brokers (₹2 crore).
  • Treating the co‑lending model as a securitisation transaction — the RBI explicitly clarified it is not a true‑sale arrangement.
  • Believing NFRA can prescribe accounting standards (Ind‑AS) — its mandate is limited to auditing standards and audit quality oversight.
  • Overlooking the 48‑hour pre‑meeting disclosure rule for proxy advisors and the separate “comply‑or‑explain” duty on institutional investors.
  • Mixing the demutualisation timeline: BSE demutualised in 2005, NSE was incorporated as a demutualised entity in 1992 and recognised in 1993.
  • Assuming the Settlement Guarantee Fund corpus is funded solely by the exchange — contributions are shared between the exchange and its clearing corporation.

Commodity Derivatives Market: SEBI's Regulatory Framework, Warehouse Receipts, and Spot‑Exchange Integration

The Finance Act, 2015 transferred the regulation of commodity derivatives from the Forward Markets Commission (FMC) to SEBI, effective 28 September 2015. SEBI now administers the Securities Contracts (Regulation) Act, 1956 (SCRA) for commodity derivatives and has notified the SEBI (Commodity Derivatives) Regulations, 2018, which prescribe eligibility criteria for exchanges, product design norms, position limits, and a unified margin framework. Three recognised commodity exchanges operate under this regime: Multi Commodity Exchange of India Ltd (MCX), National Commodity & Derivatives Exchange Ltd (NCDEX), and Indian Commodity Exchange Ltd (ICEX).

Warehouse receipts form the physical‑delivery backbone. The Warehousing (Development and Regulation) Act, 2007 created the Warehousing Development and Regulatory Authority (WDRA) which registers warehouses and issues Negotiable Warehouse Receipts (NWRs). In 2017 WDRA enabled Electronic Negotiable Warehouse Receipts (e‑NWRs) on a blockchain‑based platform, allowing seamless transfer and pledge. SEBI’s 2020 circular (SEBI/HO/CDMRD/DCR/P/2020/71) mandated that commodity derivatives contracts settled by physical delivery must be backed by e‑NWRs, thereby reducing settlement risk and enabling real‑time title transfer.

Spot‑exchange integration is driven by the electronic National Agriculture Market (e‑NAM), launched in April 2016. e‑NAM connects over 1,000 wholesale mandis across states, providing a unified trading platform for agricultural produce. SEBI’s 2021 circular (SEBI/HO/CDMRD/DCR/P/2021/115) directed commodity exchanges to develop “basis‑risk” contracts that reference e‑NAM spot prices, improving price discovery and hedging effectiveness for farmers and processors. The circular also introduced a “commodity index” framework, permitting exchanges to launch sectoral indices (e.g., MCX BULLDEX, NCDEX AGRIDEX) that are used as underlying for futures and options.

Margin and surveillance enhancements include the 2021 Unified Margin Framework (SEBI/HO/CDMRD/DCR/P/2021/115) which aligns initial margin, extreme loss margin, and calendar spread margin across all commodity segments. Position limits are set at 5 % of open interest for individual clients and 10 % for a group of related clients, with additional concentration limits for sensitive commodities such as crude oil and gold. SEBI’s Market Surveillance System (MSS) employs algorithmic trade reconstruction and real‑time alert generation; the 2022 amendment (SEBI/HO/CDMRD/DCR/P/2022/45) extended the surveillance window to 30 days and mandated exchanges to report suspicious patterns to SEBI within 24 hours.

Key examiner focus areas: the 2015 transfer of powers, the distinction between WDRA (warehouse regulation) and SEBI (derivatives regulation), the role of e‑NWRs in physical settlement, the linkage between e‑NAM spot prices and derivative contract design, and the unified margin framework’s impact on capital efficiency for market participants.

External Commercial Borrowings: Track Classification, End‑Use Restrictions, and Reporting Obligations

External Commercial Borrowings (ECBs) are governed by the Foreign Exchange Management Act, 1999 (FEMA) and the RBI Master Direction – External Commercial Borrowings, Trade Credit, Borrowing and Lending in Foreign Currency (updated 2022). The framework classifies ECBs into three tracks based on minimum average maturity and end‑use flexibility. Track I permits borrowings up to USD 750 million (or equivalent) per financial year with a minimum average maturity of three years; eligible end‑uses include capital expenditure, working capital for manufacturing and infrastructure, and repayment of rupee‑denominated term loans. Track II allows the same ceiling with a five‑year minimum maturity and adds end‑uses such as overseas acquisitions and refinancing of existing ECBs. Track III, also capped at USD 750 million, requires a ten‑year minimum maturity and is restricted to long‑term infrastructure and green‑field projects.

End‑use prohibitions are explicit: ECB proceeds cannot be deployed in real‑estate activities (except affordable housing under Track I as per the 2023 amendment), capital market speculation, working capital for non‑manufacturing sectors, or repayment of short‑term rupee loans. The 2022 master direction introduced a “green ECB” sub‑category under Track I, allowing proceeds to finance renewable energy, energy efficiency, and climate‑adaptation projects, provided the borrower obtains an external review aligned with the ICMA Green Bond Principles.

Reporting is conducted through Authorised Dealer Category‑I banks using Form ECB‑2 (for drawdown and repayment) and Form ECB‑3 (for end‑use certification). The borrower must submit a quarterly return within 15 days of quarter‑end, and an annual certificate from a Chartered Accountant confirming compliance with end‑use conditions. Non‑compliance attracts penal interest at 2 % per annum above the contracted rate and may lead to debarment from future ECB access. The 2023 amendment also mandated that all ECB agreements include a clause for mandatory reporting of any change in beneficial ownership within 30 days.

Recent liberalisation measures include the 2022 increase of the all‑in‑cost ceiling for Track I from 450 bps to 500 bps over the 6‑month LIBOR/SOFR benchmark, and the 2023 permission for NBFCs classified as “Upper Layer” under the Scale‑Based Regulation to access Track I for on‑lending to MSMEs, subject to a 20 % sub‑limit of their net owned funds. These changes aim to diversify funding sources for Indian corporates while preserving macro‑prudential safeguards.

Examiner traps: confusing the three tracks’ maturity requirements; assuming ECBs can freely fund working capital for any sector; overlooking the mandatory CA certificate and quarterly Form ECB‑2 filing; missing the 2023 affordable‑housing carve‑out under Track I; and treating the all‑in‑cost ceiling as a hard cap on interest rate rather than a spread over the benchmark.

Worked Example: ECB Track Selection
A manufacturing company plans to raise USD 500 million for a new plant with a projected cash‑flow horizon of four years. It also intends to allocate USD 100 million of the proceeds to repay an existing rupee term loan. Which ECB track(s) can the company use, and what are the key compliance conditions?
The plant financing qualifies as capital expenditure, an eligible end‑use under Track I (minimum 3‑year maturity) and Track II (minimum 5‑year maturity). Since the cash‑flow horizon is four years, Track I satisfies the maturity requirement, while Track II would impose a longer maturity than needed. Repayment of a rupee term loan is permitted under Track I only if the loan being repaid was originally taken for capital expenditure; otherwise it is prohibited. The company must ensure the all‑in‑cost does not exceed 500 bps over the 6‑month SOFR benchmark, file Form ECB‑2 quarterly through its AD‑I bank, and obtain an annual CA certificate confirming end‑use compliance. If the rupee loan was for working capital, the company must either restrict the ECB to the plant portion (USD 400 million) or seek a Track II structure with a five‑year maturity and a separate refinancing approval.
Exam‑Day Strategy
Memorise the three‑track matrix (ceiling, maturity, end‑use) as a 3 × 3 table. For any scenario, first match the required maturity to the track, then verify end‑use eligibility, and finally check reporting forms (ECB‑2/ECB‑3) and the all‑in‑cost ceiling. A quick mental table eliminates 80 % of ECB‑related MCQs.

Trade Receivables Discounting System (TReDS): Registration, Participant Roles, and Settlement Mechanics

The Trade Receivables Discounting System (TReDS) was launched by the Reserve Bank of India in 2017 under the Payment and Settlement Systems Act, 2007 to address the working‑capital gap of Micro, Small and Medium Enterprises (MSMEs). Three entities have been authorised to operate TReDS platforms: Receivables Exchange of India Ltd (RXIL), M1xchange (a joint venture of SIDBI and NSE), and Invoicemart (promoted by Axis Bank and others). Each platform must obtain a Certificate of Authorisation from RBI and comply with the Master Direction – Trade Receivables Discounting System (2020), which prescribes governance, technology, and risk‑management standards.

Participants fall into three categories: (i) MSME sellers who upload invoices against confirmed purchase orders; (ii) corporate buyers (large enterprises, government departments, or public sector undertakings) who accept the invoices on the platform, thereby creating a legally enforceable payment obligation; and (iii) financiers — scheduled commercial banks, NBFCs, and factoring companies — who bid to discount the accepted invoices. The 2022 amendment (RBI/2022‑23/112) permits multiple financiers to participate in a single invoice through a “syndicated discounting” mechanism, enhancing price discovery for the seller.

The transaction flow is fully electronic: the seller creates an invoice on the platform, the buyer digitally accepts it (generating a “trade receivable” under the Factoring Regulation Act, 2011), financiers submit discount rates, and the seller selects the best offer. Funds are transferred to the seller’s bank account on a T+1 basis (settlement day plus one working day) via the platform’s settlement account maintained with a settlement bank. The buyer repays the financier on the invoice due date, which can be up to 90 days from acceptance. The platform charges a transaction fee (typically 0.05 %–0.10 % of invoice value) shared between seller and financier as per the platform’s fee schedule.

Risk management features include: (a) mandatory KYC and credit‑score integration for all participants; (b) a “buyer‑credit‑limit” framework where the platform sets exposure caps for each buyer based on their financial health; (c) real‑time monitoring of concentration risk — no single buyer may account for more than 20 % of a financier’s outstanding discounted receivables; and (d) a dispute‑resolution module that escalates unpaid invoices to the platform’s grievance redressal committee within 7 days, with an option to approach the MSME Samadhaan portal for statutory recovery.

As of March 2024, TReDS platforms have collectively discounted invoices worth over ₹2.3 trillion, with RXIL accounting for roughly 55 % of volumes. The RBI’s 2023 circular (RBI/2023‑24/45) mandated that all platforms integrate with the Account Aggregator framework to fetch GST‑verified invoice data, reducing fraud and improving data integrity. Examiners often test the distinction between TReDS (an electronic discounting platform) and traditional factoring (a bilateral assignment of receivables), the T+1 settlement timeline, and the role of the Factoring Regulation Act in providing legal enforceability to the accepted invoice.

Cyber Resilience Framework for Market Infrastructure Institutions: SEBI‑RBI Joint Guidelines, Incident Reporting, and Testing Standards

Market Infrastructure Institutions (MIIs) — stock exchanges, clearing corporations, and depositories — are subject to a dedicated cyber resilience regime issued jointly by SEBI and RBI. SEBI’s circular SEBI/HO/MIRSD/MIRSD_CRADT/P/2021/686 (dated 7 June 2021) titled “Cyber Security and Cyber Resilience Framework for Market Infrastructure Institutions” establishes a board‑level Cyber Risk Committee, mandatory adoption of ISO/IEC 27001, and a minimum set of technical controls including network segmentation, multi‑factor authentication for privileged access, and encryption of data at rest and in transit.

The framework prescribes a three‑tier incident‑reporting timeline: (i) critical incidents (e.g., ransomware, data exfiltration, trading‑system outage) must be reported to SEBI and the Indian Computer Emergency Response Team (CERT‑In) within six hours of detection; (ii) high‑severity incidents within 24 hours; and (iii) medium/low incidents within 72 hours. A root‑cause analysis and remediation plan must be submitted within 15 days. The 2022 amendment (SEBI/HO/MIRSD/MIRSD_CRADT/P/2022/112) added a requirement for MIIs to conduct quarterly “red‑team” exercises and annual “purple‑team” assessments, with results shared with the regulator.

RBI’s parallel circular RBI/2021‑22/115 (dated 12 August 2021) “Cyber Security Framework for Payment System Operators” extends similar obligations to payment system operators (including UPI, Bharat Bill Payment System, and card networks). The joint SEBI‑RBI “Cyber Security Framework for Financial Market Infrastructures” (2023) harmonises definitions, introduces a common “Cyber Resilience Index” (CRI) that MIIs must compute quarterly, and mandates participation in the Financial Sector Computer Security Incident Response Team (FS‑CSIRT) for threat‑intelligence sharing.

Testing standards are explicit: (a) annual penetration testing by a CERT‑In empanelled auditor; (b) quarterly vulnerability scanning of all internet‑facing assets; (c) semi‑annual business continuity and disaster recovery (BC/DR) drills with a recovery‑time objective (RTO) of four hours for critical trading systems; and (d) annual tabletop exercises simulating a coordinated cyber‑attack across multiple MIIs. Non‑compliance attracts monetary penalties under Section 15HB of the SEBI Act (up to ₹25 crore or three times the profit made) and under Section 58 of the Payment and Settlement Systems Act (up to ₹1 crore).

Key focus areas for examination: the six‑hour critical‑incident reporting window, the distinction between SEBI’s MII‑specific circular and RBI’s payment‑operator circular, the role of the Cyber Resilience Index in supervisory assessment, and the integration of the Account Aggregator consent layer into MII data‑sharing policies as per the 2023 joint framework.

Sustainable Finance Taxonomy Alignment: RBI Green Taxonomy, SEBI Green Bond Guidelines, and International Standards

India’s sustainable finance architecture currently operates with three parallel taxonomies. The Reserve Bank of India released its “Green Taxonomy for Scheduled Commercial Banks” in February 2023, defining 12 eligible green activity categories (renewable energy, energy efficiency, pollution prevention, sustainable agriculture, green transport, etc.) and prescribing a “do‑no‑significant‑harm” (DNSH) test aligned with the EU Taxonomy’s technical screening criteria. SEBI’s Green Bond Guidelines (first issued 2017, revised 2021) list 10 project categories for green bond eligibility and require an external review by a SEBI‑registered Credit Rating Agency or an approved verifier, referencing the International Capital Market Association (ICMA) Green Bond Principles (GBP) 2021.

The International Financial Reporting Standards (IFRS) Foundation’s International Sustainability Standards Board (ISSB) released IFRS S2 Climate‑related Disclosures in June 2023, which India has signaled intent to adopt for listed entities. Meanwhile, the Climate Bonds Initiative (CBI) taxonomy, used globally for certification of climate‑aligned bonds, includes sector‑specific criteria (e.g., solar PV, low‑carbon buildings) that are more granular than both RBI and SEBI lists.

To reduce fragmentation, SEBI issued a consultation paper in December 2022 (“Towards a Unified Sustainable Finance Taxonomy for India”) proposing a single national taxonomy that maps RBI’s 12 categories, SEBI’s 10 categories, and the EU Taxonomy’s 70+ activities into a harmonised framework with three tiers: (i) “Core Green” activities meeting all three taxonomies; (ii) “Transition” activities compliant with at least two; and (iii) “Enabling” activities that facilitate green outcomes but are not green per se. The paper also recommends a mandatory “Taxonomy Alignment Disclosure” in the Business Responsibility and Sustainability Report (BRSR) for the top 1,000 listed companies from FY 2024‑25.

Practical implications: a corporate issuing a green bond under SEBI guidelines can simultaneously claim RBI green‑loan classification if the proceeds are deployed in a “Core Green” activity, thereby accessing lower risk‑weights under RBI’s climate‑risk capital framework. Conversely, a bank extending a green loan under RBI taxonomy must ensure the borrower’s project meets the DNSH test, which may require additional environmental impact assessments beyond SEBI’s external review. The 2023 “India Climate Finance Leadership Initiative” (ICFLI) convened by the Ministry of Finance aims to finalise the unified taxonomy by end‑2024 and to establish a national taxonomy registry for cross‑border recognition.

Examiner emphasis: the distinction between RBI’s bank‑centric taxonomy (capital‑treatment focus) and SEBI’s market‑centric guidelines (disclosure and investor protection), the role of external review versus DNSH testing, the mapping exercise in SEBI’s consultation paper, and the timeline for mandatory BRSR taxonomy alignment disclosure.

  • Confusing the RBI Green Taxonomy (bank capital treatment) with SEBI Green Bond Guidelines (market disclosure) — they serve different regulatory objectives.
  • Assuming all ECB tracks allow working‑capital financing; only Track I permits it for manufacturing and infrastructure, and even then with strict end‑use certification.
  • Overlooking the six‑hour critical‑incident reporting mandate for MIIs under the SEBI‑RBI joint cyber framework; many candidates cite 24 hours.
  • Treating TReDS as a factoring arrangement without recognising the statutory enforceability conferred by the Factoring Regulation Act, 2011 on the accepted invoice.
  • Missing the 2023 affordable‑housing carve‑out under ECB Track I and the requirement for an external review aligned with ICMA Green Bond Principles for green ECBs.
  • Believing the unified margin framework for commodity derivatives applies only to agricultural commodities; it covers all segments including metals and energy.
  • Neglecting the mandatory quarterly Cyber Resilience Index computation and its use in supervisory scoring of MIIs.
